Probabilistic Inference and Learning with Stein’s Method
Qiang Liu, Lester Mackey, Chris Oates

Abstract
This monograph provides a rigorous overview of theoretical and methodological aspects of probabilistic inference and learning with Stein’s method. Recipes are provided for constructing Stein discrepancies from Stein operators and Stein sets, and properties of these discrepancies such as computability, separation, convergence detection, and convergence control are discussed. Further, the connection between Stein operators and Stein variational gradient descent is set out in detail. The main definitions and results are precisely stated, and references to all proofs are provided.



Chapter 1
Introduction

For over 50 years, Stein’s method has provided a powerful
theoretical
tool in probability theory, enabling explicit upper bounds on the convergence of random variables to their limiting distributions
(
Stein 1972
)
.
Several excellent references summarize this line of research and we refer the interested reader to
Barbour and Chen 2005
;
Chen et al. 2010a
;
Ross 2011
;
Ley et al. 2017
.
Our focus is rather on Stein’s method as a
methodological
tool that gives rise to new and powerful algorithms for probabilistic inference and learning.
Our exposition is motivated by the introduction of computable Stein discrepancies in
Gorham and Mackey 2015
, but the applications we explore have roots dating back to the early work of
Stein 1986
;
Stein et al. 2004
.
A recent high-level survey of these developments can be found in
Anastasiou et al. 2023
, but otherwise the main results are distributed across the literature.
The present text therefore aims to provide a singular reference for rigorous definitions and results relevant to probabilistic inference and learning with Stein’s method.

1
Stein’s Method in a Nutshell

Suppose
P
is a probability distribution of interest supported on an appropriate space
\mathcal{X}
; we are especially interested in settings where most expectations under
P
are not easily computed.
Stein’s method can be thought of as a recipe to measure how well integration under
P
is approximated by integration under a given surrogate distribution
Q
.
For example,
P
may represent the intractable posterior distribution arising in a Bayesian analysis, while
Q
may represent a
sample approximation

Q=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
with discrete points
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}
.
To measure how well
Q
approximates
P
, one would ideally compute an
integral probability metric

(
Müller 1997
)
that measures the maximum discrepancy

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{Q}[f(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(X)]\right|=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})-\int f(x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)\right|

between sample and target expectations over a class of test functions
\mathcal{F}
.
However, most standard integral probability metrics (including the Wasserstein metrics, Dudley metric, and maximum mean discrepancies described in
Section

9
) require explicit integration under
P
, rendering them incomputable for our target distributions of interest.
To address this challenge, we will adapt an ingenious idea that Charles M. Stein (1920-2016) introduced to quantify approximation error in the central limit theorem.
It is important to note that our presentation is adapted for
methodological
applications of Stein’s method, and the aspects that we emphasize are rather different from the presentations found in the aforementioned references on
theoretical
applications of Stein’s method.

At the heart of
Stein’s method
are a
Stein set

\mathcal{G}
and a
Stein operator

\mathcal{T}_{P}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, which together generate mean-zero expectations under
P
:

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)=0\quad\text{for all}\quad g\in\mathcal{G}.

(1)

Here
\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
denotes the set of functions that are integrable with respect to
P
, to ensure the integral in (
1
) is well-defined.
For instance, if
P
is the standard normal distribution in dimension
d=1
, and
\mathcal{G}
is the set of all monomials

g(x)=x^{m}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
then

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)\triangleq g^{\prime}(x)-xg(x)

(2)

defines a valid Stein operator. Indeed we can explicitly compute the expectation

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)

\displaystyle=\int mx^{m-1}-x^{m+1}\;\mathrm{d}P(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&m\;\text{is even}\\
m(m-2)!!-m!!&m\;\text{is odd}\end{array}\right\}=0

where
n!!
denotes the double factorial, that is, the product of all numbers from 1 to
n
that have the same parity as
n
.

Given any valid combination of Stein set
\mathcal{G}
and Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
, one can construct a
Stein discrepancy

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})\triangleq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in\mathcal{G}\end{subarray}}\left|\int(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)\;\mathrm{d}Q(x)\right|

(3)

to measure the extent to which
Q
differs from
P
.
The topology induced by a Stein discrepancy depends critically on the choice of
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{T}_{P}
, and an important challenge is to understand how to construct Stein discrepancies (
3
) that are practically useful.
To be slightly more precise, one can ask whether
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{T}_{P}
can be chosen so that some (or all) of the following desiderata hold:

•

Separation
:
\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})=0
if and only if
Q
and
P
are equal (i.e., the Stein discrepancy is a valid statistical divergence).

•

Convergence Detection
:
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})\rightarrow 0
whenever
Q_{n}
converge to
P
, in a sense to be specified.

•

Convergence Control
:
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})\nrightarrow 0
whenever
Q_{n}
fails to converge to
P
, in a sense to be specified.

•

Computability
: For any distribution
Q_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
with finite support set
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}
, the Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})
can be explicitly computed.

Several positive and negative results concerning the properties of Stein discrepancies have appeared over the last decade, but they are somewhat scattered in the probability, statistics, and machine learning literature.
One aim of the present manuscript is to gather together rigorous definitions and results in a singular reference text, with references to proofs of all results provided.

2
Illustrating Stein’s Method as a Methodological Tool

As a first illustration of the methodological potential of Stein’s method, consider the standard setting of Bayesian posterior inference on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}

(
Gelman et al. 2014
)
.
Given a prior density
\pi
and a likelihood
\mathcal{L}
, the posterior distribution
P
has a density
p
that can be characterized up to a normalizing constant via Bayes’ theorem:

\displaystyle p(x)\propto\pi(x)\mathcal{L}(x).

(4)

Unfortunately, in most cases, the normalizing constant, also called the
marginal likelihood
,

\displaystyle\int\pi(x)\mathcal{L}(x)\;\mathrm{d}x

(5)

is unavailable in closed form, and exact integration under
p
is intractable for most functions of interest. The same form of intractability naturally arises in the settings of maximum likelihood estimation
(
Geyer 1991
)
, post-selection inference
(
Tian et al. 2016
)
, and probabilistic inference more generally
(
Neal 1993
)
.
To approximate
P
and its expectations, a plethora of practical options are now available including Markov chain Monte Carlo
(
Brooks et al. 2011
)
, approximate Markov chain Monte Carlo
(
Welling and Teh 2011
, see, e.g.,)
, variational inference
(
Blei et al. 2017
)
, and quadrature. However, until recently, practitioners lacked tools suitable for measuring and comparing the quality of such diverse and potentially inconsistent sample approximations.

To fill this gap,
Gorham and Mackey 2015
introduced the notion of a Stein discrepancy and demonstrated its promise as a practical sample quality measure.
They began by deriving a multivariate and
P
-targeted generalization of Stein’s original operator for the standard normal
Eq.

2
:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)\triangleq(\nabla\cdot g)(x)+g(x)\cdot(\nabla\log p)(x).

(6)

This
Langevin
Stein operator generates mean-zero functions under
P
under mild conditions (see
Chapter

3
) and
can be computed even when the normalizing constant (
5
) is unknown.
Indeed, the operator
Eq.

6
depends on
P
only via the

Stein score
,

\displaystyle s_{p}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq(\nabla\log p)(x)=(\nabla\log\pi)(x)+(\nabla\log\mathcal{L})(x),

which is a function only of the prior and likelihood derivatives.
Remarkably, when coupled with a suitable Stein set
\mathcal{G}
of vector fields
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the resulting Stein discrepancy simultaneously enjoys all of the aforementioned properties of separation, convergence detection, convergence control, and computability (see
Chapter

4
).

Stein discrepancies are now widely used to measure sample quality, but the methodological implications of Stein’s method are broader, giving rise to new effective solutions for tasks such as gradient estimation, training generative models, goodness-of-fit testing, bias correction, sample quality improvement, and sampling; some of the diverse problems in probabilistic inference and learning that are now tackled using Stein’s method are outlined in
Chapter

6
.

3
Outline of the Monograph

This monograph aims for a systematic and rigorous presentation of probabilistic inference and learning using Stein’s method.
Throughout we will assume the reader has taken undergraduate-level courses in real analysis, but we will not assume familiarity with probability, measure theory, or functional analysis; the necessary concepts will introduced in
Chapter

2
.
An advanced reader may prefer to initially skip
Chapter

2
and return to it only when required.
Stein operators are discussed in detail in
Chapter

3
and their associated Stein discrepancies are discussed in
Chapter

4
.
Recent developments in Stein dynamics are covered in
Chapter

5
.
Although it is not possible to cover
all
of the recent and emerging applications of Stein’s method for probabilistic inference and learning, we have selected some notable examples to present in
Chapter

6
.

Chapter 2
Background

This monograph assumes familiarity with real analysis but not with any probability, measure theory, or functional analysis; the necessary concepts will be introduced.
An experienced reader may wish to skip this Chapter and return to it only when required.

7
Probability and Measure Theory

Though familiarity with measure theory is not required to understand most of the results that we present in Chapters
3
,
4
, and
5
, we briefly introduce the core concepts so that our main definitions can be precisely stated.

7.1
Measures

Our starting point is a (non-empty) set
\Omega
.

Definition 2.1

(
\sigma
-algebra)
.

A collection
\mathcal{S}
of subsets of
\Omega
for which

1.

\Omega\in\mathcal{S}

2.

\Omega\setminus S\in\mathcal{S}
whenever
S\in\mathcal{S}

3.

S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup\dots\in\mathcal{S}
whenever
S_{1},S_{2},\dots\in\mathcal{S}

is called a
\sigma
-algebra
of
\Omega
.

The pair
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
is called a
measurable space
.
If the
\sigma
-algebra is clear from context, we may refer to the measurable space using just
\Omega
.

Definition 2.2

(Measure)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega
.
A map
\mu:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
is called a
measure
if

1.

\mu(\emptyset)=0

2.

\mu(S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup\dots)=\mu(S_{1})+\mu(S_{2})+\dots
whenever the sets
S_{1},S_{2},\dots
are pairwise disjoint.

The triple
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mu)
is called a
measure space
.
If the
\sigma
-algebra and measure are clear from context, we may refer to the measure space using just
\Omega
.
A set
S\in\mathcal{S}
is called a
null set
if
\mu(S)=0
.
A measure
\mu
is called a
probability measure
if, in addition,
\mu(\Omega)=1
, and we call
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mu)
a
probability space
.

Example 2.1

(Atomic measure)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
be a measurable space and fix
\omega\in\Omega
.
Then

\displaystyle\delta_{\omega}(S)\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\omega\in S\\
0&\omega\notin S\end{array}\right.

defines a probability measure on
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
called an
atomic measure
at
\omega\in\Omega
.

The
\sigma
-algebra determines which events
S\subset\Omega
we are allowed to measure.
For example, a probability measure
\mu
constructed with respect to the
trivial

\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{S}=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
contains only the vacuous information that
\mu(\emptyset)=0
and
\mu(\Omega)=1
.

Example 2.2

(Discrete distributions)
.

Let
\Omega
be a countable set, and let
\mathcal{S}
be the
\sigma
-algebra consisting of all subsets of
\Omega
.
Then a probability measure is uniquely determined by the values
\mu(\omega)
for each
\omega\in\Omega
, and we call
\omega\mapsto\mu(\omega)
the
probability mass function
.

If
\Omega
carries additional mathematical structure, this may entail a natural choice for a
\sigma
-algebra.
Here we will consider
\Omega
to be a topological space that is
Hausdorff
, which we recall means that any two distinct elements
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
can be separated by open sets
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\subset\Omega
with
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
,
\omega_{2}\in\Omega_{2}
and
\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}=\emptyset
.
Such a space
\Omega
is further said to be
locally compact
if, for each
\omega_{1}\in\Omega
, there exists a compact set
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega
with
\omega_{1}\in\Omega_{1}
.
It is straightforward to check that examples of locally compact Hausdorff spaces include
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
.

Example 2.3

(Borel measures)
.

Let
\Omega
be a locally compact Hausdorff space, and let
\mathcal{B}_{\Omega}
be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra that contains the open sets of
\Omega
, called the
Borel

\sigma
-algebra.
A measure
\mu:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow[0,1]
is called a
Borel
measure.

The Borel
\sigma
-algebra can be insufficient, since not every subset of a set of Borel measure 0 is a Borel measurable set.
In the context of
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Lebesgue measure
completes
the Borel measure and will form the basis of Lebesgue integration in
Section

7.2
.
For a box
B=(a_{1},b_{1})\times\dots\times(a_{d},b_{d})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\text{vol}(B)\triangleq(b_{1}-a_{1})\dots(b_{d}-a_{d})
denote the volume of the box.

Example 2.4

(Lebesgue measures)
.

For a subset
S\subset\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the
Lebesgue outer measure
is defined as

\lambda^{*}(S)\triangleq\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\text{vol}(B_{i}):(B_{i})\;\text{a sequence of boxes with}\;S\subset\cup_{i}B_{i}\right\}.

The elements of the
Lebesgue

\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}
are the sets
S
such that the Carathéodory criterion,
\lambda^{*}(A\cap S)+\lambda^{*}(A\cap S^{c})=\lambda^{*}(A)
for all
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, is satisfied.
For each
S\in\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}
, the
Lebesgue measure

\lambda(S)
is defined as
\lambda(S)=\lambda^{*}(S)
.

Definition 2.3

(Measurable function)
.

A function
f:\Omega_{1}\rightarrow\Omega_{2}
between measurable spaces
(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{S}_{1})
and
(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{S}_{2})
is said to be
measurable
if
f^{-1}(S)=\{\omega\in\Omega_{1}:f(\omega)\in S\}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
whenever
S\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
.

7.2
Lebesgue Spaces

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mu)
be a measure space.
A function
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow[0,\infty]
is called
simple
if there exist
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in[0,\infty]
,
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
, such that

\phi(\omega)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathrm{1}_{S_{i}}(\omega)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
, and we define an integral

\int\phi\;\mathrm{d}\mu\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mu(S_{i}).

Definition 2.4

(Lebesgue integral)
.

For a positive measurable function
f:\Omega\rightarrow[0,\infty]
, mapping from
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
to
(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}})
, the
Lebesgue integral
is defined as

\int f\;\mathrm{d}\mu\triangleq\sup\left\{\int\phi\;\mathrm{d}\mu:\phi\;\text{is a simple function and}\;\phi\leq f\right\},

while if
f
also takes negative values then we define its Lebesgue integral as

\int f\;\mathrm{d}\mu\triangleq\int f_{+}\;\mathrm{d}\mu-\int f_{-}\;\mathrm{d}\mu

where
f_{\pm}=f\cdot 1_{S_{\pm}}
and
S_{\pm}=f^{-1}([0,\pm\infty])
, whenever at least one of these integrals is finite.

The nomenclature of the Lebesgue integral derives from its original construction, involving Lebesgue measure
\lambda
from
Example

2.4
, but here we present it for general measures
\mu
.
In the particular case of Lebesgue measure
\lambda
on
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we follow standard convention and often write
\int f(x)\;\mathrm{d}x
as an alternative notation for
\int f\;\mathrm{d}\lambda
.
It will sometimes be convenient to emphasize the argument of integration, in which case we write
\int f(\omega)\;\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)
.
On the other hand, the shorthand

\displaystyle\mu(f)\triangleq\int f\;\mathrm{d}\mu

(7)

will be used extensively in
Chapter

3
.
It will also be convenient to extend the definition of the Lebesgue integral to functions
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which we achieve by applying the above construction to each coordinate function
f_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,d
.

For
\mu
and
\nu
probability measures on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
, we say that
\mu
is
absolutely continuous
with respect to
\nu
(written
\mu\ll\nu
) if
\mu(S)=0
for every set
S\in\mathcal{S}
for which
\nu(S)=0
.

Theorem 2.1

(Radon–Nikodym)
.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be probability measures on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
with
\mu\ll\nu
.
Then there exists a measurable function
p:\Omega\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
\mu(S)=\int p(\omega)1_{S}(\omega)\;\mathrm{d}\nu(\omega)
for any
S\in\mathcal{S}
.

The function
p
appearing in
Theorem

2.1
is called a
Radon–Nikodym derivative
of
\mu
with respect to
\nu
and is denoted
\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}\nu
.
In the particular case where
\nu
is the Lebesgue measure
\lambda
from
Example

2.4
, we recover the familiar concept of a probability density function:

Definition 2.5

(Continuous distributions)
.

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
\mu
be a probability measure such that
\mu(S)=\int p(\omega)1_{S}(\omega)\;\mathrm{d}\lambda(\omega)
for some measurable function
p:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure from
Example

2.4
.
Then
p=\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}\lambda
is known as a
probability density function
for
\mu
with respect to
\lambda
, or simply a
(Lebesgue) density
for
\mu
.

Let
1\leq s<\infty
.
Consider the real vector space
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)
of functions
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for which the Lebesgue integral

|f|_{\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)}\triangleq\left(\int|f|^{s}\;\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{1/s}<\infty

is well-defined.
The space
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)
is a seminormed vector space when equipped with
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)}
but not a normed space because
|f|_{\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)}=0
only implies that
f
is non-zero on a
\mu
-null set and not that
f=0
on
\Omega
.
(An elementary discussion of norms on spaces of functions can be found in
Section

8
.)
A useful convention used in this monograph is that, for a vector- or matrix-valued function
f
, we will use the shorthand
f\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)
to denote that all components of
f
are in
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)
.
In situations where the measure
\mu
on
\Omega
is unambiguous we may write
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\Omega)
.
The Lebesgue spaces are obtained by identification of functions that agree up to a Lebesgue null set:

Definition 2.6

(Lebesgue spaces)
.

Let
1\leq s<\infty
.
The
Lebesgue space

L^{s}(\mu)
is the vector space whose elements are the equivalence classes
[f]\triangleq\{g\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu):g\sim f\}
of
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)
under the equivalence relation that
f\sim g
if and only if
|f-g|_{\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)}=0
.
The space
L^{s}(\mu)
becomes a normed vector space when equipped with

\|[f]\|_{L^{s}(\mu)}\triangleq|g|_{\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mu)},\quad g\in[f],

the latter being identical for all
g
and thus well-defined.

In the case where
\mu
is the Lebesgue measure
\lambda
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it is common to write
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\lambda)
as
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
L^{s}(\lambda)
as
L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

7.3
Random Variables and Independence

An (almost) equivalent, and often more intuitive, representation of probability measures is through the lens of random variables, which are now precisely defined:

Definition 2.7

(Random variable)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mu)
be a probability space, called the
sample space
, and let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}})
be a measurable space, called the
state space
.
A
random variable
with these sample and state spaces is a measurable function
X:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
.

A random variable
X
is associated with a probability measure
P
on the state space
\mathcal{X}
, called the
law
of
X
, defined as
P(S)=\mu(X^{-1}(S))
for each measurable set
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
, and we write
X\sim P
as shorthand.
The
expectation
of a random variable
X
is defined as the integral
\mu(X)
, and is conventionally denoted
\mathbb{E}[X]
.
Since the expectation is fully determined by the law, it is common to also write
\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}[Y]
where
Y
is understood to be any random variable with law
P
.
The set
\mathcal{S}_{X}
whose elements are the sets
X^{-1}(S)
,
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
, itself satisfies the axioms of a
\sigma
-algebra and is therefore called the
\sigma
-algebra
generated
by
X
.

Definition 2.8

(Independence)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mu)
be a probability space and let each
\mathcal{X}_{i}
be a measurable space, with index
i
running over a possibly uncountably infinite set
I
.
A collection
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
of random variables
X_{i}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{i}
are said to be
independent
if

\mu\left(\cap_{i\in I}S_{i}\right)=\prod_{i\in I}\mu(S_{i})

for all
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
with
S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}
for each
i\in I
.

Definition 2.9

(Conditional expectation)
.

Let
X:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
Y:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be random variables on a common probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mu)
.
A
conditional expectation
of
X
given
Y
is a measurable function, denoted
\mathbb{E}[X|Y]
, from
(\Omega,\mathcal{S}_{Y})
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which

\int\mathbb{E}[X|Y]\cdot 1_{S}\;\mathrm{d}\mu=\int X\cdot 1_{S}\;\mathrm{d}\mu

for all
S\in\mathcal{S}_{Y}
.

The
conditional probability
of an event
S\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
given
Y
is
\mu(S|Y)=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{1}_{S}|Y]
.

Definition 2.10

(Conditional independence)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mu)
be a probability space and let
\mathcal{X}_{i}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be measurable spaces, with index
i
running over a possibly uncountably infinite set
I
.
A collection
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
of random variables
X_{i}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{i}
are said to be
conditionally independent
given a random variable
Y:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
if,
\mu
-almost surely,

\mu\left(\cap_{i\in I}S_{i}\mid Y\right)=\prod_{i\in I}\mu(S_{i}|Y)

for all
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
with
S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{X_{i}}
for each
i\in I
.

The shorthand
X_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{j}|Y
is used to denote the statement that the random variables
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
are conditionally independent given
Y
.

7.4
Markov Processes

In dealing with collections of random variables
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
, the structure of the index set
I
, together with the conditional independence relationships among the random variables, are used to signify different settings of interest.

Definition 2.11

(Stochastic process)
.

Let
\Omega
be a probability space and
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space.
A
stochastic process
is a collection
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
of random variables
X_{t}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
with index
t
running over a set
T
.

In this monograph we will primarily be concerned with two cases; (1) the index set
T
is discrete, e.g.
t=0,1,2,\dots
, with the index
t
interpreted as a time associated with the random variable
X_{t}
; in this case the stochastic process is said to be a
discrete time
process; (2) there is a continuous temporal index, e.g.
t\in[0,\infty)
; in this case the stochastic processes is said to be a
continuous time
process.
In both cases the index set
T
is ordered.

Definition 2.12

(Markov process)
.

A stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
with the property that

X_{r}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{t}|X_{s}

for all
r<s<t
is said to be a
Markov process
.

Intuitively, this property means that the future states of the stochastic process are conditionally independent of the past states given the current state, and thus knowledge of the current state is sufficient for predicting future states.

The law associated to each random variable
X_{t}
is denoted
P_{t}
, which we recall is defined as
P_{t}(S)\triangleq\mu(X_{t}^{-1}(S))
.

Definition 2.13

(Markov kernel)
.

A Markov process is
time-homogeneous
if, for all
t\geq 0
, there exists a
Markov kernel

K_{t}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
such that

1.

x\mapsto K_{t}(x,S)
is measurable for each
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}

2.

S\mapsto K_{t}(x,S)
is a probability measure for each
x\in\mathcal{X}

3.

it holds that

P_{s+t}(S)=\int K_{t}(x,S)\;\mathrm{d}P_{s}(x)

for all
s\geq 0
and
S\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

The Markov processes that we consider in this monograph are all time-homogeneous, and are thus completely characterised by their initial distribution together with their Markov kernel.
Often we will implicitly assume time-homogeneity when describing a time-homogeneous Markov process in terms of its Markov kernel.

Definition 2.14

(Invariance)
.

A Markov kernel
K_{t}
is said to leave a probability measure
P

invariant
if

\displaystyle P(S)=\int K_{t}(x,S)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)

for all
t>0
and all
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Example 2.5

(Overdamped Langevin diffusion I)
.

Let
P
be a continuous distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with positive and differentiable density
p
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The continuous time process
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defined by the stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\log p)(X_{t})\;\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W_{t}

\displaystyle t>0

\displaystyle X_{0}\sim P_{0}

is a time-homogeneous Markov process, with a Markov kernel for which
P
is invariant, called the
overdamped Langevin diffusion
with
initial distribution

P_{0}
.
Here
\nabla\log p:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the gradient of
\log p:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denotes a standard
d
-dimensional Wiener process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; see Chapter 5 of
Oksendal 2013
.

Definition 2.15

(Generator of a Markov process)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a continuous time Markov process with Markov kernel
K_{t}
.
If it exists, the
infinitesimal generator
of the process is defined as the operator

(Af)(x)\triangleq\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\;\frac{1}{t}\left\{\int f\;\mathrm{d}K_{t}(x,\cdot)-f(x)\right\}

acting on sufficiently regular functions
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Example 2.6

(Overdamped Langevin diffusion II)
.

The infinitesimal generator of the overdamped Langevin diffusion from
Example

2.5
is the differential operator
(Af)(x)=(\Delta f)(x)+(\nabla f)(x)\cdot(\nabla\log p)(x)
; see Section 7.3 of
Oksendal 2013
.

Definition 2.16

(Ergodic process)
.

A stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
is said to be
ergodic
if the associated measures
P_{t}
converge to a limit
P
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, where the sense of convergence is to be specified.

Some different senses of convergence are discussed in
Section

9
.
One may also consider refined notions of ergodicity in which, for example, the rate of convergence is specified.
Further background on ergodicity of Markov processes can be found in
Gallegos-Herrada et al. 2024
.

8
Functional Analysis

Functional analysis concerns the mathematical properties of function spaces.
In this section we let
\mathcal{X}
be a set.

Definition 2.17

(Function space)
.

A
function space

\mathcal{F}
, in this book, is a vector space (over the reals) whose elements are functions of the form
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

The most common function spaces that we will encounter consist of functions that are scalar-valued (i.e.
d=1
), but we will also require vector-valued functions in
Section

18
and examples of such function spaces are discussed in
Section

8.3
.

Example 2.7

(Polynomial space I)
.

A familiar function space is the space of polynomials of fixed maximal order on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
: this is the set
\mathcal{F}_{p}\triangleq\{f(x)=a_{0}+a_{1}x+\dots+a_{p}x^{p}\;:\;a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{p}\in\mathbb{R}\}
equipped with pointwise addition and scalar multiplication, meaning that
(\alpha f+\beta g)(x)=\alpha f(x)+\beta g(x)
for all
f,g\in\mathcal{F}_{p}
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Function spaces can often be endowed with additional mathematical structure that is useful for theory and computation, and in particular we will refer to a
normed space
in the context of a function space when the function space is equipped with a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, and an
inner product space
when the function space is equipped with an inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
All inner products give rise to an associated
induced
norm, defined as
\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}}=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}}
, but not all norms are induced by an inner product.

Example 2.8

(Polynomial space II)
.

The polynomial space
\mathcal{F}_{p}
from
Example

2.7
is an inner product space when equipped with
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{F}_{p}}=a_{0}b_{0}+\dots+a_{p}b_{p}
where
f(x)=a_{0}+\dots+a_{p}x^{p}
and
g(x)=b_{0}+\dots+b_{p}x^{p}
.

Example 2.9

(Sup and bounded Lipschitz norms)
.

Let
\rho
be a metric on
\mathcal{X}
.
For a function space
\mathcal{F}
whose elements are continuous and bounded functions
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the
sup norm
and
Lipschitz semi-norm
, are defined, respectively, as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\infty}\triangleq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|f(x)\|,\qquad|f|_{\text{Lip},\rho}\triangleq\sup_{x\neq y,\;x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{\|f(x)-f(y)\|}{\rho(x,y)}.

The
bounded Lipschitz norm
is defined as
\|f\|_{\text{BL},\rho}\triangleq\|f\|_{\infty}+|f|_{\text{Lip},\rho}
.

In settings where the metric
\rho
is unambiguous, we will simply write
|f|_{\text{Lip}}
for
|f|_{\text{Lip},\rho}
.

A sequence
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements in a normed space
\mathcal{F}
is said to be
Cauchy
if for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|f_{i}-f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}}<\epsilon
for all
i,j>n
.
A normed space
\mathcal{F}
is said to be
complete
if all Cauchy sequences in
\mathcal{F}
also have a limit in
\mathcal{F}
(meaning that there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\|f_{i}-f\|_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow 0
as
i\rightarrow\infty
).

Definition 2.18

(Hilbert space)
.

An inner product space that is complete (with respect to the norm induced by the inner product) is called a
Hilbert space
.

Example 2.10

(Polynomial space III)
.

The polynomial space
\mathcal{F}_{p}
from
Example

2.7
is Hilbert.
Indeed, the map
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{p+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{p}
that sends a coefficient vector
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p+1}
to the corresponding polynomial
f(x)=a_{0}+\dots+a_{p}x^{p}
is an isometry of
\mathbb{R}^{p+1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{p}
, under which the completeness of
\mathbb{R}^{p+1}
is preserved.

Example 2.11

(Lebesgue space
L^{2}(\mu)
)
.

The Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}(\mu)
, introduced in
Definition

2.6
, are strictly speaking
not
function spaces because their elements are not functions
per se
, but rather equivalence classes of functions.
Nevertheless, in the case
p=2
the Lebesgue space admits a natural inner product

\displaystyle\langle[f],[g]\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\int f(\omega)g(\omega)\;\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega),

for which the induced norm is
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, and it can be verified that
L^{2}(\mu)
equipped with this inner product is Hilbert.

One of the most important mathematical results on Hilbert spaces is the
Riesz representer theorem
:

Theorem 2.2

(Riesz representation)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
L:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous linear functional, meaning that
L
is linear and that there exists a constant
C
such that
|L(f)|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}
.
Then
L(f)=\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
for some
g\in\mathcal{H}
.

The element
g
in
Theorem

2.2
is called the
Riesz representer
of
L
in
\mathcal{H}
.

8.1
Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces

The Lebesgue space
L^{2}(\mu)
from
Example

2.11
is Hilbert, but its elements are not functions that can be pointwise evaluated.
For the most part in this monograph, we will want to exploit the convenient mathematical structure of Hilbert spaces whilst excluding certain Hilbert spaces, such as
L^{2}(\mu)
, whose elements are more abstract.
To proceed, we will restrict attention to Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
for which pointwise evaluation
f\mapsto f(x)
is a continuous linear functional on
\mathcal{H}
, so that individual function values
f(x)
are well-defined for all elements
f\in\mathcal{H}
.

Definition 2.19

(Reproducing kernel Hilbert space)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a set.
A
reproducing kernel Hilbert space
is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of real-valued functions on
\mathcal{X}
, such that for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the map
f\mapsto f(x)
is a continuous linear functional on
\mathcal{H}
.

From
Theorem

2.2
, we deduce that
f\mapsto f(x)
has a Riesz representer which we denote
k_{x}\in\mathcal{H}
.
The
reproducing kernel
of the reproducing kernel Hilbert space is defined as a bivariate function
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
k(x,y)=\langle k_{x},k_{y}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.
This construction implies that
k_{x}(y)=\langle k_{y},k_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=k(y,x)
and we will therefore use the notation
k_{x}
and
k(\cdot,x)
interchangeably in the text.
It is straightforward to verify that a reproducing kernel
k
is symmetric and positive semi-definite and is therefore an instance of a
kernel
:

Definition 2.20

(Kernel)
.

A bivariate function
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is said to be a
kernel
on a set
\mathcal{X}
if it is

1.

symmetric:

k(x,y)=k(y,x)
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}

2.

positive semi-definite:
for all
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}k(x_{i},x_{j})\geq 0.

This second property of a kernel is closely related to a familiar concept from linear algebra:
A matrix
K\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is said to be
positive semi-definite
if
w^{\top}Kw\geq 0
for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and we write
K\succeq 0
.
If the inequality is strict for all
w\neq 0
, we say that the matrix
K
is
positive definite
, written
K\succ 0
.
Thus the second property in
Definition

2.24
states that all matrices
K
of the form
K_{i,j}=k(x_{i},x_{j})
are positive semi-definite for all choices of
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
If all such matrices are (strictly) positive definite then we say that
k
is a
positive definite
kernel.

A fundamental result in functional analysis is that reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces are completely characterized by their kernel:

Theorem 2.3

(Existence and uniqueness)
.

For all kernels
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, there exists a unique reproducing kernel Hilbert space for which
k
is a reproducing kernel, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
This Hilbert space is characterized as the unique Hilbert space for which

1.

k_{x}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

2.

f(x)=\langle f,k_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
,

with the latter called the
reproducing property
of the kernel.

The proof of
Theorem

2.3
is beyond the scope of this monograph; see Section 4.2 of
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
.
The proof itself is constructive, and demonstrates that the elements of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
are (limits of) finite linear combinations of the Riesz representers;

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k}\triangleq\overline{\mathrm{span}\left\{k_{x}\;|\;x\in\mathcal{X}\right\}}

where the bar notation denotes the completion taken with respect to the inner product

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}k_{x_{i}},\sum_{j=1}^{m}v_{j}k_{y_{j}}\right)\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}u_{i}v_{j}k(x_{i},y_{j}).

One route to establishing whether a general bivariate function
k
is or is not a kernel is to consider its Taylor series; we will not discuss this further, but see e.g. Lemma 4.8 of
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
.
Common examples of kernels on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
include the following:

Example 2.12

(Gaussian kernel)
.

The
Gaussian
kernel has the form

k(x,y)=\exp(-\|x-y\|^{2}/2\ell^{2})
, for some
\ell>0
.

Example 2.13

(Inverse multi-quadric kernel)
.

The
inverse multi-quadric
kernel has the form

k(x,y)=(\ell^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2})^{-\beta}
, for some
\ell>0
,
\beta\in(0,\infty)
.

The Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{k}
reproduced by the Gaussian and inverse multi-quadric kernels are "small", in the sense that their elements are functions whose derivatives of all orders exist.
To reproduce larger Hilbert spaces – for example, containing functions with only a finite number of derivatives – we can employ alternative kernels, such as the Matérn kernel:

Example 2.14

(Matérn kernel)
.

The Matérn kernel has the form

k(x,y)=\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|}{\ell}\right)\frac{s!}{(2s)!}\sum_{i=0}^{s}\frac{(s+i)!}{i!(s-i)!}\left(2\frac{\|x-y\|}{\ell}\right)^{s-i}

for some
\ell>0
,
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Elements of the Hilbert space reproduced by the Matérn kernel have partial derivatives up to order
s
existing in the weak sense, as elements of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
The parameter
\ell
, appearing in all of these kernels, is called a
bandwidth
or a
length-scale
of the kernel.

Each of the kernels in
Examples

2.12
,
2.13
, and
2.14
had the property that the value of
k(x,y)
depended on
x
and
y
only through the difference
x-y
; such kernels are called
translation-invariant
.
A famous theorem of Bochner characterizes all continuous translation-invariant kernels on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

Theorem 2.4

(Bochner’s Theorem; Theorem 6.6 of
Wendland 2004
)
.

A continuous function
k:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a translation-invariant kernel if and only if there exists a finite non-negative (Borel) measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, called the
spectral measure
, such that

\displaystyle k(x,y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int e^{-\mathrm{i}\langle x-y,\omega\rangle}\;\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)

(8)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

That is, all translation-invariant kernels
k
can be viewed as the Fourier transform of a certain non-negative measure
\mu
.
Further, it can be shown that a continuous, translation-invariant kernel
k
is positive definite whenever the
carrier
of the spectral measure
\mu
in (
8
), defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{carrier}(\mu)\triangleq\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{U:U\;\text{is open and}\;\mu(U)=0\},

contains an open subset; see
Chang 1996
and Theorem 6.8 of
Wendland 2004
.

Access to an explicit formula for a reproducing kernel allows us to perform calculations in reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, for example to calculate norms:

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}k(\cdot,x_{i})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}k(\cdot,x_{i}),\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}k(\cdot,x_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}\langle k(\cdot,x_{i}),k(\cdot x_{j})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}k(x_{i},x_{j})}.

This is quite a remarkable feature of reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, since in general it is difficult to obtain an explicit characterization of the elements of these spaces directly from the kernel.
Some notable exceptions, where both the reproducing kernel Hilbert space and the kernel are explicit, include:

Example 2.15

(Polynomial space IV)
.

The polynomial space
\mathcal{F}_{p}
from
Example

2.7
is an reproducing kernel Hilbert space with kernel
k(x,y)=1+(xy)+\dots+(xy)^{p}
.

Example 2.16

(Gaussian kernel II)
.

The Gaussian kernel
k
from
Example

2.12
reproduces (in dimension
d=1
for simplicity) the Hilbert space

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k}=\left\{f=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{i}\phi_{i}\;:\;\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}<\infty\right\},

where

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(x)\triangleq\frac{1}{\ell^{i}\sqrt{i!}}x^{i}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\right).

See
Steinwart et al. 2006
.

8.2
Kernel Mean Embedding

The convenient mathematical structure of reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces stands in contrast to the limited structures that are available on sets of probability measures, which are not vector spaces in general.
Nevertheless, it is possible to embed (sufficiently regular) probability measures into reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, in order that their mathematical structure can be exploited.
In this section,
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a positive definite kernel defined for a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
.
It is assumed that
k_{x}
is measurable for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, which is equivalent to assuming that the elements of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
are measurable
(
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, Lemma 4.24)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
be the set of probability distributions
\mu
on
\mathcal{X}
for which the map
\mathrm{I}_{\mu}:\mathcal{H}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by
\mathrm{I}_{\mu}(f)=\int f\;\mathrm{d}\mu
is a continuous linear functional.

Definition 2.21

(Kernel mean embedding)
.

For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
, the Riesz representer of
\mathrm{I}_{\mu}
is called the
kernel mean element
, denoted
\phi_{\mu}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
The map

\displaystyle\phi:\mathcal{P}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(k)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\phi_{\mu}

is called the
kernel mean embedding
of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
into
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.

This definition is well-defined as a consequence of
Theorem

2.2
.
The kernel mean embedding enables us to immediately make use of the Hilbert space structure of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
to perform analysis and computation on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.
For example we may measure the dissimilarity between a pair of measures
\mu
and
\nu
in terms of the magnitude of the vector
\phi_{\mu}-\phi_{\nu}
that connects their kernel mean elements
\phi_{\mu}
and
\phi_{\nu}
; we will return to this idea in detail in
Section

9.3
.

To calculate the kernel mean element associated to a probability measure
\mu
, we can take
f=k_{x}
in the Riesz representation statement
\mathrm{I}_{\mu}(f)=\langle f,\phi_{\mu}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
to see immediately that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mu}(x)=\int k(x,y)\;\mathrm{d}\mu(y).

(9)

For certain pairs of probability measure
\mu
and kernel
k
, such as the Gaussian distribution paired with the Gaussian kernel, it is possible to analytically calculate the kernel mean element
(
Briol et al. 2025
)
.
Outside of these tractable cases, numerical methods can be used to approximate the kernel mean element.
For example, if
\int k(x,x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty
then the
Monte Carlo
estimator

\displaystyle\phi_{\mu}^{n}(\cdot)\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(\cdot,Y_{i}),\qquad Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mu

(10)

provides a strongly consistent approximation to
\phi_{\mu}
, in the sense that
\|\phi_{\mu}^{n}-\phi_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\rightarrow 0
almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
It is useful also to note that
\phi_{\mu}^{n}
can be interpreted as the
exact
kernel mean element associated to the approximating measure
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{Y_{i}}
.
See
Section

9.3
for further discussion on Monte Carlo estimation of the kernel mean element.
Several more sophisticated approaches to approximating a kernel mean element have been developed, under names such as quasi-Monte Carlo, determinantal point processes, gradient flows, and kernel herding; we do not attempt to survey these in detail here, but a subset of these algorithms with particular relevance to Stein’s method are described in
Section

28
.

In general it is not possible to embed all probability measures into the same Hilbert space, and for a given kernel
k
the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
determines which measures can be safely embedded.
Sufficient conditions for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
will now be discussed.
Recall that
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
denotes the vector space of functions
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for which the semi-norm
|f|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)}=\int|f|^{p}\;\mathrm{d}\mu
is finite, from
Section

7.2
.
Our presentation here follows
Barp et al. 2024
.

Definition 2.22

(Scalarly integrable)
.

A map
\Phi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{k}
is said to be
scalarly

\mu
-
integrable
if
\{x\mapsto\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}:h\in\mathcal{H}_{k}\}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.

The argument used in the following proof can be traced back to
Dunford 1937
, with our account based on
Schwabik and Ye 2005
.

Proposition 2.1
.

If
\Phi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{k}
is scalarly
\mu
-integrable, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{\Phi}:\mathcal{H}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\int\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

is a continuous linear functional.

Proof.

First we claim that the graph of the linear map

\displaystyle T:\mathcal{H}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(x\mapsto\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}).

is closed.
To see this, let
h_{n}\rightarrow h
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
and suppose that
T(h_{n})\rightarrow g
in
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.
The claim is that
g
and
T(h)
are equal in
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.
Since every sequence converging in
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
has an almost surely converging subsequence, there is a subsequence
(h_{n_{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\langle h_{n_{i}},\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\rightarrow g(x)

for
\mu
-almost all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
Since
\langle h_{n},\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\rightarrow\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, it follows that
g(x)=\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=T(h)(x)
for
\mu
-almost all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
Thus
g
and
T(h)
are equal in
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
and the graph of
T
is indeed closed.
The conditions of the closed graph theorem, which states that a linear map
T
between Banach spaces is bounded if and only if its graph is closed, have now been verified.
Thus
T
is continuous and

\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1}\int|T(h)|\;\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty.

This allows us to conclude that
\mathrm{I}_{\Phi}
is a continuous linear functional, since

\displaystyle|\mathrm{I}_{\Phi}(h)|=\left|\int\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int|\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}|\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int|T(h)|\;\mathrm{d}P\mu\leq\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}},

as required.
∎

These results furnish the following elegant characterization of the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
of probability measures that can be safely embedded into
\mathcal{H}_{k}
:

Proposition 2.2

(Characterization of
\mathcal{P}_{k}
)
.

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
if and only if
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.

Proof.

Take
\Phi(x)=k(\cdot,x)
to be the
canonical feature map
, so that
k
being measurable implies the scalar functions
x\mapsto\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=h(x)
are measurable and
\mathrm{I}_{\mu}=\mathrm{I}_{\Phi}
.
If
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
then
x\mapsto k(\cdot,x)
is scalarly
\mu
-integrable, and
Proposition

2.1
shows that
\mathrm{I}_{\mu}
is a continuous linear functional.
Conversely, if
\mathcal{H}_{k}\not\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
then it is clear that
\mathrm{I}_{\mu}
is not continuous and thus
\mu\notin\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.
∎

The question of which measures can be embedded then reduces to whether
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.
A well-known sufficient condition is provided in the following result:

Proposition 2.3
.

If
\int\sqrt{k(x,x)}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty
then
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.

Proof.

For
h\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
, from the reproducing property and Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\int|h(x)|\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int|\langle h,k(\cdot,x)\rangle|\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\int\|k(\cdot,x)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

where the reproducing property again yields
\|k(\cdot,x)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sqrt{k(x,x)}
, as required.
∎

One can weaken the above integrability condition under mild assumptions on
k
and
\mathcal{X}
:

Proposition 2.4
.

If
k
is continuous,
\mathcal{X}
is separable, and

\iint|k(x,y)|\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)<\infty,

then
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.

Proof.

Since
k
is measurable and real-valued,
k
is
strongly measurable
in the sense of
Carmeli et al. 2006
.
Further, since
k
is strongly measurable and
\iint|k(x,y)|\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)<\infty
, then
k
is
\infty
-bounded
in the sense of
Carmeli et al. 2006
; see
Carmeli et al. 2006
.
Since
k
is continuous and
\mathcal{X}
is separable, it follows that
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is separable
(
Carmeli et al. 2006
, Corollary 5.2)
.
Since
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is separable,
k
being
\infty
-bounded is equivalent to
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)

(
Carmeli et al. 2006
, Proposition 4.4)
.
∎

8.3
Vector-Valued Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces

The development of multivariate Stein discrepancies later in this monograph relies on a generalization of reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces to spaces of functions that are vector-valued.
To generalize the concept of a bounded linear functional, which was used to define (scalar-valued) reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces in
Definition

2.19
, we say that a map
L:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
acting on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is a
continuous linear operator
if
L
is linear and there exists a constant
C
such that
\|L(f)\|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}
.

Definition 2.23

(Vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a set.
A
vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space
is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of vector-valued functions on
\mathcal{X}
, such that for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the map
f\mapsto f(x)
is a continuous linear operator on
\mathcal{H}
.

If
L:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a continuous linear operator, then the components
L_{i}
of
L
are each continuous linear functionals and the Riesz representation theorem can be applied.
It follows that, if
\mathcal{H}
is a vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space whose elements are functions of the form
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}

there exists a Riesz representer
K_{x,i}\in\mathcal{H}
for the functional
f\mapsto f_{i}(x)
, meaning that
f_{i}(x)=\langle f,K_{x,i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}
.
The
reproducing kernel
of a vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space is defined as a bivariate function
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
K_{i,j}(x,y)=\langle K_{x,i},K_{y,j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.
A reproducing kernel
K
can be verified to be an instance of a
matrix-valued kernel
:

Definition 2.24

(Matrix-valued kernel)
.

A bivariate function
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
, is said to be a
kernel
on a set
\mathcal{X}
if it is

1.

transpose-symmetric
;
K(x,y)=K(y,x)^{\top}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}

2.

positive semi-definite
;

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle u_{i},K(x_{i},x_{j})u_{j}\rangle\geq 0

(11)

for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
, all
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

For clarity we emphasize that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, appearing in (
11
) is the usual Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
K_{x}\triangleq K(\cdot,x)
, so that
K_{x}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is matrix-valued.
In analogy with the case of a scalar-valued kernel, there is a unique Hilbert space reproduced by a given kernel:

Theorem 2.5

(Existence and uniqueness)
.

For all kernels
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, there exists a unique Hilbert space for which
K
is a reproducing kernel, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
This Hilbert space is characterized as the unique Hilbert space for which

1.

K_{x}u\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

2.

\langle f,K_{x}u\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}=\langle f(x),u\rangle
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

with the latter called the
reproducing property
of the kernel.

The proof of
Theorem

2.5
can be found in
Carmeli et al. 2006
.
The elements of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
are (limits of) finite linear combinations of vector-valued functions of the form
K_{x}u
, where
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
; that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{K}\triangleq\overline{\mathrm{span}\{K_{x}u:x\in\mathcal{X},u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}}

where here the completion is taken with respect to the inner product

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{x_{i}}u_{i},\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{y_{j}}v_{j}\right)\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle u_{i},K(x_{i},y_{j})v_{j}\rangle.

The simplest examples of matrix-valued kernels are those constructed from scalar-valued kernels, for which the corresponding vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces can be explicitly characterized:

Example 2.17

(Diagonal matrix-valued kernel)
.

Let
k_{i}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a (scalar-valued) kernel for each
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
.
Then
K(x,y)\triangleq\mathrm{diag}(k_{1}(x,y),\dots,k_{d}(x,y))
is a matrix-valued kernel.
The associated vector-valued Hilbert space has inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\langle f_{i},g_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{i}}}.

Example 2.18

(Tensor product matrix-valued kernel)
.

Let
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a (scalar-valued) kernel and let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a symmetric positive definite matrix.
Then
K(x,y)\triangleq k(x,y)B
is a matrix-valued kernel.
The associated vector-valued Hilbert space has inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}B_{i,j}\langle f_{i},g_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}.

9
Statistical Divergences

This section introduces tools that can be used to quantify the extent to which two probability distributions differ.
The abstract formulation is that of a
statistical divergence
:

Definition 2.25

(Statistical divergence)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the set of probability measures on a common measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
.
A map
D:\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
is called a
statistical divergence
if
D(\mu,\nu)\geq 0
with equality if and only if
\mu=\nu
.

Any metric on
\mathcal{P}
is automatically a statistical divergence, but in general a statistical divergence need not be symmetric or satisfy a triangle inequality.
In what follows we review several existing statistical divergences, noting that explicit computation of these divergences may be difficult or impossible in the context of the problems that motivate the use of Stein’s method.

9.1
Classical Divergences

Perhaps the most classical statistical divergence is the Kullback–Leibler divergence.
Recall that the concept of a Radon–Nikodym derivative introduced in
Section

7.2
.

Definition 2.26

(Kullback–Leibler divergence)
.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be probability measures on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
with
\mu\ll\nu
.
The
Kullback–Leibler divergence
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\mu||\nu)\triangleq\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu}\right)\;\mathrm{d}\mu

with the convention that
\mathrm{KL}(\mu||\nu)
is infinite whenever the integral is ill-defined.

The Kullback–Leibler divergence is indeed a statistical divergence, since
\log(x)\leq x-1
implies

\mathrm{KL}(\mu||\nu)=-\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right)\;\mathrm{d}\mu\geq-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}-1\;\mathrm{d}\mu=0

with equality if and only if
(\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}\nu)(\omega)=1
for
\nu
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega
.
The Kullback–Leibler divergence is central to the field of information theory, where it is known as the
relative entropy
from
\nu
to
\mu
.
Compared to other statistical divergences (see below), the Kullback–Leibler divergence is a strong notion of divergence, requiring that both
\mu
and
\nu
have the same support.
For example, if
x_{n}\rightarrow x
with
x_{n}\neq x
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu_{n}=\delta_{x_{n}}
and
\nu=\delta_{x}
, then
\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{n}||\nu)\nrightarrow 0
.
Here
\delta_{x}
denotes the atomic measure at
x
, defined in
Example

2.1
.

An alternative and often more useful notion is that of
weak convergence
.
There are several equivalent formulations of weak convergence, but from the point of view of statistical divergences we can metrize weak convergence using the Dudley metric
(
Dudley 2018
, Chapter 11)
:

Definition 2.27

(Dudley metric)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\rho)
be a metric space equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
Let
\|\cdot\|_{\text{BL},\rho}
denote the bounded Lipschitz norm from
Example

2.9
.
For
\mu
and
\nu
(Borel) probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
, the
Dudley
(or
bounded Lipschitz
)
metric
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{BL}_{\rho}(\mu,\nu)\triangleq\sup_{\|f\|_{\text{BL},\rho}\leq 1}\mu(f)-\nu(f),

where we recall the shorthand
\mu(f)=\int f\;\mathrm{d}\mu
introduced in (
7
).

A sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of probability distributions is said to
converge in distribution
to a a probability distribution
\nu
if
\mathrm{BL}_{\rho}(\mu_{n},\nu)\rightarrow 0
, and we use the shorthand
P_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}P
.
Equivalently, a sequence of random variables
X_{n}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
defined on a common probability space
\Omega
with
X_{n}\sim P_{n}
, is said to
converge weakly
to a random variable
X:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
with
X\sim P
if
P_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}P
.
It is straightforward to verify that
\mathrm{BL}_{\rho}
is a metric on the set of all (Borel) probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
, and in particular it is a statistical divergence.

Computation of the Kullback–Leibler divergence and the Dudley metric requires detailed information about both the distributions
\mu
and
\nu
, either through their Radon–Nikodym derivative or their generalized moments.
However, in our motivating context it is the case that one of the distributions involved is implicitly defined via an intractable normalization constant.
In such cases alternative statistical divergences are needed.

9.2
Wasserstein Metric

An important statistical divergence is given by the
Wasserstein metric
.
For
\mu
and
\nu
probability distributions on a metric space
(\mathcal{X},\rho)
, a
coupling
of
\mu
and
\nu
is a probability distribution on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
whose marginals are
\mu(S)=\gamma(S\times\mathcal{X})
and
\nu(S)=\gamma(\mathcal{X}\times S)
.
Let
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
denote the set of all possible couplings of
\mu
and
\nu
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{s}(\mathcal{X})
denote the set of probability distributions
\mu
on a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
for which the
s
th moment
\int\rho(x,x_{0})^{s}\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
is finite, for any (and therefore all)
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 2.28

(
s
-Wasserstein metric)
.

Let
\rho
be a metric on a Radon
1
1

1

All separable complete metric spaces are Radon.
space
\mathcal{X}
.
For
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{s}(\mathcal{X})
, the
s
-
Wasserstein metric
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{s}(\mu,\nu)\triangleq\left(\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\int\rho(x,y)^{s}\;\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\right)^{1/s}.

It can be proven that
Definition

2.28
defines a metric on the set
\mathcal{P}_{s}(\mathcal{X})
, and in particular a statistical divergence
(
Clement and Desch 2008
)
.
The
s
-Wasserstein metric is routinely used in applications of optimal transport since it is naturally adapted to the metric structure of
\mathcal{X}
; for example,
\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{s}(\delta_{x},\delta_{y})=\rho(x,y)
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.
Convergence of a sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to a limit
\nu
in the sense of
\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{s}(\mu_{n},\nu)\rightarrow 0
is equivalent to the statement that both
\mu_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\nu
and
\int\rho(x,x_{0})^{s}\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x)\rightarrow\int\rho(x,x_{0})^{s}\;\mathrm{d}\nu
for some
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
.
Thus in general the
s
-Wasserstein metric induces a stronger topology compared to the Dudley metric from
Definition

2.27
.
This fact is especially clear in the case
s=1
, where the 1-Wasserstein metric has the dual representation

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{1}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{|f|_{\text{Lip},\rho}\leq 1}\mu(f)-\nu(f),

(12)

where
|\cdot|_{\text{Lip},\rho}
is the Lipschitz semi-norm from
Example

2.9
.
Thus, compared to the Dudley metric, the 1-Wasserstein metric involves a supremum over a larger set of test functions
f
by relaxing the boundedness requirement.
Of course, since continuous functions are automatically bounded on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the topology induced by the 1-Wasserstein metric coincides with that of the Dudley metric when
\mathcal{X}
is compact.
In settings where the metric
\rho
is unambiguous, we will simply write
\mathrm{W}^{s}
for
\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{s}
.

In addition to the standard 1-Wasserstein metric, we also introduce a
tilted
counterpart which, given a
tilting function

t:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
sets
2
2

2

To be clear, here
tf
denotes the pointwise product
(tf)(x)\triangleq t(x)f(x)
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{1,t}(\mu,\nu)\triangleq\sup_{|f|_{\mathrm{Lip},\rho}\leq 1}\mu(tf)-\nu(tf).

There is no general dominance relation between tilted Wasserstein distances; the topologies they induce are different and depend on the tilting function
t
.
Note that the standard 1-Wasserstein distance (
12
) is recovered when
t(x)\equiv 1
.
As with the standard Wasserstein distances, the tilted Wasserstein distances induces much weaker topologies than, for example, divergences such as Kullback–-Leibler or Hellinger, since they do not require absolute continuity of measures.

Though the Wasserstein metrics are elegant and natural, they are not well-suited to computation in our motivating context.
Indeed, computing
\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{s}(\mu,\nu)
requires integration under
\mu
and
\nu
, which is typically intractable for at least one of the distributions involved.
Numerical approximations schemes can sometimes be used, but these encounter prohibitive
sample complexity
:
Suppose that
\mu
and
\nu
are defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and that we have access to each distribution only via a (possibly large) number
n
of independent samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim\mu
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\sim\nu
.
A natural approach to approximate
\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{s}(\mu,\nu)
is to use the plug-in estimator
\mathrm{W}_{\rho}^{s}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})
where
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
and
\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{Y_{i}}
.
However, this approximation incurs an error of size
O_{P}(n^{1/d})
.
That is, the difficulty of computing Wasserstein metrics increases exponentially in the dimension
d
of the domain on which the distributions are defined; see
Sriperumbudur et al. 2012
.
Furthermore, since one of our main motivations was to use a discrepancy to assess the quality of approximate sampling algorithms, requiring an exact sample to carry out this assessment leads to a chicken-and-egg problem.

9.3
Maximum Mean Discrepancy

The maximum mean discrepancy, introduced in this section and based on the kernel mean embedding introduced in
Section

8.2
, offers an alternative to the 1-Wasserstein metric for which the sample complexity is essentially dimension-independent.
Recall that the kernel mean element corresponding to a distribution
\mu
is denoted
\phi_{\mu}
; see
Definition

2.21
.

Definition 2.29

(Maximum mean discrepancy)
.

Let
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a positive definite kernel and let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.
The
maximum mean discrepancy
between two distributions
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
is
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)\triangleq\|\phi_{\mu}-\phi_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.

To understand why the maximum mean discrepancy enjoys favorable sample complexity compared to the Wasserstein metrics, we first derive an equivalent form that is more algebraically explicit.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\|\phi_{\mu}-\phi_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{\mu}-\phi_{\nu},\phi_{\mu}-\phi_{\nu}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

(13)

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{\mu},\phi_{\mu}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}-2\langle\phi_{\mu},\phi_{\nu}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle\phi_{\nu},\phi_{\nu}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

where, considering for example the term
\langle\phi_{\mu},\phi_{\nu}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
, we have from the fact that
\phi_{\nu}
is the Riesz representer of
\mathrm{I}_{\nu}
and (
9
) that

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\nu},\phi_{\mu}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\mathrm{I}_{\nu}(\phi_{\mu})

\displaystyle=\int\phi_{\mu}(y)\;\mathrm{d}\nu(y)

\displaystyle=\iint k(x,y)\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(y).

(14)

Proceeding similarly with all three terms in (
13
) results in the explicit expression

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}^{2}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\iint k(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)-2\iint k(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\iint k(x,y)\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(y).

(15)

In settings where the integrals in (
15
) cannot be exactly computed, but where it is nevertheless possible to sample from
\mu
and
\mu
, the maximum mean discrepancy could be approximated by replacing the intractable kernel mean elements
\phi_{\mu}
and
\phi_{\nu}
with their respective Monte Carlo estimators
\phi_{\mu}^{n}
and
\phi_{\nu}^{n}
, respectively based on independent samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim\mu
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\sim\nu
, as defined in (
10
).
From our observation following (
10
) that
\phi_{\mu}^{n}
can be interpreted as the
exact
kernel mean element corresponding to the approximating measure
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
, it follows that the plug-in estimator
\|\phi_{\mu}^{n}-\phi_{\nu}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
carries the dual interpretation of computing the
exact

\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})
between the two approximating measures
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
and
\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{Y_{j}}
.
From this perspective, it is trivial to obtain conditions under which the approximation to maximum mean discrepancy obtained in this manner is consistent.
Indeed, from (
15
) we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}^{2}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}k(X_{i},X_{i^{\prime}})-2\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}k(X_{i},Y_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}k(Y_{j},Y_{j^{\prime}})

(16)

where each of these three terms represents a Monte Carlo approximation of the corresponding integral in (
15
) whose error converges at
O_{P}(n^{-1/2})
.
As such, the sample complexity of maximum mean discrepancy is essentially dimension-independent.
Furthermore, the apparent
O(n^{2})
computational complexity associated with the Monte Carlo estimator (
16
) can be mitigated by noting that computation is "embarrassingly parallel".

Though the maximum mean discrepancy can have computational advantages compared with the Wasserstein metrics, they are similarly poorly-suited to computation in our motivating context.
Indeed, approximating
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)
using exact samples from
\mu
and
\nu
is problematic since one of our main motivations was to use a discrepancy to assess the quality of approximate sampling algorithms, leading again to a chicken-and-egg problem.
Nevertheless, it is instructive to understand the appealing theoretical properties of maximum mean discrepancy, as we will later seek to construct Stein discrepancies that enjoy similar appeal.

The maximum mean discrepancy is also known as the
worst case cubature error
due to its dual interpretation as

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1}\mu(f)-\nu(f).

(17)

From this perspective, it is clear that the kernel
k
determines the topology induced by maximum mean discrepancy, through determining the regularity of functions contained in the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
In particular, if
k(x,y)=\varphi(x-y)
for a Lipschitz function
\varphi
with Lipschitz constant
L_{\varphi}
, then
\{f:\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1\}\subset\{f:|f|_{\text{Lip}}\leq L_{\varphi}\}
, in which case the topology of maximum mean discrepancy is weaker than or equal to that of the 1-Wasserstein distance, since
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)\leq L_{\varphi}\mathrm{W}^{1}(\mu,\nu)
.
A kernel that reproduces a sufficiently large Hilbert space will, intuitively, contain enough test functions to be capable of distinguishing between different probability measures:

Definition 2.30

(Characteristic kernel)
.

A kernel
k
is said to be
characteristic
if
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)=0
implies
\mu=\nu
for all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.

Example 2.19

(Polynomial kernel is not characteristic)
.

From
Example

2.15
we have the kernel
k(x,y)=1+(xy)+\dots+(xy)^{p}
, which reproduces a Hilbert space whose elements are the polynomials of degree at most
p
on the domain
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
.
Thus
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)=0
if and only if the moments
\int x^{i}\;\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
and
\int x^{i}\;\mathrm{d}\nu(x)
are identical for
i=1,\dots,p
.
In particular,
k
is
not
a characteristic kernel.

Example 2.20
.

The Gaussian kernel
k(x,y)=\exp(-\|x-y\|^{2}/(2\ell^{2}))
is a characteristic kernel on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for all
\ell>0
.

The characteristic property says nothing about "small" values of
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)
, only about the case when
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu,\nu)
is exactly 0.
Thus characteristicness on its own does not provide strong justification for using a kernel
k
to measure the discrepancy between
\mu
and
\nu
.
For this reason we now introduce a stronger property, called (ironically, in this context)
weak convergence control
.

Definition 2.31

(Weak convergence control)
.

A kernel
k
is said to have
weak convergence control
if
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(\mu_{n},\mu)\rightarrow 0
implies
\mu_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\mu
.

Convergence control justifies the use of maximum mean discrepancy as an optimization criterion for the purposes of quantization and more general distributional approximation.
See
Muandet et al. 2017
for a general discussion of applications in which maximum mean discrepancy is often used.

Remark 2.1
.

Perhaps surprisingly, for a compact Hausdorff space
\mathcal{X}
, a bounded and measurable characteristic kernel
k
is guaranteed to have weak convergence control.
This equivalence no longer holds when the domain
\mathcal{X}
is non-compact, and a bounded and characteristic kernel can fail to have weak convergence control; see
Simon-Gabriel et al. 2023
.
Clearly a kernel that is not characteristic fails to have weak convergence control.

Example 2.21
.

The Gaussian kernel
k(x,y)=\exp(-\|x-y\|^{2}/(2\ell^{2}))
controls weak convergence of probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{d}
.
It can also be shown that the Gaussian kernel controls weak convergence on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
; this can be deduced from e.g. Theorem 7 of
Simon-Gabriel et al. 2023
and the general results in
Sriperumbudur et al. 2011
.

Chapter 3
Stein Operators

This chapter introduces the concept of a Stein operator.
Informally, a Stein operator is a mapping that generates functions that integrate to zero under a target probability measure
P
.
Stein operators are the key ingredient that we will use to construct Stein discrepancies in
Chapter

4
.
This chapter will make extensive use of the shorthand notation
P(f)=\int f\;\mathrm{d}P
, introduced in
Equation

7
.

Definition 3.1

(Stein operator and Stein set)
.

Let
P
be a probability distribution on a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
.
We call a set
\mathcal{G}
a
Stein set
and a linear map
\mathcal{T}_{P}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
a
Stein operator
for
P
if

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0\quad\text{for all}\quad g\in\mathcal{G}.

(18)

The integral
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)
appearing in
Definition

3.1
can be viewed as the expectation of a random variable
(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(X)
when
X\sim P
, and in this sense
\mathcal{T}_{P}
is a Stein operator when
(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(X)
has mean zero for all elements
g
in the stein set
\mathcal{G}
.

There are numerous constructions for Stein operators, but, from both a practical and theoretical standpoint,
not all Stein operators are equal.
For example, at one extreme we have the always-zero Stein operator

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=0,

(19)

which is simple to compute but provides absolutely no information about the target distribution
P
.
At the other extreme, we have the mean-recentering Stein operator

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=g(x)-P(g)

(20)

with Stein set
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.
This operator is ideal from the standpoint of theoretical analysis but is only computable when integration under
P
is tractable. Sadly, for most of our applications of interest, exact integration under
P
is decidedly intractable.
In fact, for many applications, our ultimate goal is to approximate intractable integrals under
P
or to assess approximations thereof.

Remarkably, there are computable Stein operators that yield useful discrepancies without requiring explicit integration under
P
. For example,
Stein 1986
showed that if
P
is a univariate probability distribution on
\mathbb{R}
with a positive and differentiable probability density function
p
(see
Definition

2.5
) then, for all differentiable functions
g
in an appropriate Stein set
\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=g\textquoteright(x)+g(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\log p(x)

(21)

is a Stein operator for
P
.
Indeed, integrating by parts

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=\int\mathcal{T}_{P}g\;\mathrm{d}P

\displaystyle=\int\left[g\textquoteright(x)+g(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\log p(x)\right]\;p(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

\displaystyle=\int\left[g\textquoteright(x)+\frac{g(x)p^{\prime}(x)}{p(x)}\right]\;p(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

(22)

\displaystyle=\int g^{\prime}(x)p(x)+g(x)p^{\prime}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=\int(gp)^{\prime}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

which from the fundamental theorem of calculus will be equal to zero provided that
g(x)p(x)\rightarrow 0
as
|x|\rightarrow\infty
.

The operator
Eq.

21
is called Stein’s
density method
operator, as it exchanges explicit integration under
P

Eq.

20
for evaluation of the log density derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\log p(x)
.
Importantly, this log density derivative is often computable even when exact integration under
p
is intractable.
For example, if
P
is a posterior distribution arising from a Bayesian statistical analysis (c.f.
Section

2
), then Bayes’ theorem implies that
p(x)\propto\pi(x)\mathcal{L}(x)
where
\pi(x)
is the density of the prior distribution and
\mathcal{L}
is the likelihood.
These two quantities are usually explicit and are sufficient for use of the density method since

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\log p(x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\log\pi(x)+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\log\mathcal{L}(x),

which can in principle be computed provided that both the prior and the likelihood can each be differentiated.
Indeed, the fact that gradients of the log posterior density can be computed without access to the intractable marginal likelihood underpins a wide range of approximate sampling algorithms used in Bayesian statistical contexts
(
Brooks et al. 2011
)
.

In this Chapter, we will explore similarly practical Stein operators for a wide variety of target distributions
P
.
Our starting point is a canonical Stein operator for distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, called the Langevin Stein operator, which we present in
Section

10
.
The Langevin Stein operator is a special case of a more general construction called a diffusion Stein operator, which we present in
Section

11
.
Often we encounter distributions whose domain is a subset
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for which special considerations are required when designing a Stein operator; for these situations we present the mirrored Stein operator in
Section

12
.
On the other hand, we might seek to avoid computation of gradients of the density of the target distribution
P
, and gradient-free Stein operators have been developed for this situation, as discussed in
Section

13
.
Finally we discuss the case of a discrete state space
\mathcal{X}
, for which several different Stein operators have been developed, as described in
Section

14
.

10
Langevin Operator

Stein’s density method operator
Eq.

21
can be applied to univariate distributions
P
, but we would like to develop analogous operators for the multivariate distributions commonly encountered in Bayesian statistics and probabilistic inference, where
P
is supported on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
To achieve this, we will first consider a beautiful idea due to
Barbour 1988
;
Barbour 1990
;
Gotze 1991
, known today as the
generator method
.
Barbour and Götze noticed that if one can identify a (time-homogeneous) Markov process for which
P
is invariant (
Definition

2.14
) then, under mild conditions, the infinitesimal generator (
Definition

2.15
) of that Markov process is a Stein operator for
P
. This is intuitively sensible; if we initialize a Markov process at a state sampled from
P
, then the distribution of later states will also be
P
provided that the process is
P
-invariant.
The generator of a Markov process tells us the rate of change in the expected value of a test function, which is zero for a process initialized at an invariant distribution.

Gorham and Mackey 2015
instantiated this idea for a specific Markov process – the overdamped Langevin diffusion introduced in
Example

2.5
.

Assumption 3.1

(Regularity of
P
)
.

Let
P
be a probability distribution with a positive and differentiable density
p
on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Under
3.1
, the infinitesimal generator of the overdamped Langevin diffusion is the differential operator
u\mapsto(\Delta u)+(\nabla u)\cdot(\nabla\log p)
; see
Example

2.6
.
Replacing
\nabla u
with a more general vector-valued function
g
, we arrive at what is now called the Langevin Stein operator:

Definition 3.2

(Langevin Stein operator;
Gorham and Mackey 2015
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, the
Langevin Stein operator
for
P
satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=(\nabla\cdot g)(x)+g(x)\cdot(\nabla\log p)(x)

(23)

for suitably regular vector-valued functions
