g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The Langevin Stein operator exactly recovers the density method operator
Eq.

21
in the case
d=1
.
Let us now be more precise about the regularity that we require of the vector-valued function
g
appearing in
Definition

3.2
.
Recall from
Section

7.2
that, for a vector-valued function
g
, we use the shorthand
g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
to denote that all components of
g
are in
\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.
For what follows we will require a version of the divergence theorem that applies to potentially unbounded domains.
To this end, we first present several useful versions of the divergence theorem and their proofs in full.
We do this to emphasize that continuity of the divergence – which is typically assumed in most textbook treatments of the divergence theorem – is
not
required for the conclusion of the divergence theorem to hold:

Theorem 3.1

(Divergence theorem on
[-1,1]^{d}
)
.

Suppose that the divergence
\nabla\cdot{v}
exists everywhere on
\mathcal{X}\triangleq[-1,1]^{d}
for a vector-valued function
v:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
If
\nabla\cdot{v}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

where
\mathrm{n}(x)
is the outward pointing unit normal at
x\in\partial\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

For each dimension
j\in[d]
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, let
x_{-j}
represent the subvector of
x
with the
j
-th coordinate removed.
Since
\nabla\cdot{v}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
, Fubini’s theorem
(
Rudin 1987
, Thm. 8.8)
implies that
x_{j}\mapsto\nabla_{j}v_{j}(x)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}([-1,1])
for each
j\in[d]
and almost every
x_{-j}\in[-1,1]^{d-1}
.
Since, in addition,
\nabla\cdot{v}
exists everywhere on
\mathcal{X}
, the fundamental theorem of calculus
(
Rudin 1987
, Thm. 7.21)
implies that

\displaystyle{v_{j}(x)}\big|_{x_{j}=1}-{v_{j}(x)}\big|_{x_{j}=-1}=\int_{-1}^{1}\nabla_{j}v_{j}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x_{j}

for each
j\in[d]
and almost every
x_{-j}\in[-1,1]^{d-1}
.
Hence we find that

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{[-1,1]^{d-1}}{v_{j}(x)}\big|_{x_{j}=1}-{v_{j}(x)}\big|_{x_{j}=-1}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x_{-j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{[-1,1]^{d-1}}\int_{-1}^{1}\nabla_{j}v_{j}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x_{j}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x_{-j}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x,

where the final step again uses Fubini’s theorem.
∎

Theorem 3.2

(Divergence theorem on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
)
.

Suppose that the divergence
\nabla\cdot{v}
exists everywhere on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
for a vector-valued function
v:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
If
\nabla\cdot{v}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
then

\displaystyle\int(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=\lim_{r\to\infty}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

where, for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{X}_{r}\triangleq[-r,r]^{d}
and
\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)
is the outward pointing unit normal at
x\in\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}
.
If, in addition,
v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, then

\displaystyle\int(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=0.

Proof.

Since
\nabla\cdot{v}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
exists everywhere on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the first result follows from
Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem
(
Rudin 1987
, Thm. 1.34)
and the divergence theorem (
Theorem

3.1
):

\displaystyle\int(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

\displaystyle=\int\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathbb{I}\left[{x\in\mathcal{X}_{r}}\right](\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{r}}(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=\lim_{r\to\infty}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x.

Now suppose
v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
as well and, for each
j\in[d]
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, define the functions

\displaystyle f_{j}(r)\triangleq\int{|v_{j}(x)|}\big|_{x_{j}=r}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x_{-j}\geq\int_{\left\|{x_{-j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq|r|}{|v_{j}(x)|}\big|_{x_{j}=r}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x_{-j}

where
x_{-j}
is the subvector of
x
obtained by dropping the
j
-th coordinate.
This definition provides the bound

\displaystyle\left|\int(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x\right|

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to\infty}\left|\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x\right|

\displaystyle=\liminf_{r\to\infty}\left|\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x\right|

\displaystyle=\liminf_{r\to\infty}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\left\|{x_{-j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq r}{v_{j}(x)}\big|_{x_{j}=r}-{v_{j}(x)}\big|_{x_{j}=-r}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x_{-j}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{r\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{d}f_{j}(r)+f_{j}(-r).

To conclude, we will show that the right-hand side of this inequality is zero using a proof by contradiction.

To this end, fix any
j\in[d]
and
\epsilon>0
, and suppose that
\liminf_{r\to\infty}f_{j}(r)+f_{j}(-r)\geq\epsilon
.
Then, there exists an
r^{\prime}>0
for which
\inf_{r\geq r^{\prime}}f_{j}(r)+f_{j}(r)\geq\epsilon/2
so that

\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{j}(r)+f_{j}(-r)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}r\geq\int_{r^{\prime}}^{\infty}f_{j}(r)+f_{j}(-r)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}r\geq\int_{r^{\prime}}^{\infty}\epsilon/2\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}r=\infty
.
This is a contradiction as
Fubini’s theorem
(
Rudin 1987
, Thm. 8.8)
and the assumed integrability of
v
imply that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{j}(r)+f_{j}(-r)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}r=\int|v_{j}(x)|\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x<\infty.

Therefore, we must have
\sum_{j=1}^{d}\liminf_{r\to\infty}f_{j}(r)+f_{j}(-r)=0
and hence
\left|\int(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x\right|=0
, as advertised.
∎

Theorem 3.3

(Divergence theorem on a convex set)
.

Suppose that a vector-valued function
v:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is differentiable on a convex set
\mathcal{X}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
If
\nabla\cdot{v}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=\lim_{r\to\infty}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

where, for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{X}_{r}\triangleq\mathcal{X}\cap[-r,r]^{d}
and
\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)
is the outward pointing unit normal at
x\in\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is convex, each
\mathcal{X}_{r}
is bounded and convex and therefore has finite perimeter
(
Buttazzo et al. 1995
, Lem. 2.4)
.
Since, in addition,
v
is differentiable on
\mathcal{X}
, the result follows from Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem
(
Rudin 1987
, Thm. 1.34)
and the Gauss-Green theorem of
Pfeffer 2012
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

\displaystyle=\int\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathbb{I}\left[{x\in\mathcal{X}_{r}}\right](\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{r}}(\nabla\cdot{v})(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=\lim_{r\to\infty}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}_{r}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}_{r}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x.

∎

Proposition 3.1

(Domain of Langevin operator)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, suppose that
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is differentiable.
The following claims hold true for
\mathcal{T}_{P}
as defined in
Eq.

23
.

1.

If
g\cdot\nabla\log p,\nabla\cdot g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, then
\mathcal{T}_{P}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.

2.

If
g,\mathcal{T}_{P}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, then
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
.

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from
Eq.

23
.
For the second claim,

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)=\int\frac{1}{p(x)}(\nabla\cdot(pg))(x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)=\int(\nabla\cdot(pg))(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x.

Under our assumptions, the vector field
v=pg
satisfies
v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\nabla\cdot v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that we can evoke the divergence theorem on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
Theorem

3.2
) to establish the result.
∎

Analogous Stein operators have been developed for distributions supported on smooth manifolds
(
Le et al. 2024
;
Barp et al. 2022
)
, where appropriate generalizations of the Langevin diffusion can be constructed.
This requires a manifold generalization of the divergence theorem; for a technical discussion of the mathematics involved, see
Pigola and Setti 2014
.

For our later discussion of Stein variational gradient in
Section

25
it will be useful to additionally introduce an operator that acts on scalar-valued functions
h
to produce vector-valued functions
\mathcal{A}_{P}h
, in such a manner that each component of
\mathcal{A}_{P}h
has expectation equal to zero under
P
.

Definition 3.3

(Vector-valued Langevin Stein operator;
Liu et al. 2016
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, the
vector-valued Langevin Stein operator
for
P
satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{P}h)(x)=(\nabla h)(x)+h(x)\cdot(\nabla\log p)(x)

(24)

for suitably regular scalar-valued functions
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We can derive a suitable domain for the vector-valued Langevin operator in much the same way we did for its scalar counterpart.

Proposition 3.2

(Domain of vector-valued Langevin operator)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, suppose that
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is differentiable.
The following claims hold true for
\mathcal{A}_{P}
as defined in
Eq.

24
.

1.

If
h\cdot\nabla\log p,\nabla h\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, then
\mathcal{A}_{P}h\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.

2.

If
h,\mathcal{A}_{P}h\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, then
P(\mathcal{A}_{P}h)=0
.

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from the definition
Eq.

24
.
We will deduce the second result by reducing to the case of the scalar Langevin operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
of
Definition

3.2
.
Fix any coordinate
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, and define the vector-valued function
g(x)=e_{j}h(x)
where
e_{j}
is the
j
-th standard basis vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then
(\mathcal{A}_{P}h)_{j}=\mathcal{T}_{P}g
, and
g
satisfies the preconditions of
Proposition

3.1
, hence
P((\mathcal{A}_{P}h)_{j})=P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
.
∎

11
Diffusion Operators

In the previous section we saw that the generator of a particular Markov process can be used to construct a Stein operator for a target distribution
P
provided that the Markov process is
P
-invariant.
However, there are an infinite number of Markov processes which meet this requirement!
Depending on the context for which we require a Stein discrepancy, there is no reason to suppose that the choice of the Langevin Stein operator is optimal.
Fortunately,
Gorham et al. 2019
demonstrated that it is possible to extend the construction in
Section

10
to a broad class of Markov processes known as
Itô diffusions
.

Definition 3.4

(Itô diffusion)
.

A (time-homogeneous)
Itô diffusion

with starting point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
locally Lipschitz, linear-growth
3
3

3

The locally Lipschitz and linear-growth assumptions on
b
and
\sigma
ensure that the stochastic differential equation
Eq.

25
has a unique
(
Ikeda and Watanabe 2014
, Chap. IV, Thm. 3.1)
and non-explosive
(
Oksendal 2013
, Thm. 5.2.1)
solution.

drift

b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

diffusion

\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}

coefficients

is a stochastic process
({Z}_{t,x})_{t\geq 0}
solving the Itô stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}Z_{t,x}=b(Z_{t,x})\;\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(Z_{t,x})\;\mathrm{d}W_{t}\quad\text{with}\quad Z_{0,x}=x,

(25)

where
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denotes a standard
d
-dimensional Wiener process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

To exploit Itô diffusions in a similar manner to how we exploited the Langevin diffusion, we require constraints on the drift coefficient
b
and the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
to ensure that the process is
P
-invariant.
The following result, stated in
Gorham et al. 2019
but distilled from
Ma et al. 2015
and
Pavliotis 2016
, completely characterizes the set of Itô diffusions that leave
P
invariant.
Recall that, for a matrix-valued function
M(x)
, the notation
\nabla\cdot M
refers to the vector with
j
th component
\nabla\cdot(M^{\top}e_{j})
where
e_{j}
is the
j
th basis vector of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Theorem 3.4

(Complete recipe for
P
-invariant diffusions)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, consider the Itô diffusion
Eq.

25
with drift coefficient
b\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
and diffusion coefficient
\sigma\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times m})
, and define the
covariance coefficient

a(x)\triangleq\sigma(x)\sigma(x)^{\top}
.
The diffusion is
P
-invariant if and only if

\displaystyle b(x)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{p(x)}(\nabla\cdot(pa))(x)+f(x)

(26)

for a
non-reversible component

f\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying
\nabla\cdot(pf)=0
.
In addition, if
b
satisfies
Eq.

26
with
f\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, then
4
4

4

To avoid ambiguity, let us emphasize that in what follows
p(a+c)
denotes the function
x\mapsto p(x)(a(x)+c(x))
and not “the application of
p
to
a+c
”.

\displaystyle b(x)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{p(x)}(\nabla\cdot(p(a+c)))(x)

(27)

for a differentiable
P
-integrable skew-symmetric
d\times d
matrix-valued function
c
termed the
stream coefficient

(
Conca and Vanninathan 2007
;
Landim et al. 1998
)
.
In this case, the infinitesimal generator of the diffusion takes the form

\displaystyle u\mapsto\frac{1}{2p}\nabla\cdot(p(a+c)(\nabla u))

for all suitably regular
u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
.

These observations motivated
Gorham et al. 2019
to propose the following generalization of the Langevin Stein operator:

Definition 3.5

(Diffusion Stein operator;
Gorham et al. 2019
, (8)
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, suppose
a(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is symmetric positive-semidefinite, and
c(x)
is skew-symmetric, with
x\mapsto p(x)(a(x)+c(x))
differentiable over
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then the
(a,c)
-diffusion Stein operator
for
P
satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\frac{1}{p(x)}(\nabla\cdot(p(a+c)g))(x)

(28)

for all suitably regular vector-valued functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The choice of covariance coefficient
a
and stream coefficient
c
appearing in
Eq.

28
can be application-dependent, conferring a range of different properties to Stein discrepancies based on the diffusion Stein operator.
Several important special cases are generated by

1.

the
preconditioned Langevin diffusion
, for which
c\equiv 0
and
a=\sigma\sigma^{\top}
for a constant diffusion coefficient
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}

(
Stuart et al. 2004
)
;

2.

the
Riemannian Langevin diffusion
, for which
c\equiv 0
and
a(x)=G^{-1}(x)
, where
G
is a positive-definite metric tensor
(
Patterson and Teh 2013
;
Xifara et al. 2014
;
Ma et al. 2015
)
;

3.

the
non-reversible preconditioned Langevin diffusion
, for which
c\not\equiv 0
and
a=\sigma\sigma^{\top}
for a constant
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}

(
Ma et al. 2015
;
Duncan et al. 2016
;
Rey-Bellet and Spiliopoulos 2015
)
; and

4.

the
underdamped Langevin diffusion

(
Horowitz 1987
)
, which targets the augmented distribution
P\times\mathcal{N}(0,I)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with

\displaystyle c\equiv 2\begin{pmatrix}0&-I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad a\equiv 2\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}.

Further discussion is deferred to
Section

18.2
.

The following result, a generalization of
Gorham et al. 2019
, clarifies the minimum regularity we will require for the vector-valued functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Recall that, for
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the notation
\nabla g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is understood as
[\nabla g]_{i,j}=\partial_{j}g_{i}
, while for matrices
A
and
B
, the double dot product is
A:B=\mathrm{tr}(AB^{\top})
.
In particular, for a matrix-valued function
M(x)
, the product rule writes as
\nabla\cdot(Mg)=(\nabla\cdot M)\cdot g+M:\nabla g
.

Proposition 3.3

(Domain of diffusion operator)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, let
\mathcal{T}_{P}
be the
(a,c)
-diffusion Stein operator from
Eq.

28
, let
b
be defined as in
Eq.

27
,
and let
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be differentiable.
The following claims hold true

1.

If
b\cdot g,(a+c):\nabla g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
,
then
\mathcal{T}_{P}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.

2.

If
(a+c)g,\mathcal{T}_{P}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, then
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of
Proposition

3.2
.
For the first claim, since
g
is differentiable with
b\cdot g,(a+c):\nabla g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, it follows immediately that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{P}g=2b\cdot g+(a+c):\nabla g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P).

For the second claim,

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)=\int(\nabla\cdot(p(a+c)g))(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x.

Under our assumptions, the vector field
v=p(a+c)g
satisfies
v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\nabla\cdot v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that we can again evoke
Theorem

3.2
to establish the result.

∎

Finally, we remark that since only the composition
\mathcal{T}_{P}g
of a Stein operator and a function
g
appears in Stein’s method, there is some flexibility to simultaneously modify the Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
and the functions
g
while leaving the composition
\mathcal{T}_{P}g
unchanged.
In this way one can consider the diffusion Stein operator as equivalent to applying the Langevin Stein operator (
Definition

3.2
) to functions of the form
(a+c)g
; this perspective enables the results that we present for Stein discrepancy based on the Langevin Stein operator in
Chapter

4
to be transferred to Stein discrepancy based on the diffusion Stein operator.

12
Constrained Operators

So far, we have only introduced the Langevin and diffusion Stein operators only for distributions supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but oftentimes we wish to consider distributions supported on subsets
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Unfortunately, taking a Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
designed for distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and restricting it to
\mathcal{X}
does not work; the condition
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
in
Definition

3.1
will be violated as the boundary term

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}}p(x)g(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)\;\mathrm{d}x

appearing in the divergence theorem will not vanish in general.
Recall that
\partial\mathcal{X}
denotes the boundary of the set
\mathcal{X}
, and that by definition
\partial\mathcal{X}
is empty when
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
There is then a choice for how to proceed.

One option is to restrict attention to functions
g
chosen such that the boundary term is zero by construction; this can be achieved by requiring the vanishing on the boundary condition
(
Gorham and Mackey 2015
)

\displaystyle g(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)=0\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\partial\mathcal{X}.

(29)

This construction is compatible with the classical Stein discrepancies defined in
Section

16
, with the graph Stein discrepancies defined in
Section

17
, and with the kernel Stein discrepancies defined in
Section

18
.

A second option is to employ a Stein operator adapted to
\mathcal{X}
.
The
mirror Langevin diffusion

(
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Chewi et al. 2020
)
,

\displaystyle Z_{t,x}=\nabla\psi^{*}(\eta_{t,x}),\quad\mathrm{d}\eta_{t,x}

\displaystyle=\nabla\log p(Z_{t,x})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\nabla^{2}\psi(Z_{t,x})^{1/2}\mathrm{d}B_{t}

\displaystyle\quad\text{for}\quad\eta_{0,x}

\displaystyle=\nabla\psi(x),

(30)

provides an elegant example of a
P
-invariant Markov process that can be used to construct a Stein operator in situations where the domain
\mathcal{X}
is convex and closed.
Here a
mirror map

\nabla\psi
is used to transform an input variable into a
mirror variable

\eta_{0,x}
,
the mirror variables
\eta_{t,x}
evolve according to the stochastic differential equation
Eq.

30
,
and finally
\eta_{t,x}
is mapped backed into the original constrained sample space
\mathcal{X}
via the inverse mirror map
\nabla\psi^{*}
.

To be clear, we are not restricting attention to domains
\mathcal{X}
that are bounded.
A simple example of a convex and closed set
\mathcal{X}
that is not bounded is
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\geq 0\}
.
For a closed convex set whose boundary
\partial\mathcal{X}
can be locally represented as
F(x)=0
, its outward unit normal vector satisfies

n(x)\in\left\{\pm\frac{\nabla F(x)}{\left\|{\nabla F(x)}\right\|_{2}}\right\}.

Zhang et al. 2020
highlighted that the mirror diffusion is an instance of the Riemannian Langevin diffusion with metric tensor
G=\nabla^{2}\psi
, for a twice differentiable function
\psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, which we require to have

1.

(\nabla^{2}\psi)^{-1}
Lipschitz and differentiable on
\mathcal{X}
,

2.

\left\|{(\nabla\psi)(x_{n})}\right\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty
whenever
x_{n}\rightarrow x\in\partial\mathcal{X}
, and

3.

for some
c>0
,
\nabla^{2}\psi(x)\succcurlyeq cI\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\mathcal{X}.

Assumption 3.2

(Regularity of
P
)
.

Let
P
be a probability distribution on a convex and closed set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite mean and positive differentiable density
p
on
\mathcal{X}
.

Consideration of the infinitesimal generator of mirror Langevin diffusion led
Shi et al. 2022a
to propose the following Stein operator:

Definition 3.6

(Mirrored Stein operator;
Shi et al. 2022a
)
.

Consider a convex and closed set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\psi
be a function with the properties just described.
In the setting of
3.2
, the
mirrored Stein operator
for
P
satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P,\psi}g)(x)

\displaystyle=g(x)\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi)(x)^{-1}(\nabla\log p)(x)

(31)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi)(x)^{-1}g(x)),

for suitably regular vector-valued functions
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Since the mirrored Stein operator is a special case of a diffusion Stein operator, the domain of the mirrored Stein operator can be deduced following a similar argument to
Proposition

3.3

(see also
Shi et al. 2022a
, Prop. 1)
.
The only real point of distinction here is that care must be taken to ensure that
(\nabla^{2}\psi)^{-1}
cancels any growth of
p
on the boundary of
\mathcal{X}
.

Recall that, for a matrix-valued function
M(x)
, the notation
\nabla\cdot M
refers to the vector with
j
th component
\nabla\cdot(M^{\top}e_{j})
where
e_{j}
is the
j
th basis vector of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proposition 3.4

(Domain of mirrored operator)
.

In the setting of
3.2
, suppose that
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is differentiable and
\psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is sufficiently regular for the following quantities to exist.
Then the following claims hold:

1.

If
g\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi)^{-1}(\nabla\log p)
,
\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi)^{-1}g)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
then
\mathcal{T}_{P,\psi}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.

2.

If
\mathcal{T}_{P,\psi}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
,
v\triangleq p(\nabla^{2}\psi)^{-1}g
is differentiable, and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}_{n}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x=0

(32)

where
\mathcal{X}_{n}\triangleq\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\|x\|_{\infty}\leq n\}
, then
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
.

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from
Eq.

31
.
The second follows from the divergence theorem for convex sets (
Theorem

3.3
) and
Eq.

32
since
\nabla\cdot v\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
.
∎

As an illustration, consider the probability simplex
\mathcal{X}=\{x\in[0,1]^{d}:\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\leq 1\}
, where for example we may be interested in a Dirichlet target
P
.
Taking
\psi
to be the negative entropy
\psi(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}x_{i}\log x_{i}
with
x_{d+1}\triangleq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}
, the mirror Langevin diffusion coincides with the
Wright–Fisher diffusion

(
Ethier 1976
)
.

13
Gradient-Free Operators

The previous examples of Stein operators were based on the idea that the gradient
\nabla\log p
, and certain linear transformations of this gradient, integrate to zero under
P
.
However, in some applications the calculation of gradients is associated with a prohibitive computational cost, or gradients may even fail to exist.
In such scenarios we can adapt ideas from importance sampling to leverage instead gradients of a tractable approximating distribution
\Pi
.

Assumption 3.3

(Regularity of
P
)
.

Let
P
and
\Pi
be probability distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
P
admit a positive density
p
and let
\Pi
admit a positive and differentiable density
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The following Stein operator is termed
gradient-free
because gradients with respect to the density function of the target distribution
P
are not involved:

Definition 3.7

(Gradient-free Stein operator;
Han and Liu 2018
)
.

In the setting of
3.3
, the
gradient-free Langevin Stein operator
for
P
, based on
\Pi
, satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P,\Pi}\,g)(x)=\frac{\pi(x)}{p(x)}\left[(\nabla\cdot g)(x)+g(x)\cdot(\nabla\log\pi)(x)\right]

(33)

for suitably regular vector-valued functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

If
P
and
\Pi
are equal then the gradient-free Stein operator coincides with the Langevin Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
in
Eq.

28
.
If
P
and
\Pi
are not equal then it is straightforward to see that
P(\mathcal{T}_{P,\Pi}\,g)=0
if and only if
\Pi(\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}g)=0
.
Thus an analogous argument leads to the following result:

Proposition 3.5

(Domain of gradient-free operator)
.

In the setting of
3.3
, suppose that
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is differentiable.
The following claims hold true for
\mathcal{T}_{P,\Pi}
as defined in
Eq.

33
.

1.

If
g\cdot\nabla\log\pi,\nabla\cdot g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\Pi)
, then
\mathcal{T}_{P,\Pi}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.

2.

If
g,\mathcal{T}_{P,\Pi}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
, then
P(\mathcal{T}_{P,\Pi}g)=0
.

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from
Eq.

33
.
For the second claim,

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{T}_{P,\Pi}g)(x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)=\int(\nabla\cdot(\pi g))(x)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x.

Under our assumptions, the vector field
v=\pi g
satisfies
v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\nabla\cdot v\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that we can evoke
Theorem

3.2
to establish the result.
∎

The gradient-free Stein operator was first introduced in the context of variational sampling algorithms
(
Han and Liu 2018
)
, which will be discussed in
Section

28.2
.
The operator can also be viewed as a diffusion Stein operator in the special case where
a+c=\pi/p
in
Eq.

28
.

14
Discrete Operators and Beyond

The final part of this Chapter is mainly devoted to the case where the target
P
has
discrete
support
\mathcal{X}
, meaning that
\mathcal{X}
is a countable set, such as encountered in the analysis of count data, text data, network data, and so forth.
However, we will also indicate how these ideas can be extended to more general state spaces, such as manifolds or infinite-dimensional state spaces, in
Section

14.3
.

In the discrete setting,
P
can be represented by the values
p(x)
of its probability mass function (
Example

2.2
).

Assumption 3.4

(Regularity of
P
)
.

Let
P
be a probability distribution with positive probability mass function
p
on a countable set
\mathcal{X}
.

As observed in
Henderson 1997
;
Shi et al. 2022b
, the generator method of
Barbour 1988
;
Barbour 1990
;
Gotze 1991
can also be applied to discrete state spaces.
The generator
A
of a (time-homogeneous) Markov process on a discrete state space
\mathcal{X}
(
Definition

2.15
) can be represented as a transition rate matrix
Q
with elements

\displaystyle Q_{x,y}=(Af_{y})(x),\qquad f_{y}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&x=y\\
0&x\neq y\end{array}\right.,

describing the infinitesimal rate at which the process transitions from state
x
to state
y
, with
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.
Several examples will now be presented.

14.1
Zanella Operator

The first construction we explore attempts to encode sparsity into the Stein operator, which can simplify its evaluation in the case of a large or uncountable set
\mathcal{X}
.
To this end, endow
\mathcal{X}
with an undirected graph structure, using
\mathcal{N}_{x}\subset\mathcal{X}
to denote the neighboring vertices of
x\in\mathcal{X}
and requiring
x\in\mathcal{N}_{y}
if
y\in\mathcal{N}_{x}
.
The edge set of this graph will be denoted
\mathcal{E}=\{(x,y):x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{N}_{x}\}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
.
The
Zanella process

(
Zanella 2019
)
has a transition matrix of the form

\displaystyle Q_{x,y}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\kappa\left(\frac{p(y)}{p(x)}\right)&y\in\mathcal{N}_{x},y\neq x\\
-\sum_{z\neq x}Q_{x,z}&y=x\end{array}\right.,

with a
balancing function

\kappa
, meaning a continuous function
\kappa:(0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,\infty)
with the
balancing property
that
\kappa(t)=t\kappa(1/t)
for all
t\in(0,\infty)
.
The role of the neighborhood structure is to impose sparsity into the process, while the balancing function ensures that
detailed balance
is satisfied so that the process is
P
-invariant.
The associated Stein operator was termed the
Zanella Stein operator
in
Hodgkinson et al. 2020
;
Shi et al. 2022b
:

Definition 3.8

(Zanella operator)
.

Fix a balancing function
\kappa:(0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,\infty)
.
In the setting of
3.4
, the
Zanella Stein operator
for
P
satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{N}_{x},y\neq x}\kappa\left(\frac{p(y)}{p(x)}\right)(g(y)-g(x))

for all sufficiently regular scalar-valued functions
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Compared to the Stein operators we have seen so far for continuous domains, the Zanella Stein operator depends on
p
through ratios
p(x)/p(y)
instead of through its gradient.
Since
p
appears only as a ratio, the Zanella Stein operator remains compatible with the situation where
p
is specified up to an intractable normalization constant, as discussed at the start of this Chapter.
Special cases of the Zanella Stein operator include the
minimum probability flow Stein operator
(
\kappa(t)=\sqrt{t}
;
Barp et al. 2019
) based on the minimum probability flow process of
Sohl-Dickstein et al. 2011
and the
Barker Stein operator
(
\kappa(t)=\frac{t}{t+1}
;
Hodgkinson et al. 2020
) based on the proposal of
Barker 1965
.

Proposition 3.6

(Domain of Zanella operator)
.

In the setting of
3.4
, let

x\mapsto\sum_{y\in\mathcal{N}_{x}}\kappa\left(\frac{p(y)}{p(x)}\right)g(y)

be in
\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.
Then
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
.

Proof.

The balancing property implies that
p(x)\kappa\left(\frac{p(y)}{p(x)}\right)=p(y)\kappa\left(\frac{p(x)}{p(y)}\right)
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, which are known as the
detailed balance
equations.
Using these detailed balance equations and using the assumed integrability to rearrange the absolutely continuous series,

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{y\in\mathcal{N}_{x}}p(x)\kappa\left(\frac{p(y)}{p(x)}\right)(g(y)-g(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}}\left[p(x)\kappa\left(\frac{p(y)}{p(x)}\right)g(y)-p(y)\kappa\left(\frac{p(x)}{p(y)}\right)g(x)\right]=0,

as required.
∎

14.2
Birth-Death Stein Operators

The intuition that we gained from the continuous case can also be brought to bear on the discrete case by endowing a discrete domain
\mathcal{X}
with an ordering.
For simplicity here we assume that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\cong\{0,1,\dots,m_{1}-1\}\times\cdots\times\{0,1,\dots,m_{d}-1\},

(36)

meaning that the set
\mathcal{X}
has cardinality
m_{1}\cdots m_{d}
and its elements can be mapped onto a Cartesian grid.
Then define the
increment
and
decrement
operators
\mathrm{inc}_{i}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
and
\mathrm{dec}_{i}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
, which take an input
x\in\mathcal{X}
and replace its
i
th coordinate with, respectively,
x_{i}+1
modulo
m_{i}
and
x_{i}-1
modulo
m_{i}
.
Define the
birth rates

b_{i,x}=p(\mathrm{inc}_{i}(x))/p(x)
and
death rates

d_{i,x}=1
noting that, as for the Zanella process, the dependence on the probability mass function
p
occurs only through a ratio, mitigating the need to obtain an explicit normalizing constant.
The
birth-death
process on
\mathcal{X}

(
Karlin and McGregor 1957
)
is a continuous time Markov process defined by the transition rate matrix

\displaystyle Q_{x,y}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}b_{i,x}\mathbb{I}\left[{y=\mathrm{inc}_{i}(x)}\right]+d_{i,x}\mathbb{I}\left[{y=\mathrm{dec}_{i}(x)}\right]-(b_{i,x}+d_{i,x})\mathbb{I}\left[{y=x}\right],

where increment events are termed "births" and decrement events are termed "deaths" in this context.
The generator of the birth-death process gives rise the the
birth-death Stein operator
, studied by authors including
Brown and Xia 2001
;
Holmes 2004
;
Eichelsbacher and Reinert 2008
;
Hodgkinson et al. 2020
;
Shi et al. 2022b
:

Definition 3.9

(Birth-death operator)
.

In the setting of
3.4
and
Eq.

36
, let the birth rates
b_{i,x}
and death rates
d_{i,x}
be as previously defined.
The
birth-death Stein operator
for
P
satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}b_{i,x}(g_{i}(\mathrm{inc}_{i}(x))-g_{i}(x))-d_{i,x}(g_{i}(x)-g_{i}(\mathrm{dec}_{i}(x)))

for all vector-valued functions
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

All functions on
\mathcal{X}
belong to the domain of the birth-death Stein operator due to the finiteness of the state space, as explained in the following result:

Proposition 3.7

(Domain of birth-death operator)
.

In the setting of
3.4
and
Eq.

36
, let
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
.

Proof.

From the change of variable
x\mapsto\mathrm{dec}_{i}(x)
, we obtain for each
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)b_{i,x}(g_{i}(\mathrm{inc}_{i}(x))-g_{i}(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(\mathrm{inc}_{i}(x))(g_{i}(\mathrm{inc}_{i}(x))-g_{i}(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)(g_{i}(x)-g_{i}(\mathrm{dec}_{i}(x)))

from which the result is established.
∎

The birth-death Stein operator is closely related to the
difference Stein operator
, which for
h:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\displaystyle[(\mathcal{G}_{P}h)(x)]_{i}=h(\mathrm{dec}_{i}(x))-b_{i,x}h(x),

studied in
Yang et al. 2018
.
Indeed, if we let
h_{i}(x)=g_{i}(x)-g_{i}(\mathrm{inc}_{i}(x))
then we have the relation
(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}[(\mathcal{G}_{P}h_{i})(x)]_{i}
, so that the birth-death Stein operator can be thought of as a coordinate-wise application of the difference Stein operator.
The extension to the case where
\mathcal{X}
is countably infinite is discussed in
Hodgkinson et al. 2020
;
Matsubara et al. 2024
.

14.3
Operators from Discrete Time Markov Chains

The generator method for constructing Stein operators is convenient because, informally speaking, computing quantities in the infinitesimal
t\rightarrow 0
limit does not require integrals to be computed.
Indeed, we recall from
Definition

2.14
that any
P
-invariant Markov transition kernel
K_{t}
gives rise to a Stein operator

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\int g(y)K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)-g(x),

(37)

but this construction is not practical as the integral over the state
y
of the Markov process at
t>0
units of time after initialization at
x
cannot typically be computed.
However, in the case of a discrete time Markov process and discrete domains exhibiting an appropriate sparsity structure, such integrals may amount to finite sums that can be computed.
Indeed, the one step transition probabilities of a discrete time Markov process on a discrete space
\mathcal{X}
can be characterized by a
transition matrix

K
, whose entries
K_{x,y}
represent the probability of moving to state
y
if the process is initialized in state
x
, for
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.
Then
Eq.

37
becomes what we term in this book a
Markov chain Stein operator
:

Definition 3.10

(Markov chain Stein operator)
.

In the setting of
3.4
, let
K
be the transition matrix of a discrete time Markov process for which
P
is invariant.
The associated
Markov chain Stein operator
for
P
satisfies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\left(\sum_{y\in\mathcal{X}}g(y)K_{x,y}\right)-g(x),

for all sufficiently regular scalar-valued functions
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

The Markov chain Stein operator can be efficiently computed when the number of non-zero entries in each row of the transition matrix is small.
A simple characterization of the domain of the Markov chain operator can be obtained under the assumption that the discrete time Markov process is reversible, meaning that the detailed balance equations are satisfied:

Proposition 3.8

(Domain of Markov chain operator)
.

In the setting of
3.4
, let the discrete time Markov process be
P
-invariant, meaning that
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)K_{x,y}=p(y)
for all
y\in\mathcal{X}
, and let
g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
.
Then
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
.

Proof.

From
P
-invariance,

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\sum_{y\in\mathcal{X}}g(y)K_{x,y}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)g(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{X}}g(y)p(y)-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)g(x)=0,

where rearrangement of the sum is justified by its absolute convergence under the integrability that we assumed.
∎

Consider for instance the
random scan Gibbs sampler
, which is a discrete time Markov chain on a
d
-dimensional product space
\mathcal{X}
defined by the transition matrix

\displaystyle K_{x,y}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}p(y_{i}|x_{-i})\mathbb{I}\left[{y_{-i}=x_{-i}}\right],

where
p(y_{i}|x_{-i})
denotes the probability that
X_{i}=y_{i}
given that
X_{j}=x_{j}
for all
j\neq i
under the law
X\sim P
.
These dynamics induce the
Gibbs Stein operator

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{y\in\mathcal{X}:y_{-i}=x_{-i}}q(y_{i}|x_{-i})g(y)\right)-g(x)

(38)

as studied in
Bresler and Nagaraj 2019
;
Reinert and Ross 2019
;
Shi et al. 2022b
.

14.4
General Stein Operators

The focus of our exposition has been for probability distributions
P
supported on subsets
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but for certain applications a generalization beyond finite-dimensional Euclidean space is required.
Though we do not attempt to discuss these generalizations of Stein operators in detail, we do wish to briefly mention two directions in which the Langevin Stein operator can be generalized.

The case where
P
is supported on a manifold has been considered by several authors; here one can proceed using generalizations of the
P
-invariant Langevin diffusion adapted to the manifold
(
Le et al. 2024
;
Xu and Matsuda 2021
;
Barp et al. 2022
;
Qu and Vemuri 2025
)
.
Such situations arise for example in spherical data analysis, or inference for a matrix-valued parameter subject to a nonlinear constraint (e.g. positive definiteness).

The case where random variables
X\sim P
are infinite-dimensional objects is commonly encountered in functional data analysis, where
P
is supported on an appropriate space of functions.
Simply taking
d\rightarrow\infty
in any of the constructions that we have discussed does not work, as one cannot interchange limits in general.
Instead,
Wynne et al. 2025
developed appropriate mathematical structure for an analogue of the Langevin Stein operator to be defined in the infinite-dimensional context.
The key observation in that work is that
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to a Gaussian measure; the structure of the Gaussian reference measure is used to ensure integrability in the infinite-dimensional context.

This completes our discussion of Stein operators, and sets the scene for the next Chapter where we will construct Stein discrepancies by combining a Stein operator with a Stein set.
The decision of which Stein operator to use for a particular application depends in part on the computational requirements of each Stein operator in a given applied context, but also on the properties that each Stein operator confers to the associated Stein discrepancy, and we therefore postpone further discussion of the choice of Stein operator to
Chapter

4
.

Chapter 4
Stein Discrepancies

A
Stein discrepancy
is a special type of statistical divergence
D:\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
(
Definition

2.25
), which takes the form of an
integral probability pseudo-metric

(
Müller 1997
)

\displaystyle D(P,Q)=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\;|P(f)-Q(f)|

(39)

where the test set
\mathcal{F}
is designed to avoid explicit computation of integrals
P(f)
with respect to the target distribution
P
.
This is achieved using a Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
(
Definition

3.1
) to generate test functions
f=\mathcal{T}_{P}g
that have zero mean under
P
, so that
P(f)=0
can be trivially computed.
Despite the relatively recent introduction of the concept of Stein discrepancy in
Gorham and Mackey 2015
, there are now myriad applications where Stein discrepancies are used; we defer all discussion to Chapter
6
.
The aims of the present Chapter are to formally define the concept of a Stein discrepancy, to introduce specific examples of Stein discrepancies and to explain how they can be computed, and to discuss the properties of Stein discrepancies, focusing in particular on concepts called
convergence detection
and
convergence control
.

First a rigorous definition of Stein discrepancy will be presented.
For a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
denote the set of probability measures on
\mathcal{X}
, and,
for an operator
\mathcal{T}
on a set
\mathcal{G}
, define
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{G}\triangleq\{\mathcal{T}g:g\in\mathcal{G}\}
.
The following definition is based on
Gorham and Mackey 2015
and
Barp et al. 2024

and uses the shorthand
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{+}(Q)
for the set of measurable functions
f
with
Q
-integrable positive part
(f)_{+}\triangleq\max(f,0)
.

Definition 4.1

(Stein discrepancy)
.

Let
P
be a probability distribution on a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
.
The
Stein discrepancy
with Stein set
\mathcal{G}
and Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
(
Definition

3.1
) is the map

\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,\infty]
with, for each distribution
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})\triangleq\sup_{f\,\in\,(\mathcal{T}_{P}\mathcal{G})\,\cap\,\mathcal{L}^{1}_{+}(Q)}|Q(f)|.

(40)

Comparing (
40
) to (
39
), we see that a Stein discrepancy has a test set
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{T}_{P}\mathcal{G})\cap\mathcal{L}^{1}_{+}(Q)
that explicitly depends on both the target distribution
P
and the candidate distribution
Q
being assessed.
By design, the Stein operator and Stein set ensure that every test function
f\in\mathcal{T}_{P}\mathcal{G}
is
P
-integrable with
P(f)=0
.
However, not every
f\in\mathcal{T}_{P}\mathcal{G}
need be integrable under
Q
, so the additional requirement
f\in\mathcal{L}^{1}_{+}(Q)
ensures that the integral
Q(f)
is well defined.

The specification of the Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
and Stein set
\mathcal{G}
appearing in
Definition

4.1
impacts both the mathematical properties of the Stein discrepancy and the ease with which the Stein discrepancy can be computed.
The remainder of this Chapter is devoted to a discussion of this issue, and several specific examples of Stein discrepancies will be presented and discussed in detail.

15
Fisher Divergence

Here we open our discussion with the Fisher divergence, a well-known statistical divergence which we will see is in fact a special instance of the Stein discrepancy formalism that we just introduced.

Definition 4.2

(Fisher divergence)
.

Let
P
and
Q
be (Borel) distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
admitting densities
p
and
q
such that
\nabla\log p,\nabla\log q\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(Q)
.
Then the
Fisher divergence
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{FD}(Q||P)\triangleq\int\|\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q\|_{2}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}Q.

This divergence can be computed without knowledge of the normalizing constant of
p
and, furthermore, expectations with respect to
P
are not required.
This makes it a popular choice for estimating parameters of intractable statistical models, where the methodology is called
score matching
.
Indeed, suppose that
P
involves parameters
\theta
, denoted
P_{\theta}
, with corresponding density
p_{\theta}
.
Score matching refers to selecting parameter values
\theta
that minimize
\mathrm{FD}(Q||P_{\theta})
.
Assuming that
\Delta\log p_{\theta}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(Q)
,
Hyvärinen 2005
showed using integration by parts that

\displaystyle\mathrm{FD}(Q||P_{\theta})=[\text{constant in $\theta$}]+Q(2\Delta\log p_{\theta}+\|\nabla\log p_{\theta}\|^{2}).

(41)

It is straightforward to obtain consistent Monte Carlo approximations of the integral appearing in (
41
) if samples from
Q
can be obtained.
This makes score matching a widely-applicable technique for statistical estimation that benefits from many of the desirable characteristics that we introduced as motivation in
Chapter

1
.
Further, several generalizations of Fisher divergence are possible, for instance to discrete domains
(
Matsubara et al. 2024
)
.

However, there are two main limitations of Fisher divergence which motivate the subsequent discussion of other Stein discrepancies in this book.
First, a practical drawback of the Fisher divergence relative to most of the Stein discrepancies considered in this book, is that second order derivatives of
p
(or
p_{\theta}
) are required to use (
41
).
For complex data-generating distributions
P
. such as those based on solving systems of physical governing equations, it might be possible to obtain first order derivatives at additional computational expense, but obtaining second order derivatives is almost always prohibitively difficult.
A second potential drawback, depending on the applied context, is that Fisher divergence is stronger than most other statistical divergences, including Kullback–Leibler (
Definition

2.26
) and the kernel Stein discrepancies that we will meet in
Section

18
; for further discussion of this point see respectively
Ley and Swan 2013
and
Liu and Wang 2016
.
The most obvious drawback with using a strong notion of divergence is that it may be infinite when comparing two distributions of genuine interest, limiting its usefulness only to a small subset of probability distributions whose regularity is sufficient to be quantitatively compared.
Nevertheless, we open our discussion of Stein discrepancies by demonstrating that the Fisher divergence can be recovered as an instance of the general Stein discrepancy framework, albeit with a
Q
-dependent Stein set:

Proposition 4.1

(Fisher divergence as Stein discrepancy)
.

Let
P
and
Q
be (Borel) distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
admitting densities
p
and
q
such that
\nabla\log p,\nabla\log q\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(Q)
and
\Delta\log p,\Delta\log q\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(Q)
.
Then
\mathrm{FD}(Q||P)=\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{Q})
where the Stein discrepancy is based on the Langevin Stein operator
(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=(\nabla\cdot g)(x)+g(x)\cdot(\nabla\log p)(x)
from
Equation

23
, and the Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{Q}=\{g:\sum_{j=1}^{d}|g_{j}|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(Q)}^{2}\leq 1,\;\mathcal{T}_{P}g,\mathcal{T}_{Q}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(Q)\}
.

Proof.

For the Langevin Stein operator and
g\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)

\displaystyle=Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)-Q(\mathcal{T}_{Q}g)

\displaystyle=Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}g-\mathcal{T}_{Q}g)=Q(g\cdot(\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q)),

where the first equality follows since
g
is in the domain of
\mathcal{T}_{Q}
from
Proposition

3.1
.
Then, from the duality structure of
\mathcal{L}^{2}(P)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{FD}(Q||P)

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int g_{i}(\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q)_{i}\;\mathrm{d}Q\;:\;\sum_{i=1}^{d}|g_{i}|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(Q)}^{2}\leq 1\right\}

where the supremum is attained at
g^{\star}\propto\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q
, since our assumptions ensure that
g^{\star}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(Q)
.
Our assumptions further imply that
g^{\star}\in\mathcal{G}
and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{FD}(Q||P)

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int g_{i}(\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q)_{i}\;\mathrm{d}Q\;:\;g\in\mathcal{G}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{G})}Q(f)

which is identical to the Stein discrepancy (
40
) since by construction
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{G})\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(Q)
, and the modulus in (
40
) can be dropped since
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{G})
is a symmetric set.
∎

The asymmetric nature of the Stein discrepancy, where
P
is fixed and
Q
is varied, suggests using a slightly finer notion than that of a statistical divergence, called
separation
.
It is stated below for any
discrepancy
with respect to
P
, meaning a map
D(\cdot||P):\mathcal{P}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
for which
D(Q||P)
measures the dissimilarity of
Q
as an approximation to
P
.

Definition 4.3

(Separation)
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P}
.
A discrepancy
D(\cdot||P)
is said to
separate
P
from
\mathcal{Q}
if
D(Q||P)=0
implies that
P=Q
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Note that any valid statistical divergence (c.f.
Definition

2.25
) separates
P
from
\mathcal{P}
, for all choices of
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
The Fisher divergence, despite the name, is not really a statistical divergence since it is not well-defined when the density
p
does not exist.

The following result illustrates separation in the context of the Fisher divergence; the statement is not as general as possible – we will present more general results later in this Chapter – but we choose to present it here because it admits a simple and instructive proof:

Proposition 4.2

(A separation result for Fisher divergence)
.

Let
P
and be a (Borel) distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
admitting a density
p
with
\nabla\log p\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(P)
.
Then the Fisher divergence separates
P
from the set
\mathcal{Q}
, consisting of (Borel) distributions
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that admit a density
q
for which
\nabla\log p,\nabla\log q\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(Q)
.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{FD}(Q||P)=0
then, since
q>0
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{FD}(Q||P)=\int q(x)\left\|\nabla\log\frac{p}{q}\right\|_{2}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x,

it follows that
\nabla\log(p/q)=0
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This implies
p/q
is almost everywhere constant, and since both
p
and
q
must integrate to one, we deduce that
P
and
Q
are equal.
∎

16
Classical Stein Discrepancies

Stein’s method is a theoretical device for which there is a natural incentive to consider Stein sets
\mathcal{G}
that are small, and thus easier to handle theoretically, compared for example to the Stein set used to construct Fisher divergence in
Section

15
.
For a target distribution
P
supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

classical Stein discrepancies

(
Gorham and Mackey 2015
;
Gorham et al. 2019
)

employ a Stein set of bounded functions with bounded Lipschitz derivatives.
To make this definition explicit some notation is required.
First introduce the following notation that will be used throughout the remainder of this book.
For a norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, recall that
\|\cdot\|^{*}
denotes the dual norm from
Chapter

1
.

Definition 4.4

(Classical Stein set;
Gorham and Mackey 2015
)
.

Given a norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the
classical Stein set

\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|}
as the set of functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which

\displaystyle\sup_{x\neq y}\quad\max\left(\left\|{g(x)}\right\|^{*},\left\|{\nabla g(x)}\right\|^{*},\frac{\left\|{\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y)}\right\|^{*}}{\left\|{x-y}\right\|}\right)\leq 1.

The construction can be extended to domains
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
as described in
Section

12
and
Gorham and Mackey 2015
, but our focus here is on the case
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
These classical Stein sets are commonly paired with the diffusion Stein operators of
Section

11
.

Definition 4.5

(Classical diffusion Stein discrepancy;
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, a
classical diffusion Stein discrepancy

\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})
combines a diffusion Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
for
P
(
Definition

3.5
) and the classical Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|}
(
Definition

4.4
).

As anticipated at the start of this Chapter, the usefulness of a Stein discrepancy depends on three factors:
convergence detection
,
convergence control
, and
computability
. The first two factors, convergence detection and convergence control, refer to the set of sequences
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for which
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})\rightarrow 0
in the large
n
limit.
This set automatically includes the constant sequence with
Q_{n}=P
whenever the Stein discrepancy is well-defined, but we would typically hope this property holds for sequences
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that converge to
P
in a sense that is reasonably standard.
From a mathematical perspective, convergence detection and convergence control refer, respectively, to upper-bounding and lower-bounding the Stein discrepancy in terms of another statistical divergence, typically one whose mathematical properties are well-understood.
The first result of this kind that we present, due to
Gorham et al. 2019
, shows that classical diffusion Stein discrepancies
detect
convergence in
s
-Wasserstein metric, provided that the diffusion coefficients are Lipschitz.
Recall that the
s
-Wasserstein metric
\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{s}
was introduced in
Definition

2.28
.
Given a function
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
M_{0}(g)\triangleq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|g(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
and

M_{k}(g)\triangleq\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{\|(\nabla^{k-1}g)(x)-(\nabla^{k-1}g)(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\|x-y\|_{2}}

for
k\in\{1,2\}
.
The upper bounds presented in
Proposition

4.3
and
Theorem

4.1
feature constants that are expressed in terms of e.g.
M_{1}(\cdot)
; it is implicit that these quantities are assumed to be finite, otherwise the bound becomes trivial.

Proposition 4.3

(Convergence detection; Prop. 8 of
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, consider a classical diffusion Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})
where
\|\cdot\|\geq\|\cdot\|_{2}
.
Let
a
denote the covariance coefficient,
b
denote the drift, and
c
denote the stream coefficient.
If
a,b,c\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
and
p(a+c)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, then, with
m\triangleq a+c
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{s}(Q,P)(2M_{1}(b)+M_{1}(m))

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{s}(Q,P)^{t}\,2^{1-t}\,\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}\left[{(2\left\|{b({Z})}\right\|+\left\|{m({Z})}\right\|)^{\frac{s}{s-t}}}\right]^{\frac{s-t}{s}}

for any
s\geq 1
and
t\in(0,1]
.
Moreover, for
\mu_{0}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}[e^{2\left\|{b({Z})}\right\|+\left\|{m({Z})}\right\|}]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{1}(Q,P)(2M_{1}(b)+M_{1}(m))

\displaystyle\qquad+\min(\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{1}(Q,P),2)\log\left(\frac{e\mu_{0}}{\min(\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{1}(Q,P),2)}\right).

Thus if a sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
P
in the sense of
s
-Wasserstein, meaning that
\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{s}(Q_{n},P)\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, it follows that this convergence is
detected
by the classical diffusion Stein discrepancy, in the sense that
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})\rightarrow 0
in the same limit.

The second factor determining the usefulness of a Stein discrepancy is
convergence control
, which refers to the construction of a Stein discrepancy lower bound.
The following theorem of
Gorham et al. 2019
shows that classical diffusion Stein discrepancies based on fast mixing diffusions
control
convergence in
1
-Wasserstein metric.
To state this result, we adopt the same notation introduced in
Section

7.4
and consider a collection of Markov processes
(X_{t}^{x})_{t\geq 0}
, each corresponding to the solution of the Itô diffusion on which the diffusion Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
is based (
Definition

3.4
), but with initial distributions
P_{0}=\delta_{x}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
P_{t}^{x}
denote the probability measure associated with
X_{t}^{x}
.
In addition, if
r:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a non-increasing integrable function such that
\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{1}(P_{t}^{x},P_{t}^{y})\leq r(t)\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{1}(\delta_{x},\delta_{y})
holds for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we say that
r
is a
Wasserstein decay rate
associated with
\mathcal{T}_{P}
.
Further, for a matrix-valued function
g
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

F_{k}(g)\triangleq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\|v_{1}\|_{2}=1,\dots,\|v_{k}\|_{2}=1}\|(\nabla^{k}g)(x)[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]\|_{F}

where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the Frobenius norm, and let

M_{1}^{*}(g)\triangleq\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{\|g(x)-g(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{*}}{\|x-y\|_{2}}.

Theorem 4.1

(Convergence control; Thms. 6 & 7 of
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, consider a classical diffusion Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})
where
\|\cdot\|\geq\|\cdot\|_{2}
.
Let
a
denote the covariance coefficient,
b
denote the drift,
c
denote the stream coefficient, and
\sigma
denote the diffusion coefficient.
Further assume that the Itô diffusion corresponding to
\mathcal{T}_{P}
has Wasserstein decay rate
r
, and
s_{r}\triangleq\int_{0}^{\infty}r(t)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}t
.

•

If
m\triangleq a+c
is constant, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}^{1}(Q,P)

\displaystyle\leq\ 3s_{r}\max\Big(\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|}),

(42)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt\textstyle\sqrt[3]{\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})\sqrt{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|{G}\right\|_{2}}\right]^{2}(2M_{1}(b)+\frac{1}{s_{r}})^{2}}\Big),

where

G\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a standard normal vector.

•

If
\sigma
is Lipschitz and
b
and
\sigma
have locally Lipschitz second derivatives, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}^{1}(Q,P)\leq\beta_{1}\max\left(\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|}),\sqrt{\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|{G}\right\|_{2}}\right]}\right),

for
\beta_{1}
a constant depending on

M_{0}(\sigma^{-1}),M_{1}(\sigma),F_{2}(\sigma),M_{1}(b),M_{2}(b),s_{r},

and
M_{1}^{*}(m)
.

•

If
\sigma
is Lipschitz and
\nabla^{3}b
and
\nabla^{3}\sigma
are locally Lipschitz, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}^{1}(Q,P)\leq\,

\displaystyle\beta_{2}\,\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})\sqrt{d}\max\left(1,\log\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})}\right)\right)

for a constant
\beta_{2}>0
depending on
\beta_{1},M_{3}(\sigma),M_{3}(b),
and
r
.

Thus if a sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
P
according to the classical diffusion Stein discrepancy, in the sense that
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, it follows that the sequences converges in the
1
-Wasserstein metric, in the sense that
\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}^{1}(Q_{n},P)\rightarrow 0
in the same limit. See also
Mackey and Gorham 2016
and
Gorham and Mackey 2015
for analogous convergence control results under alternative preconditions.

The final factor determining the usefulness of a Stein discrepancy is the ease with which it can be computed.
If the candidate distribution
Q
is supported on a finite set of size
n
in dimension
d=1
, then
Gorham and Mackey 2015
and
Gorham et al. 2019
showed that the classical diffusion Stein discrepancy can be computed exactly as the solution of a convex quadratically-constrained quadratic program with linear objective,
O(n)
variables, and
O(n)
constraints.
However, an efficient procedure for computing the classical diffusion Stein discrepancy is unknown for larger
d
.
This limitation stems from the origin of the classical Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|}
as a theoretical rather than a computational tool.
Fortunately this computational challenge can be side-stepped if we consider alternatives for the Stein set, as we will discuss next.

17
Graph Stein Discrepancies

To enable generally computable Stein discrepancies,
Gorham and Mackey 2015
introduced
graph Stein sets
which impose boundedness and smoothness constraints only at pairs of points belonging to the support of the approximating distribution
Q
.

Definition 4.6

(Graph Stein set;
Gorham and Mackey 2015
)
.

For a probability distribution
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with discrete support and a graph
G=(V,E)
with
V=\mathrm{supp}({Q})
, we define the
graph Stein set
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\|\cdot\|,Q,G}\triangleq\bigg\{g:

\displaystyle\max\left(\left\|{g(v)}\right\|^{*},\left\|{\nabla g(v)}\right\|^{*},{\textstyle\frac{\left\|{g(x)-g(y)}\right\|^{*}}{\left\|{x-y}\right\|}},{\textstyle\frac{\left\|{\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y)}\right\|^{*}}{\left\|{x-y}\right\|}}\right)\leq 1,

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\left\|{g(x)-g(y)-{\nabla g(x)}{(x-y)}}\right\|^{*}}{\frac{1}{2}\left\|{x-y}\right\|^{2}}\leq 1,\textstyle\frac{\left\|{g(x)-g(y)-{\nabla g(y)}{(x-y)}}\right\|^{*}}{\frac{1}{2}\left\|{x-y}\right\|^{2}}\leq 1,

\displaystyle\;\forall(x,y)\in E,v\in V\bigg\}.

As such, the graph Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{\|\cdot\|,Q,G}
is a superset of the classical Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|}
(
Definition

4.4
).
Note that, unlike the classical Stein set, the graph Stein set is
Q
-dependent and also requires the edge set
E
of the graph
G
to be specified.
It turns out that this dependence can be theoretically justified provided that an appropriate graph
G
is used to construct the graph Stein set, as we will see in
Proposition

4.5
of the sequel.

Gorham and Mackey 2015
;
Gorham et al. 2019
paired these graph Stein sets with diffusion Stein operators (
Definition

3.5
) to obtain what is called a
graph diffusion Stein discrepancy
:

Definition 4.7

(Graph diffusion Stein discrepancy;
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, a
graph diffusion Stein discrepancy

Q\mapsto\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\|\cdot\|,Q,G})
combines a diffusion Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
for
P
(
Definition

3.5
) and the graph Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{\|\cdot\|,Q,G}
(
Definition

4.6
).

Remarkably, when
Q
has finite support
\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
and the norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
is
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{1}
, so that the dual norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|^{*}
is
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{\infty}
, a graph diffusion Stein discrepancy can be computed by solving
d
independent linear programs in parallel using efficient off-the-shelf solvers, as captured in the following result:

Proposition 4.4

(Computing graph diffusion Stein discrepancy; Sec. 4.2 of
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

Suppose
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{n}q(x_{i})\delta_{x_{i}}
for
x_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
q
a probability mass function on
V=\mathrm{supp}({Q})
.
For any edge set
E
, the graph diffusion Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}({Q},{\mathcal{T}{}},{\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{1},Q,{G}}})
with graph
G=(V,E)
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sup_{\psi_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\Psi_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}}\;\bigg\{

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}q(x_{i})\bigg(2b_{j}(x_{i})\psi_{ji}+\sum_{k=1}^{d}m_{jk}(x_{i})\Psi_{jki}\bigg)

(43)

\displaystyle\;\text{s.t. }\left\|{\psi_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\;\left\|{\Psi_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1,

\displaystyle\;\text{and for all }i\neq l,(x_{i},x_{l})\in E,

\displaystyle\;\max\bigg(\textstyle\frac{|\psi_{ji}-\psi_{jl}|}{\left\|{x_{i}-x_{l}}\right\|_{1}},\textstyle\frac{\left\|{\Psi_{j}(e_{i}-e_{k})}\right\|_{\infty}}{\left\|{x_{i}-x_{l}}\right\|_{1}},\textstyle\frac{|\psi_{ji}-\psi_{jl}-\langle{\Psi_{j}e_{i}},{x_{i}-x_{l}}\rangle|}{\frac{1}{2}\left\|{x_{i}-x_{l}}\right\|_{1}^{2}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\textstyle\frac{|\psi_{ji}-\psi_{jl}-\langle{\Psi_{j}e_{i}},{x_{l}-x_{i}}\rangle|}{\frac{1}{2}\left\|{x_{i}-x_{l}}\right\|_{1}^{2}}\bigg)\leq 1\bigg\},

where
\psi_{ji}
and
\Psi_{jki}
represent the values
g_{j}(x_{i})
and
\nabla_{k}g_{j}(x_{i})
respectively from
Definition

4.6
.

The number of constraints in each linear program is determined by the number of edges
|E|
in the graph, and roughly speaking a larger number of constraints renders the numerical solution of the linear program more difficult.
On the other hand, if the edge set
E
is too small then the graph Stein set may be too large to detect convergence.
Consideration of the convergence detection and control properties of the graph diffusion Stein discrepancy can provide insight into how the edge set
E
should be selected.

To analyze the effect of the choice of edge set
E
on convergence detection and control, some additional terminology is required.
A graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex set
V
embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is termed a
t
-spanner

(
Chew 1986
;
Peleg and Schäffer 1989
)
if, when each edge
(x,y)\in E
is assigned a weight
\left\|{x-y}\right\|
equal to its length in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then for all
x,y\in V
with
x\neq y
there exists a path between
x
and
y
with total path weight no greater than
t\left\|{x-y}\right\|
.
To gain intuition, in the case
d=1
a
t
-spanner can be obtained by first sorting the vertices into ascending order, at a cost
O(n\log n)
, and then joining adjacent vertices, requiring
n-1
edges in total.
Provided that the graph
G
is a
t
-spanner, the graph diffusion Stein discrepancy inherits the convergence detection and control properties of the corresponding classical diffusion Stein discrepancy, and in fact the two are equivalent:

Proposition 4.5

(Equivalence of classical and graph diffusion Stein discrepancies; Prop. 13 of
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

If
G=(\mathrm{supp}({Q}),E)
is a
t
-spanner for
t\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\|\cdot\|})\leq\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\|\cdot\|,Q,G})\leq\kappa_{d}t^{2}\,\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\|\cdot\|})

where
\kappa_{d}
is independent of
(Q,P,\mathcal{T}_{P},G)
and depends only on
d
and
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
.

Thus from the perspective of retaining the desirable convergence detection and control properties of the classical diffusion Stein discrepancy, we ought to pick
G
to be a
t
-spanner for some
t\geq 1
.
What is the associated computational cost?
If we consider the norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|=\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{p}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one can construct a
2
-spanner with
O(\kappa_{d}^{\prime}n)
edges in
O(\kappa_{d}^{\prime}n\log(n))
expected time, where
\kappa_{d}^{\prime}
is a
d
- and
p
-dependent constant
(
Har-Peled and Mendel 2005
)
.
Once the
t
-spanner is constructed, the graph diffusion Stein discrepancy can be computed by solving a finite-dimensional convex optimization problem with a linear objective,
O(n)
variables, and
O(\kappa_{d}^{\prime}n)
convex constraints, using
Proposition

4.4
.
Finally, we note that graph Stein discrepancies can also be used to enforce boundary constraints on constrained domains
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
as described in
Gorham and Mackey 2015
.

18
Kernel Stein Discrepancies

Although graph Stein discrepancies overcome the algorithmic complexity associated with classical Stein discrepancies, their recourse to linear programming renders their practical implementation non-trivial.
This section discusses an alternative class of Stein discrepancies, called
kernel Stein discrepancies
, for which computation is more straightforward but analysis of convergence detection and control is more involved.
First we present a definition in which the Stein operator and the kernel can be general, before exploring the consequences of this definition for specific Stein operators and kernels in detail.

Definition 4.8

(Kernel Stein discrepancy)
.

Let
P
be a probability distribution on a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
and let
\mathcal{T}_{P}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
be a Stein operator for
P
whose domain
\mathcal{D}
contains functions of the form
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a (matrix-valued, if
d>1
) reproducing kernel for which
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{D}
.
A
kernel Stein discrepancy

\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q)\triangleq\mathcal{S}(Q,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{K})

combines a Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
for
P
and a Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{K}\triangleq\{g:\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\leq 1\}
, the unit ball in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
.

Perhaps the most important property of kernel Stein discrepancys is that they can be computed in closed form for distributions
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
with finite support.
Indeed, for such
Q
we automatically have
\mathcal{T}_{P}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(Q)
and thus from
Definition

4.1
the kernel Stein discrepancy can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q)=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{G}_{K})}|Q(f)|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\leq 1}Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=\sup_{\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\leq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x_{i})

(44)

where we have removed the modulus using the fact that
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{G}_{K})
is a symmetric set (which follows from the fact that
\mathcal{G}_{K}
is a symmetric set).
The subsequent steps require some regularity to hold; here we sketch the argument, before stating precise results in
Section

18.1
.
Consider the linear functional
L:\mathcal{H}_{K}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle L(g)\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x_{i})\quad\text{for each}\quad g\in\mathcal{H}_{K}.

Whenever
L
is continuous, we can write
L(g)=\langle g,\ell\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}
for some Riesz representer
\ell\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
by
Theorem

2.2
.
Using this Riesz representation, the supremum in (
44
) is seen to be achieved when
g=\ell/\|\ell\|_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}
, and the value of the supremum itself is
\|\ell\|_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}
.
To explicitly determine
\ell
, we can fix
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and use the reproducing property from
\mathcal{H}_{K}
(
Theorem

2.5
) together with the notation
K_{x}=K(\cdot,x)
, to see that

\displaystyle\langle\ell(x),u\rangle=\langle\ell,K_{x}u\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}=L(K_{x}u)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{P}K_{x}u)(x_{i}).

Since this holds for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

\displaystyle\ell(\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}K_{\cdot})(x_{i})

where
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}
indicates that the Stein operator acts on the rows on
K_{\cdot}
, so that
(\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}K_{\cdot})(x_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Assuming that
\mathcal{T}_{P}
is a first order differential operator, we can use the
differential reproducing property

(
Barp et al. 2024
, Lem. 4)
to obtain

\displaystyle\|\ell\|_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{P}K_{\cdot})(x_{i}),\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}(\mathcal{T}_{P}K_{\cdot})(x_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}\underbrace{\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(2)}K(x_{i},x_{j})}_{(\star)},

where
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(2)}
acts on the columns of
K
.
The expression
(\star)
itself defines a kernel

\displaystyle k_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)\triangleq\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(2)}K(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

(45)

and we call this the
Stein kernel
associated to the Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
and the
base kernel

K
.
Putting this all together, we have shown that

\displaystyle\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j})},

which is an
explicit
formula, in contrast to the situation for graph Stein discrepancies where linear programming tools were required.
The availability of an explicit formula for the Stein discrepancy underpins many of the algorithms discussed in
Chapter

6
.
The next section makes the above argument rigorous.

18.1
Langevin KSD in Detail

This section sets the scene for describing some precise results for the Langevin Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
(
Definition

3.2
).
As such, we will be in the setting of
3.1
, where
P
admits a positive and differentiable density
p
on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for which this Stein operator is well-defined.
The first result we present makes a rigorous claim about when kernel Stein discrepancys can be explicitly computed.

Theorem 4.2

(Langevin KSD as MMD; Thm. 1 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, let
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a matrix-valued kernel for which the elements of
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K})\triangleq\{\mathcal{T}_{P}f:f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}\}
are well-defined.
Then
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
can be endowed with the structure of an reproducing kernel Hilbert space, whose reproducing kernel is

\displaystyle k_{p}(x,y)\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{p(x)p(y)}\partial_{y_{j}}\partial_{x_{i}}\left(p(x)K_{i,j}(x,y)p(y)\right).

(46)

Moreover, if
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K})\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
and
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K}))=0
, meaning that
\mathcal{T}_{P}g\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
and
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=\{0\}
for all
g\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
, then the kernel Stein discrepancy coincides with maximum mean discrepancy based on the
Stein kernel

k_{p}
; i.e.
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q)=\mathrm{MMD}_{k_{p}}(P,Q)
for all
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

The computability of maximum mean discrepancy for distributions
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
with finite support was discussed in
Section

9.3
, and following a similar argument we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j})},

demonstrating a computational role of the Stein kernel.
This formalizes the sketch argument of
Section

18
in the case of the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy.

Our attention now turns to the conditions
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K})\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
and
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K}))=0
appearing in
Theorem

4.2
.
For convenience we let
\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
denote the set of distributions
Q
on
\mathcal{X}
for which
\mathcal{T}_{Q}(\mathcal{H}_{K})\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(Q)
and
Q(\mathcal{T}_{Q}(\mathcal{H}_{K}))=0
, so that the conditions of
Theorem

4.2
can be compactly expressed as
P\in\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
.
From
Proposition

2.2
, we have that
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K})\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
if and only if
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
if and only if
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
, and from
Proposition

2.3
each of these is implied if

\displaystyle\int\sqrt{k_{p}(x,x)}\mathrm{d}P(x)<\infty.

(47)

Using the explicit for of the Stein kernel in (
46
), one can deduce that (
47
) holds whenever the elements of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
are each bounded with bounded partial derivatives, and
\int\|\nabla\log p\|\mathrm{d}P<\infty
.
Finally,
Proposition

3.1
provides regularity conditions under which
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K}))=\{0\}
.
These arguments are collected together in
Proposition

4.6
.

Proposition 4.6

(Stein embeddability conditions; Prop. 3 and Rem. 5 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

In the setting of
3.1
, let
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a matrix-valued kernel for which
\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
is well-defined.
Then:

(a)

\mathcal{T}_{P}(\mathcal{H}_{K})\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)\Leftrightarrow P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
.

(b)

If
\int\sqrt{k_{p}(x,x)}\mathrm{d}P(x)<\infty
, then
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
.

(c)

If
\int\|\nabla\log p\|\mathrm{d}P<\infty
and all
v
in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
are bounded with bounded partial derivatives, then
\int\sqrt{k_{p}(x,x)}\mathrm{d}P(x)<\infty
.

(d)

If
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
, then

\iint k_{p}(x,y)\mathrm{d}P(x)\mathrm{d}P(y)=0\Leftrightarrow P(\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}})=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow P\in\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
.

(e)

If
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
P\in\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
.

(f)

If
x\mapsto\|K(x,x)\|
is bounded, then all
v
in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
are bounded.

(g)

If
(x,y)\mapsto\|\partial_{x^{i}}\partial_{y^{i}}K(x,y)\|
exists and is bounded,
then all
v
in
\mathcal{H}_{K}
have bounded
x^{i}
-partial derivatives.

(h)

If
K\in C^{(1,1)}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

In particular, the condition
P\in\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
required for
Theorem

4.2
is met when
\int\|\nabla\log p\|\mathrm{d}P<\infty
and
K\in C_{b}^{(1,1)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Finally, we end this section by remarking on a more explicit formula for the Stein kernel in a special case of interest:

Remark 4.1

(Scalar kernel Langevin KSD)
.

In the case where
K(x,y)=k(x,y)I
for a scalar kernel
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle k_{p}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{x}\cdot\nabla_{y}k(x,y)+(\nabla\log p)(x)\cdot\nabla_{y}k(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad+(\nabla\log p)(y)\cdot\nabla_{x}k(x,y)+(\nabla\log p)(x)\cdot(\nabla\log p)(y)k(x,y),

where e.g.
\nabla_{x}k(x,y)
denotes the gradient with respect to the
x
argument of
k(x,y)
.

18.2
Separating Distributions with Langevin KSD

The focus of this section is on sufficient conditions for the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy to separate
P
from other distributions
\mathcal{Q}
; recall from
Definition

4.3
that this means
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q)=0
if and only if
Q
and
P
are equal, for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Three main separation result will be presented.
The first result, which we call
score-based separation
(
Theorem

4.3
), requires a weak notion of characteristicness of the kernel
K
, but requires also some smoothness of
K
and in limits the set of distributions
Q
that can be separated from
P
.
The second result, which we call
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-based separation
(
Theorem

4.5
), does not require smoothness of
K
and applies to certain distributions
Q
that are excluded by the first result, but requires that
Q
admits a density on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The third result is perhaps most useful, requiring only a suitably smooth, translation-invariant kernel, and guaranteeing separation of
P
from
all
alternatives
Q\in\mathcal{P}
(
Theorem

4.7
).

Definition 4.9

(
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, embedding, and a
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic kernel; Defs. 6 & 8 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Let
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the set of continuous linear functionals on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We say that
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

embeds into

\mathcal{H}_{K}
if, for each
D\in{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists
\phi_{D}\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
such that
D(h)=\langle\phi_{D},h\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
Further, if the embedding
D\mapsto\phi_{D}
is injective then we say that
K
is a
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic kernel.

This extends the notion of a characteristic kernel from
Definition

2.30
to more general linear operators than just integration with respect to probability distributions.

Simon-Gabriel and Schölkopf 2018
showed that any
C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universal scalar-valued kernel
k
and any
