C^{(1,1)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
translation-invariant
k
with fully supported spectral
measure is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic in dimension
d=1
.
These results cover all of the translation-invariant
base kernels commonly used with kernel Stein discrepancys including Gaussian, inverse multiquadric and Matérn.
A set of tools for constructing
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic matrix-valued kernels
K
is provided next:

Proposition 4.7

(
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic conditions; Prop. 4 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Suppose a matrix-valued kernel
K
with
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic. Then the following claims hold true.

(a)

If
a\in C_{b}^{1}
is strictly positive, then
a(x)K(x,y)a(y)
is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic.

(b)

If
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a Lipschitz
C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-diffeomorphism, then the composition kernel
K(b(x),b(y))

is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic.

(c)

If
k_{j}
is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}
-characteristic for
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, then
\mathrm{diag}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})

is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic.

Now we are ready to present the first main separation result; score-based separation.
From
Proposition

2.2
, we have that
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
if and only if
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
; here we naturally extend the notation so that, given
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we let
\mathcal{P}_{f}
be the set of measures
Q
on
\mathcal{X}
for which
x\mapsto\|f(x)\|
is an element of
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Q)
.
Further, for a density
p
we use
s_{p}\triangleq\nabla\log p
as a shorthand for the Stein score.

Theorem 4.3

(Score-Based Separation with Langevin KSD; Thm. 3 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Suppose a matrix-valued kernel
K
with
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic.
If
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{K,0}}
,
then the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy separates
P
from
\mathcal{P}_{s_{p}}
.

That is, if
K
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

4.3
and
\int\|\nabla\log p\|\;\mathrm{d}Q<\infty
, then the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy satisfies
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q)=0
if and only if
P
and
Q
are equal.

Next we turn attention to the second main separation result;
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-based separation.
For this the following, somewhat restrictive assumption on the kernel
K
is required:

Definition 4.10

(
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-Integrally Strictly Positive Definite; Def. 3.1 of
Liu et al. 2016
)
.

We say that a matrix-valued kernel
K
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-integrally strictly positive definite
(ISPD) if

\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\displaystyle\iint g(x)^{\top}K(x,y)g(y)\;\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y>0

for all
g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
|g|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=(\int g(x)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x)^{1/2}>0
.

For continuous translation-invariant kernels, we can use Bochner’s theorem (
Theorem

2.4
) to deduce conditions under which
K
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-ISPD:

Theorem 4.4

(
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-ISPD conditions; Thm. 4 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

The following claims hold true for a matrix-valued kernel
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

(a)

Suppose
(k_{j})_{j=1}^{d}
are continuous translation-invariant kernels on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathcal{H}_{k_{j}}\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
If the spectral measure of each
k_{j}
is fully supported, then
K=\mathrm{diag}(k_{j})
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-ISPD.

(b)

If
K
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-ISPD and
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is bounded measurable with
A(x)
invertible for each
x
, then the tilted kernel
A(x)K(x,y)A(y)^{\top}
is also
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-ISPD.

(c)

If
\mathcal{H}_{K}
is separable,
\sup_{x}|K_{x}u|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty
, and
K_{x}u\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for each
x
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(d)

Suppose
K_{x}u\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
If
K
is translation-invariant or, more generally, if
K_{x}u
is bounded, then
K_{x}u\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Now we are ready to present the second main separation result.
Let
\partial\mathcal{H}_{K}
denote the set of functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form
g_{i}(x)=\partial_{x_{i}}h_{i}(x)
where
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
.

Theorem 4.5

(
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-Based Separation with Langevin KSD; Thm. 5 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Suppose
P\in\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
for a matrix-valued kernel
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Then

1.

If
K
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-ISPD, then the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy separates
P
from
\{Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{K,0}:(s_{p}-s_{q})q\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}
.

2.

If
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
,
(s_{p}-s_{q})q\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
.

3.

If
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\partial\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
s_{q}q\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
.

Although the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-ISPD requirement is somewhat restrictive (for instance, it precludes slowly-decaying inverse multi-quadric kernels; c.f.
Example

2.13
), it does enable separation for certain
Q
that are not covered by score-based separation (for instance,
Theorem

4.5
applies to Cauchy
Q
and Gaussian
P
, while
Theorem

4.3
does not, since
Q\notin\mathcal{P}_{s_{p}}
).

One might hope that under appropriate assumptions the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy could separate
P
from
all
alternatives
Q\in\mathcal{P}
.
The main obstacle to establishing such general separation is the unboundedness of the Langevin Stein kernel
k_{p}
; for sufficiently heavy-tailed
Q
, the kernel mean embedding
\int k_{p}(\cdot,x)\,\mathrm{d}Q(x)
will fail to exist, and
Q\notin\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}
.
A naïve solution is to modify the Stein kernel so that it is
bounded
, meaning that
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{k_{p}}
for all
Q\in\mathcal{P}
, but it is then not clear whether separation properties still hold.
A positive answer is provided by
Theorem

4.7
, and is our third main separation result.

The idea behind
Theorem

4.7
is to show that
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}
contains a sub-reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
bounded
functions that are rich enough to separate
P
from alternatives
Q\in\mathcal{P}
.
As a shorthand, we say that a Stein discrepancy or Stein kernel is
P
-separating
if it separates
P
from
\mathcal{P}
.

Definition 4.11

(
P
-separating and Bounded
P
-separating; Def. 3 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

A set of functions
\mathcal{F}
is
bounded
P
-separating
if
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\cap\mathcal{F}
is
P
-separating
,
i.e., if
Q\in\mathcal{P}
and
Q(h)=P(h)
for all
h\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\cap\mathcal{F}
then
Q=P
.

The first step in the argument is to obtain conditions on the base kernel
k
under which the Stein kernel
k_{p}
in (
46
) will be bounded.

Theorem 4.6

(Bounded Langevin Stein kernels; Thm. 7 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Suppose a matrix-valued kernel
K
with
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic.
If
\|s_{p}(x)\|\leq\theta(x)
for
\theta\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\frac{1}{\theta}\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the Stein kernel
k_{p}
in (
46
) induced by the tilted base kernel

\displaystyle\frac{K(x,y)}{\theta(x)\theta(y)}

(48)

is bounded and
P
-separating.

One can equivalently view the Stein discrepancy associated to the tilted base kernel in (
48
) as a diffusion Stein operator (c.f.
Section

11
) with covariance coefficient
a(x)=\theta(x)^{-1}I_{d\times d}
applied to the untilted base kernel
K
.

Our next step is to show that reproducing kernel Hilbert spacees based on standard translation-invariant kernels contain sub-reproducing kernel Hilbert spacees with bounded kernels of the form (
48
):

Proposition 4.8

(Translation-invariant kernels have rapidly decreasing sub-reproducing kernel Hilbert spacees; Thm. 8 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Suppose a kernel
k
with
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is translation-invariant with a spectral density bounded away from zero on compact sets.
Then there exist a translation-invariant,
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}
-characteristic kernel
k_{s}\in C^{(1,1)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and, for each
c>0
, a positive-definite
function
f
with

\frac{1}{f}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\displaystyle\max(|f(x)|,\left\|{\partial f(x)}\right\|)=O(e^{-c\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sqrt{|x_{i}|}})

such that

\mathcal{H}_{k_{f}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{k}
for
k_{f}(x,y)\triangleq f(x)k_{s}(x,y)f(y)
.

Combining
Theorems

4.6
and
4.8
leads to
Theorem

4.7
, our third main separation result.
A function
f
is said to have
at most root exponential growth
if

\displaystyle f(x)=O\left(\exp\left(c\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sqrt{|x_{i}|}\right)\right)

(49)

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
We also introduce the shorthand
\mathcal{P}_{k,0}
for
\mathcal{P}_{K,0}
when
K=kI
.

Theorem 4.7

(General
P
-separation with Langevin KSDs; Thm. 9 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Suppose a kernel
k
with
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is translation-invariant with a spectral density bounded away from zero on compact sets.
Define the tilted kernel
k_{f}(x,y)\triangleq f(x)k(x,y)f(y)
for each
strictly positive
f\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(a)

If
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{k,0}}
and

\|s_{p}\|
has at most root exponential growth, then the Stein kernel induced by the base kernel
k
is bounded
P
-separating.

(b)

If
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k_{f},0}
and
f
,
\partial f
, and
f\|s_{p}\|
have at most root exponential growth, then the Stein kernel induced by
k_{f}
is bounded
P
-separating.

Separation is the main requirement of Stein discrepancies as used in goodness-of-fit testing (c.f.
Section

27.2
), but for other applications the stronger properties of convergence detection and control are required.
Our attention therefore turns next to convergence detection (
Section

18.3
) and control (
Section

18.4
).

18.3
Detecting Convergence with Langevin KSD

In
Section

16
we saw that classical Stein discrepancies detect convergence in the 1-Wasserstein metric (
Proposition

4.3
).
The same convergence detection holds also for Langevin kernel Stein discrepancys, as stated in the following result.
In the special case of a kernel
K(x,y)=k(x,y)I
we write
\mathcal{G}_{k}
as a shorthand for
\mathcal{G}_{K}
.

Proposition 4.9

(Langevin KSD detects convergence; Prop. 9 of
Gorham and Mackey 2017
)
.

Assume that
\nabla\log p
is Lipschitz with
\nabla\log p\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(P)
.
Then, for
k\in C_{b}^{(2,2)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy satisfies

\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\to 0
whenever
\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}^{1}(Q_{n},P)\to 0
.

Compared to classical Stein discrepancies (
Proposition

4.3
for the Langevin Stein operator; i.e.
a=I
,
c=0
,
b=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\log p
), no boundedness assumption on the gradient of
\nabla\log p
is required in
Proposition

4.9
.
However,
Proposition

4.9
requires
\nabla\log p\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(P)
, while
Proposition

4.3
requires only
\nabla\log p\in\mathcal{L}^{1+\epsilon}(P)
for some
\epsilon>0
.

18.4
Controlling Convergence with Langevin KSD

Next we consider the converse question; whether Langevin kernel Stein discrepancys offer control of more standard statistical divergences.
Our principal focus is on controlling the Dudley metric (which we recall metrizes weak convergence of distributions; c.f.
Definition

2.27
), but the possibility to control Wasserstein metrics (similarly to classical Stein discrepancies; c.f.
Theorem

4.1
) will also be discussed.

As a useful shorthand, we say that the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy
controls weak
P
-convergence
if
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
implies
\mathrm{BL}_{\|\cdot\|}(P,Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
.
The concept of
tightness
is often a necessary criterion for proving the weak convergence of a sequence of probability measures:

Definition 4.12

(Tightness)
.

A sequence of probability distributions
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is called
tight
if, for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
Q_{n}(S^{c})\leq\epsilon
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Intuitively, a tight sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
cannot have probability mass ‘escaping to infinity’.

Theorem 4.8

(Controlling tight convergence with Langevin KSD; Thms. 7 & 9 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Under the conditions of
Theorem

4.6
or
4.7
, if
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight, then
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
implies
\mathrm{BL}_{\|\cdot\|}(P,Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
.

The question thus becomes one of understanding when convergence of the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy implies tightness of
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Recall from (
4.2
) that
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})
can be expressed as
\mathrm{MMD}_{k_{p}}(P,\cdot)
using the Stein kernel
k_{p}
in (
46
).
Further, from (
17
) the maximum mean discrepancy has the representation

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k_{p}}(P,Q)=\sup_{\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}\leq 1}P(f)-Q(f)

so that the reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}
determines the topology that is induced by the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy.
A richer set of test functions corresponds to a more stringent statistical divergence, and if we seek to deduce tightness of
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
from convergence of the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy then the following will be required of
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}
:

Definition 4.13

(
P
-dominating indicators; Def. 4 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

A set of functions
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
is said to
P
-dominate indicators
if, for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a function
h\in\mathcal{F}
that satisfy

\textstyle h(x)-P(h)\geq\mathbb{I}_{S^{c}}(x)-\epsilon

(50)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Definition

4.13
ensures that a sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
can only approximate
P
well in the sense of integrating the elements of
\mathcal{F}
if it places uniformly little mass outside of a compact set
S
.
Combining
Theorem

4.8
with
Definition

4.13
provides sufficient conditions for weak
P
-convergence control:

Corollary 4.9

(Controlling weak
P
-convergence with KSDs; Cor. 3 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Under the conditions of
Theorem

4.3
,
Theorem

4.6
or
4.7
, if
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}
also
P
-dominates indicators, then the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy controls weak
P
-convergence.

All that remains is to relate the property of
P
-dominating indicators to the choice of the kernel
K
in the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy.
As a useful stepping stone, we recall the definition of a
coercive
function:

Definition 4.14

(Coercive function;
Hodgkinson et al. 2020
)
.

A function
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
coercive
if, for any
M>0
, there exists a compact set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\inf_{x\in S^{c}}h(x)>M
.

Note that any continuous coercive function is also bounded below, as continuous functions are bounded on any compact set.

Lemma 4.10

(Coercive functions dominate indicators; Lem. 1 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

If there exists an element
h\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}
that is coercive and bounded below and
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}}
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}

P
-dominates indicators.

Recall that the elements of
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}
have the form
\mathcal{T}_{P}h=\nabla\cdot h+s_{p}\cdot h
for
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
For most typical choices of
K
the first term,
\nabla\cdot h
, will be bounded.
On the other hand, for
K
sufficiently heavy-tailed and appropriately increasing
s_{p}
, the second term
s_{p}\cdot h
can be coercive and lower-bounded.
It turns out that the inverse multi-quadric kernel (
Example

2.13
) fulfills this requirement:

Theorem 4.11

(IMQ KSDs control weak
P
-convergence; Thm. 11 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Consider a target measure
P\in\mathcal{P}
with score
s_{p}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{L}^{1}(P)
and suppose that, for some dissipativity rate
u>1/2
and
c,c_{1},c_{2}>0
,
P
satisfies the
generalized dissipativity
condition

\displaystyle-\langle{s_{p}(x)},{x}\rangle-c\left\|{s_{p}(x)}\right\|_{1}\geq c_{1}\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}^{2u}-c_{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(51)

If
k
is the inverse multi-quadric kernel (
Example

2.13
) with exponent
\beta\in(0,2u-1)
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}

P
-dominates indicators.
If, in addition,
\left\|{s_{p}}\right\|
has at most root exponential growth in the sense of (
49
), then the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy controls weak
P
-convergence.

Theorem

4.11
established weak convergence control using a Stein kernel
k_{p}
that can be unbounded.
Oftentimes in the analysis and application of kernel methods it is helpful to work with kernels that are bounded, and it turns out that one can also arrange for a Stein kernel to be bounded.
Moreover, the bounded Stein kernels we construct in
Theorem

4.12
exactly metrize weak convergence to
P
, meaning that
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
if and only if
\mathrm{BL}_{\|\cdot\|}(P,Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
.
The price that we pay for this stronger result is that the base kernel
K
is no longer translation-invariant, meaning some reasonable choice of an origin is required:

Theorem 4.12

(Metrizing weak
P
-convergence with bounded Stein kernels; Thm. 12 of
Barp et al. 2024
)
.

Consider a target measure
P\in\mathcal{P}
with score
s_{p}

that, for some dissipativity rate
u>1/2
and
c,c_{1},c_{2}>0
, satisfies the generalized dissipativity condition
Eq.

51
.
Define the Stein kernel with diagonal base kernel
K
such that
K_{i,i}(x,y)=a(\left\|{x}\right\|_{2})(x_{i}y_{i}+k(x,y))a(\left\|{y}\right\|_{2})
, i.e.,

k_{p}(x,y)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq d}\frac{\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}x\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}y(p(x)a(\left\|{x}\right\|_{2})(x_{i}y_{i}+k(x,y))a(\left\|{y}\right\|_{2})p(y))}{p(x)p(y)},

for
k
characteristic to
{D}^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L^{1}}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and
a(\left\|{x}\right\|_{2})\triangleq(\sigma^{2}+\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}^{2})^{-\gamma}
a tilting function with
\sigma>0
and
\gamma\leq u
.
The following statements hold true:

(a)

If
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{K,0}}
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}

P
-dominates indicators.

(b)

If
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle{K,0}}
,
\gamma\geq 0
, and
\left\|{s_{p}(x)}\right\|_{2}\leq(\sigma^{2}+\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\gamma}
, then
k_{p}
is bounded
P
-separating and controls weak
P
-convergence.

(c)

If
\left\|{s_{p}(x)}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}\leq(\sigma^{2}+\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\gamma}
and
s_{p}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p}}\subset C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
k_{p}
metrizes weak
P
-convergence; i.e.
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
if and only if
\mathrm{BL}_{\|\cdot\|}(P,Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
.

This discussion focused on controlling weak convergence with Langevin kernel Stein discrepancys, but stronger notions of convergence are also available, and one might want to understand when these can be controlled using a kernel Stein discrepancy.
The contribution of
Kanagawa et al. 2025
was to establish sufficient conditions on
P
and on
K
under which the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy provides control over convergence in the sense of the Wasserstein metrics (c.f.
Definition

2.28
); i.e.
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
implies
\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|}^{s}(P,Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
.
Convergence in
s
-Wasserstein is equivalent to weak convergence plus convergence in moments up to order
s
when
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and it is thus clear that a stronger conclusion is obtained.

18.5
Constrained Kernel Stein Discrepancies

Consider now a constrained domain
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with boundary
\partial\mathcal{X}
and
n(x)
the outward-facing unit normal to
\partial\mathcal{X}
at
x\in\partial\mathcal{X}
.
Selecting a kernel that vanishes on the boundary, i.e.,
K(\cdot,x)n(x)=0
for all
x\in\partial\mathcal{X}
, also ensures that each element
g
of the kernel Stein set
\mathcal{G}_{K}
vanishes on the boundary in the sense of
Eq.

29
.
This provides a particularly convenient way to ensure that

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial\mathcal{X}}p(x)g(x)\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)\;\mathrm{d}x

and hence that
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)=0
via the divergence theorem
Theorem

3.2
.

18.6
Kernel Stein Discrepancies in General

Kernel Stein sets are computationally convenient, circumventing the need to numerically evaluate the supremum in
Equation

40
.
The previous sections focused on the combination of the Langevin Stein operator and a kernel Stein set, but several other choices of Stein operators are available (c.f.
Chapter

3
) and can also be combined with a kernel Stein set.
For example,
diffusion KSDs
, combining the diffusion Stein operators (
Definition

3.5
) with kernel Stein sets, were introduced in
Barp et al. 2019
, where sufficient conditions for separation were presented, and further analyzed in
Kanagawa et al. 2025
, where conditions for Wasserstein convergence control were presented. Squared diffusion KSDs have also been applied and analyzed in the setting of causal model learning under the name
kernel deviation from stationary

(
Lorch et al. 2024
;
Bleile et al. 2026
)
.
The combination of mirrored Stein operators (
Definition

3.6
) and kernel Stein sets was studied in
Shi et al. 2022a
, where sufficient conditions for weak convergence control were presented.
The gradient-free Stein operator (
Definition

3.7
) was paired with a kernel Stein set in
Fisher and Oates 2023
, with conditions for convergence detection and control established.
The combination of discrete Stein operators (
Section

14
) and kernel Stein sets was considered in
Yang et al. 2018
, where sufficient conditions for separation were presented.
In addition, extension of kernel Stein discrepancys to more complicated domains, such as high-dimensional Euclidean spaces
(
Gong et al. 2021
)
, sequence spaces
(
Baum et al. 2023
)
, Riemannian manifolds
(
Xu and Matsuda 2020
;
Barp et al. 2022
)
, and infinite-dimensional spaces of functions
(
Wynne and Duncan 2022
)
have been studied.

19
Random Feature Stein Discrepancies

One potential drawback of graph and kernel Stein discrepancies is that both have computational costs that grow at least quadratically with the sample size
(
Gorham and Mackey 2017
)
.
To address this limitation,
Huggins and Mackey 2018
developed a class of Stein discrepancies that can be cheaply and accurately approximated with random sampling.
The idea is to consider a so-called
feature
Stein set:

Definition 4.15

(Feature Stein set;
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

Consider a
feature function

\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}

which, for some
r\in[1,\infty)
and all
x,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, satisfies
\Phi(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Phi(\cdot,z)\in C^{1}
.
We define the associated
feature Stein set
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\Phi,r}\triangleq\Bigg\{g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\Bigg|

\displaystyle g_{i}(x)=\int\Phi(x,z)\overline{f_{i}(z)}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}z\quad\text{ with }\quad

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|{f_{i}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\leq 1\text{ for }s=\frac{r}{r-1}\Bigg\}.

Naturally a Stein discrepancy associated with a feature Stein set is called a
feature
Stein discrepancy:

Definition 4.16

(Feature Stein discrepancy;
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a convex subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and

P
has density
p>0
on
\mathcal{X}
.
If
\mathcal{G}_{\Phi,r}
is a feature Stein set (
Definition

4.15
)
and

\mathcal{T}_{P}
is the Langevin Stein operator (
Definition

3.2
) for
P
, then
we call

\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r}(\cdot,P)\triangleq\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{\Phi,r})

a
feature Stein discrepancy
.

For the presentation in this section it is convenient to decompose the Langevin Stein operator as a sum

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{x_{i}}g(x)+g_{i}(x)\partial_{x_{i}}(\log p)(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\mathcal{T}_{i}g)(x)

(52)

where
\mathcal{T}_{i}
is used to indicate the
i
th coordinate of
\mathcal{T}_{P}
, with the dependence on (fixed)
P
left implicit.
For a bivariate function
\Phi(\cdot,\cdot)
we let
\mathcal{T}_{i}\Phi
denote the action of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
on the first argument of
\Phi
.

Proposition 4.10

(
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
explicit form;
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

In the setting of
Definition

4.16
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r}^{2}(Q,P)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|{Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

(53)

for all
Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.

Proof.

Using the notation in (
52
), and the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{\Phi,r}
in
Definition

4.15
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r}^{2}(Q,P)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\Phi,r}}|Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)|^{2}=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\Phi,r}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{d}Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{g_{i}})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt=\sup_{f:v_{i}=\left\|{f_{i}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\left\|{v}\right\|_{2}\leq 1}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})(z)\overline{f_{i}(z)}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}z\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-50.0pt=\sup_{v:\left\|{v}\right\|_{2}\leq 1}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|{Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}v_{i}\right|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|{Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2},

as required.
∎

At this point no reduction in computational cost has been achieved; for instance when
r=2
, the
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
is simply the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy with base kernel

K(x,y)=k(x,y)I_{d\times d}
with
k(x,y)=\int\Phi(x,z)\overline{\Phi(y,z)}\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}z
, and as such it is associated with a quadratic computational cost.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\mathrm{KSD}_{P}^{2}(Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(Q\times Q)((\mathcal{T}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{i})k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|{Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}=\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,2}(Q,P)^{2}.

To reduce the computational cost some form of approximation is needed.
Approximating the norm term
\|{Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
using a importance sampling leads to a randomized approximation of the feature Stein discrepancy:

Definition 4.17

(Random feature Stein discrepancy;
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

For a target feature Stein discrepancy
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r}
and an importance sampling distribution with (Lebesgue) density
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the
random feature Stein discrepancy

\displaystyle\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r,\nu,M}^{2}(Q,P)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\frac{|Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})(Z_{j})|^{r}}{\nu(Z_{j})}\right)^{2/r}

for
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{M}\overset{\textrm{\tiny{i.i.d.} }}{\sim}\nu
and
Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.

For
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
with finite support, the
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
computation reduces to computing
mnd
scaled
random features
,
(\mathcal{T}_{i}{\Phi})(x_{i},Z_{j})/\nu(Z_{j})^{1/r}
, and this evaluation can be carried out in parallel.

19.1
Special Cases of
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}

One can straightforwardly use the
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
framework of
Definition

4.17
to construct low-cost approximations to standard KSDs.
For example when the kernel Stein discrepancy base kernel
k(x,y)=\Psi(x-y)
for some
\Psi\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, one can choose
r=2
,
\nu\propto{F}(\Psi)
, and
\Phi(x,z)=e^{-i\langle{z},{x}\rangle}{F}(\Psi)(z)^{1/2}
to design a random Fourier feature
(
Rahimi and Recht 2007
, RFF)
approximation
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,2,\nu,M}
to
\operatorname{KSD}_{k}

(
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.
While eminently practical, this RFF-KSD does suffer from a known limitation: for an uncountable number of distributions
Q\neq P
, this
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,2,\nu,M}(Q,P)=0
with high probability
(
Chwialkowski et al. 2015
, Prop. 1)
.

To avoid this undesirable characteristic,
Jitkrittum et al. 2017
developed an alternative
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
called the random finite set Stein discrepancy (FSSD-rand).
In the notation of
Definition

4.17
, FSSD-rand is obtained by selecting
r=2
, any feature count
M
and importance sampling distribution
\nu
, and
\Phi(x,z)=f(x,z)\nu(z)^{1/2}
where
f
is a real analytic and
C_{0}
-universal
(
Carmeli et al. 2010
, Def. 4.1)
reproducing kernel.
The real analyticity in particular ensures that for each
Q\neq P
,
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,2,\nu,M}(Q,P)>0)=1

(
Jitkrittum et al. 2017
, Thm. 1)
.
In
Sections

19.2
and
19.3
, we will see that
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
features of a different form additionally give rise to strong convergence-determining properties and that selecting
r\neq 2
can yield provably accurate approximations with a substantially smaller sampling budget
M
.

19.2
Selecting a Feature Function
\Phi

The main concern when selecting a feature function
\Phi
is that we do not ‘lose’ information that would be useful in comparing an approximation
Q_{n}
to the target
P
.
The content of
Proposition

4.11
is to establish conditions under which
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
controls the Langevin KSD, whose properties are now well-understood (c.f.
Section

18.1
).
Let
{F}(f)
denote the generalized Fourier transform of
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
The following follows from the generalized Hölder inequality and the Babenko–Beckner inequality:

Proposition 4.11

(Controlling Langevin KSD with
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
; Prop. 3.1 of
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

If
{r\in[1,2]}
,
\rho\in\mathcal{L}^{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
t={r}{/(2-r)}
, and
K(x,x^{\prime})=k(x,x^{\prime})I_{d\times d}
with

\displaystyle k(x,y)=\int{F}(\Phi(x,\cdot))(\omega)\overline{{F}(\Phi(y,\cdot))(\omega)}\rho(\omega)\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}\omega,

then
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}^{2}(Q_{n})\leq\textstyle\|{\rho}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}^{2}_{\Phi,r}(Q_{n},P)
.

That is, for a kernel
k
of the above form, convergence of the
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
implies convergence of the Langevin KSD.
Once we have selected a feature function, we can now set about approximating it using so-called
random features

(
Rahimi and Recht 2007
)
.
Before presenting results for convergence detection and control using random features, we first describe assumptions on the base kernel.

Assumption 4.1
.

The base kernel has the form
k(x,y)=A_{n}(x)\Psi(x-y)A_{n}(y)

for
\Psi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
A\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
A_{n}(x)\triangleq A(x-m_{n})
,
where
A>0
,
\nabla\log A
is bounded and Lipschitz, and
m_{n}\triangleq\int x\;\mathrm{d}Q_{n}(x)
.

Notice that
4.1
allows the base kernel
k
to be dependent (via the sequence index
n
) on the distribution
Q_{n}
whose approximation quality is being assessed.

Assumption 4.2
.

4.1
holds and
\Phi(x,z)=A_{n}(x)F(x-z)
, where

F\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is positive, and there exist a norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
and constants
s,C_{1}>0
such that

|\partial_{x_{d}}\log F(x)|\leq C_{1}(1+\left\|{x}\right\|^{s}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \lim_{\left\|{x}\right\|\to\infty}(1+\left\|{x}\right\|^{s})F(x)=0,

and

F(x-z)\leq C_{1}\frac{F(z)}{F(x)}.

In addition, there exist a constant
C_{2}\in(0,1]
and continuous, non-increasing function
f
such
that
C_{2}\,f(\left\|{x}\right\|)\leq F(x)\leq f(\left\|{x}\right\|)
.

The following result establishes that convergence in a
tilted
1-Wasserstein distance (c.f.
Section

9.2
) is detected by an appropriate
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
:

Theorem 4.13

(Detecting Convergence with
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
; Prop. 3.3 of
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

Suppose
4.2
holds with
F\in\mathcal{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

1/A
bounded,
x\mapsto x/A(x)
Lipschitz, and
\mathbb{E}_{P}[{A}(Z)\left\|{Z}\right\|_{2}^{2}]<\infty
.
Then

\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}^{1,A_{n}}(Q_{n},P)\rightarrow 0\implies\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r}(Q_{n},P)\to 0\\
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r,\nu_{n},M_{n}}(Q_{n},P)\overset{P}{\to}0\end{array}\right.

for any choices of
r\in[1,2]
,
\nu_{n}
, and
M_{n}\geq 1
.

19.3
Selecting an Importance Sampling Distribution
\nu

The choice of importance sampling distribution
\nu
is motivated by having
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
close to its reference
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
even when the importance sample size
M
is small.
The strategy pursued in
Huggins and Mackey 2018
was to choose
\nu
such that the second moment of each random feature
|Q(\mathcal{T}_{i}\Phi)(Z)|^{r}/\nu(Z)
is bounded by a
power of its mean:

Definition 4.18

(
(C,\gamma)
second moments)
.

Fix a target distribution
P
.
For
Z\sim\nu
,
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, and
n\geq 1
, let
Y_{n,i}\triangleq w_{i}(Z,Q_{n})\triangleq|(Q_{n}\mathcal{T}_{i}\Phi)(Z)|^{r}/\nu(Z)
. If for some
C>0
and
\gamma\in[0,2]
we have

\mathbb{E}[Y_{n,i}^{2}]\leq C\mathbb{E}[Y_{n,i}]^{2-\gamma}

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
and
n\geq 1
,
then we say
(\Phi,r,\nu)
yields
(C,\gamma)
second moments for
P
and
Q_{n}
.

Theorem 4.14

(Controlling Langevin KSD with
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
; Prop. 3.6 of
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

Suppose
(\Phi,r,\nu)
yields
(C,\gamma)
second moments for
P
and
Q_{n}
.
If
M\geq 2C\mathbb{E}[Y_{n,i}]^{-\gamma}\log(d/\delta)/\epsilon^{2}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, then,
with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r,\nu,M}(Q_{n},P)\geq(1-\epsilon)^{1/r}\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r}(Q_{n},P).

Under the further assumptions of
Proposition

4.11
, if the reference
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\geq c_{1}n^{-1/2}
for some
c_{1}
, then there exists
c_{2}>0
such that a sample size

M\geq\frac{c_{2}Cn^{\gamma r/2}\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\gamma r/2}\log(d/\delta)}{\epsilon^{2}}

suffices to have, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|{\rho}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1/2}\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}_{\Phi,r,\nu,M}(Q_{n},P)\geq(1-\epsilon)^{1/r}\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n}).

There are several settings where
\mathrm{KSD}_{P}(Q_{n})\gtrsim n^{-1/2}
holds; most notably when the states
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are independent and identically distributed.
This makes the
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
suitable for goodness-of-fit testing (c.f.
Section

27.2
), for example, since in this context the performance of the Stein discrepancy test statistic under the IID null is of interest.
A smaller
r
leads to significant improvements in the sample complexity
M=\Omega(n^{\gamma r/2})
.
For example, if the weight function is bounded (so that
\gamma=1
), it suffices to choose
r=1
and
M=\Omega(n^{1/2})
. In what follows, we will demonstrate how to select
\nu
with
\gamma

arbitrarily close to 0.

To make use of
Theorem

4.14
it remains to establish when
(\Phi,r,\nu)
yields
(C,\gamma)
second moments for
P
and
Q_{n}
.

Assumption 4.3

(Distant Dissipativity;
Eberle 2015
;
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

The distribution
P
has
\nabla\log p
Lipschitz and there exist
\kappa>0
and
r\geq 0
for which the
(\kappa,r)
-
distant dissipativity
condition

\displaystyle\langle{\nabla\log p(x)-\nabla\log p(y)},{x-y}\rangle\leq-\kappa\left\|{x-y}\right\|_{2}^{2}+r,\qquad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is satisfied.

The following provides simple conditions and a choice for
\nu
under which
(C,1)
second moments are guaranteed:

Proposition 4.12

(Prop. 3.7 of
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

Assume that
4.1
and
4.2
hold with
s=0
, that
4.3
holds, and there exists a constant
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

Q_{n}([1+\left\|{\cdot}\right\|]A_{n})\leq\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
If
\nu(z)\propto Q_{n}([1+\left\|{\cdot}\right\|]\Phi(\cdot,z))
,
then for any
r\geq 1
,
(\Phi,r,\nu)
yields
(C,1)
second moments for
P
and
Q_{n}
.

In order to obtain
(C,\gamma)
moments for
\gamma<1
, we will choose
\nu
such that
w_{i}(z,Q_{n})
decays sufficiently quickly as
\|z\|\rightarrow\infty
.
For this, two integrability conditions involving the Fourier transforms of
\Psi
and
F
are required:

Assumption 4.4
.

4.2
and
4.1
hold,
\omega_{1}^{2}{F}(\Psi)^{1/2}(\omega)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
for
t=r/(2-r)
,
{F}(\Psi)/{F}(F)^{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The
\mathcal{L}^{1}
condition is weak, while the
\mathcal{L}^{t}
condition ensures that
Proposition

4.11
applies to our chosen
\operatorname{\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
.

Theorem

4.15
shows that one can improve the importance sample growth rate
\gamma
of an
\operatorname{R\mathrm{\Phi}SD}
by increasing the smoothness
\lambda
of
F
and decreasing the over-dispersion parameter
\xi
of
\nu
.

Theorem 4.15

(Thm. 3.8 of
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
.

Let
4.2
,
4.1
,
4.3
, and
4.4
hold, and suppose there exists
\mathcal{C}>0
such that,

Q_{n}\left([1+\left\|{\cdot}\right\|+\left\|{\cdot-m_{n}}\right\|^{s}]A_{n}/F(\cdot-m_{n})\right)\leq\mathcal{C}\quad\text{for all}\quad n\geq 1.

Then there is a constant
b\in[0,1)
such that the following holds.
For any
\xi\in(0,1-b)
,
c>0
, and
\alpha>2(1-\overline{\lambda})
,
if
\nu(z)\geq c\,\Psi(z-m_{n})^{\xi r}
, then
there exists a constant
C_{\alpha}>0
such that

(\Phi,r,\nu)
yields
(C_{\alpha},\gamma_{\alpha})
second moments
for
P
and
Q_{n}
, where
\gamma_{\alpha}\triangleq\alpha+(2-\alpha)\xi/(2-b-\xi)
.

20
Stochastic Stein Discrepancies

The final class of Stein discrepancies that we consider were developed to deal with the scenario where the target distribution
P
has a density that is the product

\displaystyle p(x)=\prod_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}(x)

of a large number of factors
p_{l}(x)
, which need not individually integrate to 1.
This structure arises in the
tall data
context, where
x
represents the parameter of a statistical model conditional on which data are treated as independent.
The principal challenge with using a Langevin Stein discrepancy in this setting is that computation of the gradient
\nabla\log p
requires summing over
L
separate terms, which for discrete
Q_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
entails a
O(nL)
cost, this can be a non-trivial computational requirement.
However, this is the same issue that is encountered in empirical risk minimization in machine learning, where powerful computational solutions have been proposed based on sub-sampling.

Stochastic Stein discrepancies
aim to also exploit sub-sampling to provide a meaningful discrepancy at reduced computational cost.
The main idea is to consider a decomposable Stein operator of the form
\mathcal{T}_{P}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathcal{T}_{l}
where
\mathcal{T}_{l}
is a Stein operator targeting
p_{l}
, albeit
p_{l}
need not be a normalized probability distribution in general.
Then, for a subset
\sigma\subseteq[L]
, we denote the subset operator

\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}\triangleq\sum_{l\in\sigma}\mathcal{T}_{l}
whose computational cost is linear in the size
|\sigma|
of the subset.

Definition 4.19

(Stochastic Stein discrepancy;
Gorham et al. 2020
)
.

Consider a decomposable Stein operator of the form
\mathcal{T}_{P}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathcal{T}_{l}
and
Q_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
.
Fix a batch size
m
and, for each
i\in[n]
, independently select a uniformly random subset
\sigma_{i}
of size
m
from
[L]
.
Then for any Stein set
\mathcal{G}
in the domain of each of the
\mathcal{T}_{i}
, we define the
stochastic Stein discrepancy
as the random quantity

\displaystyle\mathcal{SS}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{L}{m}(\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{i}}{g})({x_{i}})\right|.

(54)

Although the stochastic Stein discrepancy coincides with the standard Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G})
when the batch size
m
is equal to
L
, we do not view stochastic Stein discrepancy as an attempt to approximate Stein discrepancy.
Rather, stochastic Stein discrepancy is a discrepancy
in its own right
and is accompanied by (probabilistic) guarantees of convergence detection (
Section

20.1
) and convergence control (
Section

20.2
).

20.1
Detecting Convergence with SSDs

The following allows for an evolving sequence of Stein sets
\mathcal{G}_{n}
to accommodate the graph Stein sets of
Gorham and Mackey 2015
;
Gorham et al. 2019
.
Let
{[L]\choose m}\triangleq\{\sigma\subseteq[L]:|\sigma|=m\}
represent all size
m
subsets of
[L]
.

Theorem 4.16

(SSDs detect convergence; Thm. 2 of
Gorham et al. 2020
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}=\cup_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{G}_{n}
.
Consider a decomposable Stein operator of the form
\mathcal{T}_{P}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathcal{T}_{l}
where
P(\mathcal{T}_{P}\mathcal{G})=\{0\}
.
Suppose that for some
a,c>0
and each
\sigma\in{[L]\choose m}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}\mathcal{G}\subset C(\mathcal{X})
,

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|(\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}{g})({x})|\leq c(1+\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}^{a})

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\sup_{x,y\in K}\frac{|(\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}{g})({x})-(\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}{g})({y})|}{\left\|{x-y}\right\|_{2}}<\infty

for each compact set
K
.
If
\mathrm{W}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}^{a}(Q_{n},P)\to 0
, then
\mathcal{SS}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\to}}0
.

Theorem

4.16
shows that SSD detects Wasserstein convergence with probability 1 if the operators
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}
generate continuous functions that grow no more quickly than a polynomial and have locally bounded derivatives.

20.2
Controlling Convergence with SSDs

First, to each Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{n})
a
bounded Stein discrepancy
based on the modified Stein set

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{b,n}\triangleq\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n}:\|\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}g\|_{\infty}\leq 1\forall\sigma\in{\textstyle{[L]\choose m}}\right\}

(55)

in which each Stein function is constrained to be bounded under each subset operator
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}
.

Theorem 4.17

(Bounded Stein discrepancies control tight convergence; Thm. 3 of
Gorham et al. 2020
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Consider the Langevin Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}{}
(c.f.
Definition

3.2
) satisfying
4.3
.
Suppose
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}{\left\|{\nabla\log p_{\sigma}(x)}\right\|_{2}}{/(1+\left\|{x}\right\|_{2})}<\infty
for each
\sigma\in{[L]\choose m}
, fix a sequence of probability measures
(Q_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
,
and
consider the bounded Stein set

\mathcal{G}_{b,n}
in (
55
)
for any of the following sets
\mathcal{G}_{n}
:

(
A.1
)

\mathcal{G}_{n}=\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|}
, the classical Stein set with arbitrary vector norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
(
Definition

4.4
).

(
A.2
)

\mathcal{G}_{n}=\mathcal{G}_{\left\|{\cdot}\right\|,Q,{G}}
, the graph Stein set with arbitrary vector norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
and a finite graph
G=(V,E)
with vertices
V\subset\mathcal{X}
(
Definition

4.6
).

(
A.3
)

\mathcal{G}_{n}=\mathcal{G}_{K}
, the kernel Stein set with
K(x,y)=\Phi(x-y)I_{d\times d}
for
\Phi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
with non-vanishing Fourier transform (
Definition

4.8
).

If
Q_{n}\not\Rightarrow P
, then either
\mathcal{S}({Q_{n}},{\mathcal{T}_{P}{}},{\mathcal{G}_{b,n}})\not\to 0
or
(Q_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is not tight (c.f.
Definition

4.12
).

Theorem 4.18

(SSDs control bounded Stein discrepancy convergence; Thm. 4 of
Gorham et al. 2020
)
.

If
\mathcal{S}({Q_{n}},{\mathcal{T}{}},{\mathcal{G}_{b,n}})\not\to 0
, then, with probability
1
,

\mathcal{SS}({Q_{n}},\mathcal{T}{},\mathcal{G}_{n})\not\to 0
.

Proposition 4.13

(Coercive SSDs enforce tightness; Prop. 5 of
Gorham et al. 2020
)
.

If
(Q_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is not tight and
\frac{L}{m}\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}g
is coercive and bounded below for some
g\in\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{n}
and all
\sigma\in{[L]\choose m}
, then surely
\mathcal{SS}({Q_{n}},\mathcal{T}{},\mathcal{G}_{n})\not\to 0
.

Taken together, these results imply that stochastic Stein discrepancys equipped with the Langevin operator and any of the Stein sets in
Theorem

4.17
control convergence with probability
1
under standard dissipativity and growth conditions on the subsampled operator:

Theorem 4.19

(SSDs control convergence; Thm. 6 of
Gorham et al. 2020
)
.

Under the notation of
Theorem

4.17
, suppose

\nabla\log p
is Lipschitz,
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|{\nabla\log p_{\sigma}(x)}\right\|_{2}/(1+\left\|{x}\right\|_{2})<\infty
for all
\sigma\in{[L]\choose m}
, and, for some
\kappa>0
and
r\geq 0
, a
(\kappa,r)
-distant dissipativity condition (c.f.
4.3
) holds uniformly for all
p_{\sigma}
with
\sigma\in{[L]\choose m}
.
Consider the radial function
\Phi(x)\triangleq(1+\left\|{\Gamma x}\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\beta}
for
\beta\in(-1,0)
and any positive definite matrix
\Gamma
.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{n}
be any of the Stein sets in
Theorem

4.17
.
If
Q_{n}\not\Rightarrow P
, then, with probability
1
,

\mathcal{SS}({Q_{n}},\mathcal{T}_{P}{},\mathcal{G}_{n})\not\to 0
.

Chapter 5
Stein Dynamics

The previous Chapter discussed Stein discrepancy as a statistical divergence with attractive computational properties.
In this Chapter we consider inference and learning via minimization of Kullback–Leilber divergence, drawing an important connection to Stein’s method via mass transport.

21
Dynamic Mass Transport

Assume we are interested in transforming one distribution
Q
into another distribution
P
on the same domain
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
There are two different approaches to achieve this transformation.

One approach involves simulating from
Q
and assigning each realization a weight based on the density ratio

r=\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
,
which is related to techniques such as importance sampling, thinning, and birth-death processes.
However, the performance of this reweighting method tends to deteriorate when the variance of the density ratio

\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
between
Q
and
P
is high.

Another approach is transport-based, which applies a transformation to convert a random variable distributed according to
Q
into a random variable distributed according to
P
.
This method allows gradient information of the density function of
P
to be leveraged, and thus has the potential to avoid degradation issues associated with importance sampling and related methods.

Specifically, the transport-based method seeks to design a
transport map

T\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}

such that
T(Z)
has distribution
P
whenever
Z\sim Q
, which we denote as
P=T_{\sharp}Q
.
Assume
T
is a differentiable bijection so that, by the change-of-variables formula for densities,

\displaystyle p(x)=q(T^{-1}(x))\,\bigl|\det(\nabla T^{-1}(x))\bigr|,

(56)

where
q
and
p
are the density functions of
Q
and
P
, respectively.
Unfortunately, (
56
) depends on
T
in a complicated nonlinear fashion, and practical methods based on (
56
) can be computationally challenging in high dimensions due to the presence of the Jacobian determinant.

This difficulty can be sidestepped by adopting a dynamic viewpoint, treating
T
as the flow map of a continuous-time dynamical system.
Specifically, we assume that a random variable
Z_{0}
drawn from
Q
evolves continuously according to an ordinary differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}Z_{t}=v_{t}(Z_{t}),\qquad t\geq 0,

(57)

where
v\colon[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a continuously differentiable velocity field to be specified.
Starting from
Z_{0}\sim Q
, our goal is to design the time-dependent velocity field
v_{t}
so that the distribution of
Z_{t}
, denoted by
Q_{t}
, converges to the target distribution
P
as
t\to\infty
.

Assume that the ODE (
57
) admits a unique solution for every initialization in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and that the density
q_{t}
of
Q_{t}
exists and is continuously differentiable in
(t,x)
, with

v_{t}
continuously differentiable in
(t,x)
as well.
Under these classical regularity conditions, the evolution of
Q_{t}
is governed by the

continuity equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}q_{t}(x)=-(\nabla\cdot(q_{t}v_{t}))(x).

(58)

Note that the continuity equation can be written in terms of the Langevin Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{Q_{t}}
associated with
q_{t}
(cf.
Definition

3.2
), as

\partial_{t}\log q_{t}(x)=-(\mathcal{T}_{Q_{t}}v_{t})(x)
.

An alternative form of (
58
) is
Liouville’s formula
, which gives the total time derivative of
q_{t}(Z_{t})
when
Z_{t}
evolves according to (
57
):

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log q_{t}(Z_{t})=-(\nabla\!\cdot v_{t})(Z_{t}).

(59)

This follows from the chain rule,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}q_{t}(Z_{t})=\partial_{t}q_{t}(Z_{t})+v_{t}(Z_{t})\cdot\nabla q_{t}(Z_{t})
,
together with the continuity equation (
58
).

22
Gradient Flow of the Kullback–Leibler Divergence

One approach to designing the velocity field
v_{t}
is to follow the gradient of a suitable divergence between the current distribution
Q_{t}
and the target
P
.
The choice of divergence is crucial, as it must lead to a system that is computationally feasible to evaluate or approximate.
It turns out that the Kullback–Leibler divergence
\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})
is a particularly suitable candidate, thanks to the following result, which characterizes its rate of decrease under (
58
) in connection with the Langevin Stein operator.

Theorem 5.1
.

Assume that the differential equation
\dot{Z}_{t}=v_{t}(Z_{t})
admits a unique solution on
[0,T]
for every initialization in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
Q_{t}
denote the law of
Z_{t}
, with density
q_{t}
.
Assume that
v_{t}
and
\log q_{t}
are continuously differentiable on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
P
be a probability measure with a positive and continuously differentiable density
p
, such that
\textnormal{KL}({Q_{0}}\|{P})<\infty
, and

\int_{0}^{T}Q_{t}(|(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t})(Z_{t})|)\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

where
\mathcal{T}_{P}
is the Langevin Stein operator for
P
.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})=-\,Q_{t}(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t}).

(60)

Proof.

Since
Z_{t}
follows law
Q_{t}
, we have

\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})=\mathbb{E}\left[\log q_{t}(Z_{t})-\log p(Z_{t})\right].

Define
R_{t}=\log q_{t}(Z_{t})-\log p(Z_{t})
. Its time derivative is

\displaystyle\dot{R}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log q_{t}(Z_{t})-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log p(Z_{t})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot v_{t}(Z_{t})-v_{t}(Z_{t})\cdot\nabla\log p(Z_{t})

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t}(Z_{t}),

where we used Liouville’s formula (
59
) for the first term,
and the chain rule for the second term,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log p(Z_{t})=\nabla\log p(Z_{t})^{\top}\dot{Z}_{t}=\nabla\log p(Z_{t})^{\top}v_{t}(Z_{t}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})-\textnormal{KL}({Q_{0}}\|{P})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{t}-R_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\dot{R}_{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\dot{R}_{\tau}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{\tau})(Z_{\tau})\right]\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

where we used Fubini–Tonelli’s theorem to exchange the order of integration, assuming

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\lvert(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{\tau})(Z_{\tau})\rvert]\,\mathrm{d}\tau<\infty
.
Differentiating the resulting identity and using continuity of

t\mapsto\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t}(Z_{t})\right]
yields

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})=-\,\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t})(Z_{t})\right],

concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 5.1
.

We can interpret the result in discrete time by Taylor approximation.
For a measure
Q
,
let
Q^{\prime}\triangleq(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon v)_{\sharp}Q
be the law of
Z^{\prime}\triangleq Z+\epsilon v(Z)
given
Z\sim Q
. Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}(\textnormal{KL}({(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon v)_{\sharp}Q\penalty\ }\|{\penalty\ P})-\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P}))

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}\textnormal{KL}({(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon v)_{\sharp}Q\penalty\ }\|{\penalty\ P})\bigg|_{\epsilon=0}

\displaystyle=-Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}v).

Hence, the expectation of Stein operator
Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}v)
quantifies the rate at which Kullback–Leibler divergence
\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})
decreases when we apply an small displacement following vector field
v
to
Q
.

23
Gradient Flow and Stein Discrepancy

Now that we have understood the relationship between the Kullback–Leibler divergence and the Langevin Stein operator, we seek a velocity field
v^{*}
that
efficiently
minimizes the Kullback–Leibler divergence, since this would enable computationally efficient measure transport.
This can be formulated as the optimization problem

v_{t}^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmax}_{v_{t}\in\mathcal{G}_{t}}\left\{-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})\right\},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall t\geq 0,

where
\mathcal{G}_{t}
is a candidate set of velocity fields.
Using the derivative formula for the Kullback–Leibler divergence from
Theorem

5.1
,

\displaystyle v_{t}^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmax}_{v_{t}\in\mathcal{G}_{t}}\;Q_{t}(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall t\geq 0.

(61)

Thus, the maximum decreasing rate of Kullback–Leibler divergence is
Q_{t}(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t}^{*})
which, under appropriate regularity assumptions, coincides with the definition of Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}(Q_{t},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{t})
(c.f.
Definition

4.1
).
Hence, Stein discrepancy can be viewed as the maximal rate of decrease for the Kullback–Leibler divergence under mass transport, and the optimal vector field
v_{t}^{*}
for mass transport is also the optimal element of the Stein set that realizes the Stein discrepancy.

As we show in sequel, the dynamics with
v_{t}^{*}
can be interpreted as a form of gradient flow of the Kullback–Leibler divergence,
\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})
, if the candidate space
\mathcal{G}_{t}
is defined as the tangent space of a geometric structure on the set of probability measures.
Different choices of the candidate velocity set
\mathcal{G}_{t}
allow us to derive different dynamics for mass transport.
This includes Langevin diffusions, which leverage a candidate space
\mathcal{G}_{t}\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(Q_{t})
so that

\mathcal{S}(Q_{t},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{t})
coincides with Fisher divergence (c.f.
Proposition

4.1
);
and Stein variational gradient descent, in which
\mathcal{G}_{t}
is taken to be a ball of a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS), and
\mathcal{S}(Q_{t},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}_{t})
coincides with a kernel Stein discrepancy (KSD) (c.f.
Definition

4.8
).

Remark 5.2
.

Assume
Q_{t}
has reached the equilibrium, having converged to
P
.
The Stein identity (
1
) is now equivalent to

P(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t})=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathrm{KL}(Q_{t}||P)=0,

which reflects the fact that
P
is a stationary point of the Kullback–Leibler divergence
Q\mapsto\mathrm{KL}(Q||P)
.

Geometric Interpretation

One may cast the optimization problem above in a geometric framework as a gradient flow of the Kullback–Leibler divergence functional over the space of probability measures, equipped with a suitable transport metric.
This viewpoint parallels the theory of gradient flows in spaces of distributions, developed for example in
Ambrosio et al. 2005
, although here we only work at a formal and heuristic level.

Assume that the admissible velocity fields form the unit ball of a normed space,

\mathcal{G}_{t}\triangleq\{v:\|v\|_{t}\leq 1\},

where
\|\cdot\|_{t}
is a (possibly time-dependent) norm to be specified later.
For two probability distributions
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, we define a generalized transport cost

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(Q,Q^{\prime})\triangleq\inf_{(q_{t},v_{t})}\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\|v_{t}\|_{t}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{1/2}\;\;\text{s.t.}

(62)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}q_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(v_{t}q_{t}),\qquad Q_{0}=Q,\quad Q_{1}=Q^{\prime}\Bigg\}.

This is a Benamou–Brenier formulation, generalizing the
2
-Wasserstein metric by replacing the Euclidean kinetic energy with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{t}
.

We now consider the gradient flow of
\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})
under the metric
\mathcal{W}
.
Formally, the steepest descent direction at
Q
can be characterized by:

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{Q^{\prime}}\left\{\textnormal{KL}({Q^{\prime}}\|{P})\;:\;\mathcal{W}(Q^{\prime},Q)\leq\epsilon\right\},

(63)

or by its penalized version

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{Q^{\prime}}\left\{\textnormal{KL}({Q^{\prime}}\|{P})+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}\mathcal{W}^{2}(Q^{\prime},Q)\right\},

which is standard in the theory of metric gradient flows.
Plugging the definition of
\mathcal{W}
, the optimization reduces to

\min_{\{v_{t}\}}\left\{\textnormal{KL}({Q^{v}}\|{P})+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\left\|{v_{t}}\right\|_{t}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right\},

To obtain a tractable first-order characterization, consider an infinitesimal perturbation of the form

Q^{\prime}=(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon v)_{\sharp}Q,

where
v
is a smooth vector field and
\epsilon>0
is small.
Such a perturbation corresponds to a path solving the continuity equation with constant velocity
v_{t}\equiv v
, and therefore satisfies

\mathcal{W}(Q^{\prime},Q)\leq\epsilon\|v\|_{t}.

Restricting (
63
) to these perturbations yields the approximation

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{v}\left\{\textnormal{KL}({(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon v)_{\sharp}Q}\|{P})-\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})\;:\;\|v\|_{t}\leq 1\right\}.

(64)

Next, using a first-order Taylor expansion of the Kullback–Leibler divergence along the pushforward map, we obtain

\textnormal{KL}({(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon v)_{\sharp}Q}\|{P})=\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})-\epsilon\,Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}v)+o(\epsilon),

where
\mathcal{T}_{P}
denotes the Langevin Stein operator associated with
P
.
Substituting this expansion into (
64
) and neglecting higher-order terms, we arrive at

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmax}_{v\in\mathcal{G}_{t}}\;Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}v),

which recovers (
61
) as the steepest descent direction of the KL divergence under the transport metric
\mathcal{W}
.

From this geometric perspective, the Stein variational update can be viewed as a metric-dependent gradient flow of the Kullback–Leibler divergence.
Different choices of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{t}
, and hence of the admissible set
\mathcal{G}_{t}
, induce different geometries on the space of probability measures and lead to different dynamics.

24
Langevin Diffusion as a Gradient Flow

Assuming that
Q_{t}
admits a sufficiently regular density
q_{t}
, by Stein’s identity

\displaystyle Q_{t}(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t})

\displaystyle=\int\nabla\cdot v_{t}+v_{t}\cdot\nabla\log p\;\mathrm{d}Q_{t}=\langle\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q_{t},\penalty\ v_{t}\rangle_{L^{2}(Q_{t})}

where
L^{2}(Q_{t})
denote the Lebesgue space (
Example

2.11
).
Let
\mathcal{G}_{t}=\{f\colon\left\|{f}\right\|_{L^{2}(Q_{t})}\leq 1\}
be the unit ball of
L^{2}(Q_{t})
equipped with norm
\left\|{f}\right\|_{L^{2}(Q_{t})}=Q_{t}(\left\|{f}\right\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}
.
The optimal
v_{t}\in L^{2}(Q_{t})
should solve

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmax}_{v_{t}}\;\langle\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q_{t},\penalty\ v_{t}\rangle_{L^{2}(Q_{t})}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \left\|{v_{t}}\right\|_{L^{2}(Q_{t})}\leq 1,

whose solution is
Q_{t}
-a.e. equal to

v_{t}^{*}=\frac{\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q_{t}}{\|\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(Q_{t})}}.

Plugging
v_{t}^{*}
into the continuity equation (
58
) yields

\displaystyle\dot{q}_{t}=-\nabla\cdot((\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q_{t})q_{t})=-\nabla\cdot((\nabla\log p)q_{t})+\Delta q_{t},

(65)

where
\Delta q_{t}\triangleq\nabla\cdot\nabla q_{t}
it the Laplacian operator.

Equation

65
coincides with the Fokker–Planck equation of over-damped Langevin diffusion (c.f.
Example

2.5
)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\nabla\log p(X_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}W_{t}.

This shows that overdamped Langevin dynamics is the gradient flow of Kullback–Leibler divergence under the metric (
62
).
Related, the rate of decrease of the Kullback–Leibler divergence along this flow is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\textnormal{KL}({Q_{t}}\|{P})

\displaystyle=-Q_{t}(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t}^{*})

\displaystyle=-\left\|{\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q_{t}}\right\|_{L^{2}(Q_{t})}^{2}=-\mathrm{FD}(Q_{t}||P),

with the final expression being the Fisher divergence between the current distribution
Q_{t}
and the target
P
(
Definition

4.2
).
This indicates that the decrease in Kullback-Leibler is more rapid when
Q_{t}
is far from
P
in the sense of
\mathrm{FD}(Q_{t}||P)
.

25
Stein Variational Gradient

The problem with the velocity in Langevin diffusion is that it depends on the log density of the evolving density, i.e.
\log q_{t}
, which is not computationally available.
Using Stein’s method we can instead obtain a computationally tractable optimal velocity by taking the candidate space
\mathcal{G}_{t}
to be a unit ball in an reproducing kernel Hilbert space.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{t}=\{v\in\mathcal{H}_{K}\colon||v||_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\leq 1\}
be the unit ball in a vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space with kernel of the form
K(x,y)=k(x,y)I
for some scalar-valued kernel
k:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
(c.f.
Section

8.3
).
For
v_{t}\in\mathcal{G}_{t}
,
by the linearity of the Langevin Stein operator,

\displaystyle Q_{t}(\mathcal{T}_{P}v_{t})=\langle v_{t},v_{t}^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}

where

\displaystyle v_{t}^{*}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\int k(x,\cdot)\nabla_{x}\log p(x)+\nabla_{x}k(x,\cdot)\;\mathrm{d}Q_{t}(x).

(66)

Hence, the optimization problem in (
61
) reduces to

\displaystyle\max_{v_{t}\in\mathcal{G}_{t}}\;\langle v_{t},v_{t}^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}

whose optimal solution is

v_{t}=\frac{v_{t}^{*}}{\|v_{t}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}}.

Thus we have shown that the corresponding flow
(Q_{t})_{t\geq 0}
from (
58
) monotonically decreases the Kullback–Leibler divergence, and the rate of decrease equals the kernel Stein discrepancy (KSD) (c.f.
Definition

4.8
):

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}t}\mathrm{KL}(Q_{t}\penalty\ ||\penalty\ P)=-\operatorname{KSD}_{P}(Q_{t}).

(67)

The velocity field
v_{t}
characterizes the so-called
Stein variational gradient
, which forms the basis for several powerful numerical algorithms as detailed in
Section

28.2
.

Remark 5.3
.

This result suggests a path integration formula of for the Kullback–Leibler divergence;
\mathrm{KL}(Q_{0}||P)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{KSD}_{P}(Q_{t})\,\mathop{\mathrm{d}\!}t,

which can be useful for estimating the Kullback–Leibler divergence or the normalization constant
(
Han and Liu 2017
)
.

Chapter 6
Applications

This Chapter provides a succinct overview of recent and emerging applications of Stein discrepancies across a range of important practical tasks arising in probabilistic inference and learning.

26
Overview

The unique feature of the Stein discrepancies introduced in
Chapter

4
is that they enable the discrepancy between a distribution
P
and an empirical measure

\displaystyle Q_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}},

(68)

to be explicitly computed without tractable access to a probability mass or density function for
P
, when the intractability arises from difficulty in computing the normalization constant.
Here
P
and
Q_{n}
are probability distributions on a common measurable space
\mathcal{X}
, and
Q_{n}
is supported on a finite set of points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
weighted by
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\sum_{i}w_{i}=1
and
w_{i}\geq 0
.
Recall that a Stein discrepancy is defined by a Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
and a Stein set
\mathcal{G}
.
For all of the applications that we are about to discuss there is flexibility in the choice of both
\mathcal{T}_{P}
and
\mathcal{G}
, which enables an opportunity to tailor the Stein discrepancy to the task at hand.
However, to simplify the presentation in this Chapter we will leave these dependencies implicit and adopt the shorthand

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})\triangleq\mathcal{S}(Q_{n},\mathcal{T}_{P},\mathcal{G}).

Stein discrepancies and their related Stein dynamics from
Chapter

5
provide versatile tools that lend themselves naturally to a range of important statistical applications, broadly falling into four categories that will now be described.

Measuring Approximation Quality

(
Section

27
)
Given samples
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and a distribution
P
with density
p(x)\propto\exp(-V(x))
specified up to a normalization constant, the magnitude of
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
can be used to quantify the dissimilarity between the empirical distribution
Q_{n}
of the (uniformly-weighted) samples
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
P
.
As such, Stein discrepancies have emerged as a popular and flexible approach to goodness-of-fit testing, where one wishes to test the null hypothesis that
x_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}P
.
Under the null hypothesis,
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, so one rejects the null if
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})\geq\tau
, where
\tau
is an appropriately chosen threshold.
This method greatly extends traditional goodness-of-fit tests, such as chi-square tests, which only work for simple and low-dimensional distributions.
In parallel, Stein discrepancies have also become a popular tool for measuring the quality of approximations produced by algorithms that aim to sample from a posterior distribution
P
, enabling both the tuning of sampling methods and the comparison of competing methodologies in the Bayesian statistical context.

Algorithms for Particle-Based Approximation

(
Section

28
)
Given a probability distribution
P
specified in terms of a probability mass or density function
p(x)\propto\exp(-V(x))
up to an intractable normalization constant, one can cast the problem of numerically approximating
P
as the optimization problem of finding states
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
and corresponding weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the associated empirical distribution (
68
) minimizes a Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
.
Fixing uniform weights
w_{i}=\frac{1}{n}
, optimization of the particles
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
can be performed using gradient descent or any other suitable numerical optimization method, yielding new families of particle-based algorithms.
On the other hand, the Stein dynamics discussed in
Chapter

5
can be used to perform a version of gradient descent on the Kullback–Leibler divergence, giving rise to an algorithm known as Stein variational gradient descent.
Fixing the particles
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and instead optimizing the weights
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
yields algorithms that are similar in spirit to importance sampling, and can correct for bias if the
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
arose in such a manner other than being sampled from
P
.
Moreover, imposing sparsity on the weights
\{w_{i}\}
yields
thinning
algorithms that select a small subset of
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
to achieve accurate approximation of
P
.
Finally, given a specific function
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
whose expectation with respect to
P
is of interest, one can deduce optimal values for the weights
w_{i}
appearing in a cubature approximation
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}f(x_{i})
, yielding an effective variance reduction technique in situations where the
x_{i}
are randomly sampled.

Training Generative Models

(
Section

29
)
Given a dataset
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, one can seek a suitable generative model from a collection
\{P_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
by searching for appropriate values for the parameter
\theta
across an index set
\Theta
.
For tractable generative models, standard statistical techniques such as maximum likelihood estimation can be used.
However, there are many important examples of generative models that are not tractable; for example,
P_{\theta}
may be specified via a density function
p_{\theta}(x)\propto\exp(-V_{\theta}(x))
up to an intractable normalization constant.
Stein discrepancy and Stein dynamics provide a variety of useful alternative parameter estimation methods in this context.

Gradient Estimation

(
Section

30
)
A common technical challenge encountered in machine learning is that gradients
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{\theta}}[f(X)]
with respect to the parameters
\theta
of a distribution
P_{\theta}
are unavailable analytically and must be approximated.
This challenge arises in many learning problems, including training latent variable models for variational inference and reinforcement learning with policy gradients.
Monte Carlo methods can often be used, but their associated errors can be substantial, negatively affecting performance in the downstream machine learning task.
Stein’s method has given rise to several variance reduction strategies that can be employed in this context.

27
Measuring Approximation Quality

The first application that we consider in detail is the problem of measuring the quality of an empirical approximation to an unnormalized distributional target.
To be precise, suppose that
P
is a distribution of interest, defined on a probability space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}},\lambda_{\mathcal{X}})
, for which a p.d.f.
p(x)
with respect to
\lambda_{\mathcal{X}}
is available up to an intractable normalization constant: i.e.

\displaystyle p(x)\propto\frac{\tilde{p}(x)}{Z}

where
\tilde{p}(x)
is explicitly available but the normalization constant
Z=\int\tilde{p}(x)\;\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
is an intractable integral.
In what follows we consider two distinct scenarios (quantifying the performance of sampling methods in
Section

27.1
, and goodness-of-fit testing in
Section

27.2
), where in each case the task is to determine the quality of an empirical approximation
Q_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
to
P
, where
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
are the states on which
Q_{n}
is supported.

27.1
Quantifying the Performance of Sampling Methods

The problem of quantifying the performance of sampling methods commonly arises in Bayesian statistics, where
P
is a posterior distribution whose density
p(x)\propto\pi(x)\mathcal{L}(x)
is available in unnormalized form as the product of the prior
\pi(x)
and the likelihood
\mathcal{L}(x)
(c.f.
Section

2
).
Here
\tilde{p}(x)
can be identified with the product
\pi(x)\mathcal{L}(x)
and the normalization constant

Z=\int\pi(x)\mathcal{L}(x)\;\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\mathcal{X}}(x)

is recognized as the marginal likelihood.
The task of measuring sample quality is encountered in the form of monitoring the convergence of an extensible
5
5

5

A sampling algorithm is
extensible
if it can in principle produce an infinite sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of states, such that in practice one can truncate this sequence and terminate the algorithm to obtain an approximation
Q_{n}
to
P
once some appropriate stopping criterion is met.
sampling algorithm, when comparing the approximations produced by different (exact and/or approximate) sampling algorithms, and in tuning the hyper-parameters of a particular sampling algorithm.
In each case, Stein discrepancies have been employed as quantitative criteria through which performance can be explicitly measured.

Example 6.1

(Tuning the unadjusted Langevin algorithm)
.

The unadjusted Langevin algorithm for approximate sampling from a target
P
is an Euler–Maruyama discretization
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
of a
P
-invariant overdamped Langevin diffusion (c.f.
Example

2.5
):

X_{t+1}=X_{t}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\nabla\log p(X_{t})+\sqrt{\epsilon}Z_{t}

where
Z_{t}
is a standard normal random variable generated independently from
X_{1},\dots,X_{t}
.

Figure 1
:
Samples generated using the unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA).
Here the target
P
is a Gaussian mixture model with density
p
and
\epsilon
denotes the step size parameter of ULA.
Contours of
\log p
are depicted.

Here
\epsilon>0
is a
step size
parameter that must be selected.
Suppose that we have a computational budget which permits computing
(X_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
with
n=1,000
; we wish to select
\epsilon
for which the empirical distribution
Q_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{t}}
is likely to provide the best approximation to
P
.
To be precise, we suppose that we are interested in approximating the mean and variance of each component of the distribution
P
in
Figure

1
using empirical averages from unadjusted Langevin algorithm.
Taking
\epsilon
too small prevents the stochastic process from effectively exploring the high-probability regions of
P
(
Figure

1
; left panel).
On the other hand, taking
\epsilon
too big introduces bias into the distribution of the
X_{t}
due to the first order discretization of the stochastic differential equation, and can ultimately cause explosive behavior in unadjusted Langevin algorithm (
Figure

1
; right panel).
Unfortunately, an appropriate value of
\epsilon
(
Figure

1
; middle panel) will be unknown in general due to the intractable nature of the target.
A solution is provided by Stein discrepancy, which enables the quality of the approximation
Q_{n}
to be explicitly measured, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
.
Indeed, the mean square error associated with the empirical estimates of the means and variances of
P
(which cannot be computed in general) is minimized around
\epsilon\approx 10^{-2}
(
Figure

2
; left panel), while the kernel Stein discrepancy (
KSD
) (which can be computed) is also minimized around
\epsilon\approx 10^{-2}
(
Figure

2
; right panel).

Figure 2
:
Tuning the unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA).
The left panel presents the mean square error associated with the empirical estimates for the means and variances of
P
(which cannot be computed in general), while the right panel presents the kernel Stein discrepancy (
KSD
) (which can be computed).
Here we used the inverse multi-quadric kernel (
Example

2.13
) with exponent
\beta=1
and bandwidth
\ell=0.01
.
The experiment was repeated 10 times and means and standard errors are reported.

A related setting concerns so-called
intractable
probabilistic models, popular in statistical physics, which are specified in terms of relative probabilities
p(x)/p(y)
to circumvent a challenging normalization constant.
Since here exact simulation from
P
is often infeasible, a range of sampling algorithms have been developed
(
Newman and Barkema 1999
, see e.g.)
and their assessment can proceed using Stein discrepancy as just described.

27.2
Goodness-of-Fit Testing

The problem of measuring sample quality is also encountered in goodness-of-fit testing for statistical modes specified up to an intractable normalization constant.
That is, given a dataset
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and a probability distribution
P
with p.d.f. of the form
p(x)\propto\exp(-V(x))
, where the potential
V(x)
is explicitly provided, we seek to test the null hypothesis that
x_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}P
.
Any Stein discrepancy that separates distributions, meaning that
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q)=0
if and only if
Q=P
, provides a computable statistic
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
on which to conduct such a test.
If the null hypothesis holds then we might expect
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
and we would therefore want to reject the null hypothesis if
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
is not small.
In practice, the sampling distribution of the Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
under the null hypothesis is usually unknown, but can be approximated using a bootstrap method.
The main considerations in selecting a Stein discrepancy here are that it can be rapidly computed (to facilitate the bootstrap resampling step) and that, in the case of the null hypothesis being incorrect, it has sufficient power to distinguish between
P
and the true mechanism that gives rise to the dataset.

For ease of computability, we employ a kernel Stein discrepancy (KSD) (
Section

18
) with Stein kernel
k_{p}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
Assuming that
\int k_{p}(x,x)\mathrm{d}P(x)<\infty
, then under the null hypothesis

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{i})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\int k_{p}(x,x)\mathrm{d}P(x)\rightarrow 0,

where the last line follows since
x_{i}\sim P
are independent under the null and
\int k_{p}(\cdot,x)\;\mathrm{d}P(x)=0
since
k_{p}
is a Stein kernel.
Thus we may construct a goodness-of-fit test of size
\alpha
using the statistic
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
and rejecting the null hypothesis when
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})>\tau
, where the threshold
\tau
is selected such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})\leq\tau)=1-\alpha
.
Since one typically does not have the ability to simulate from
P
(due to the intractable normalizing constant), numerically calculating the
1-\alpha
quantile of the sampling distribution of
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})
under the null hypothesis is not straightforward.
An approach that has become popular in the literature
(
Liu et al. 2016
;
Chwialkowski et al. 2016
)
is to use the
wild bootstrap

(
Shao 2010
;
Fromont et al. 2012
;
Leucht and Neumann 2013
)
, which introduces additional independent Rademacher random variables
\{\epsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
(i.e. each
\epsilon_{i}
is uniform on
\{-1,1\}
) and notes that the distribution of
n\hat{D}_{P}(Q_{n})^{2}
approaches that of
n\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})^{2}
under the null, where

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{P}(Q_{n})^{2}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j})

(69)

and we consider the
n\rightarrow\infty
limit.
Generating multiple instantiations of the Rademacher random variables and re-computing (
69
), an empirical approximation to the sampling distribution of
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q)
under the null is obtained, from which an appropriate threshold
\tau
can be extracted.

Example 6.2
.

Consider testing the goodness-of-fit of a Gauss–Bernoulli restricted Boltzmann machine to a dataset
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This model has latent variables
h\in\{-1,1\}^{d^{\prime}}
and joint density of the form

\displaystyle p(x,h)=\frac{1}{Z}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}Bh+b^{\top}x+c^{\top}h+\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}x\right)

where
Z
is the appropriate normalizing constant.
The marginal distribution
P
for the observable
x
has density

\displaystyle p(x)=\sum_{h\in\{-1,1\}^{d^{\prime}}}p(x,h),

but this can be intractable due to the dependence on
Z
.
On the other hand, the score function admits a closed form

\displaystyle\nabla\log p(x)=b-x+\frac{1}{2}B\tanh\left(\frac{1}{2}B^{\top}x+c\right),

so we can exploit Stein discrepancy to construct a goodness-of-fit test.

For illustration we follow the setting of
Liu et al. 2016
, who took
d=50
,
d^{\prime}=10
,
n=100
, sampled entries of
b
and
c
from a standard Gaussian, and sampled the entries of
B
uniformly from
\{-1,1\}
.
