Our experiments used the inverse multi quadric kernel
k
(
Example

2.13
) with length scale
\ell>0
to construct the Stein kernel
k_{P}
.
Data were generated either from the true model, or from a perturbation of the true model where Gaussian noise of variance
\sigma^{2}
was added to the entries of
B
.

Figure

3
shows the null distribution of the kernel Stein discrepancy test statistic as approximated using the wild bootstrap, together with the actual value of the kernel Stein discrepancy test statistic, and the rejection threshold
\tau
corresponding to
\alpha=0.05
.
In the case
\sigma=0
, where the model is correct, the actual kernel Stein discrepancy value falls into the central region of the null and the test does not reject, while in the case
\sigma=0.1
the actual kernel Stein discrepancy falls far into the tail of the null and the null hypothesis is rejected.

Figure 3
:
Goodness-of-fit testing with kernel Stein discrepancy.
Here we plot the null distribution of the kernel Stein discrepancy test statistic as approximated using the wild bootstrap (blue), the actual value of the kernel Stein discrepancy test statistic (black), and the values for which the null would be rejected (shaded).
The amount of model misspecification is denoted with
\sigma
, so that when
\sigma=0
the model is correct.
The inverse multi-quadric kernel with length scale
\ell
was used.

A natural question is how to select a Stein kernel
k_{p}
so that the power of the test (i.e. the probability of detecting departures from the null) is maximized.
For our simple setting,
Figure

4
plots the test power for three different kernel length scale
\ell
, in each case as a function of the amount of model misspecification
\sigma
.
Interestingly, it seems that the power of this test is almost independent of
\ell
; all choices perform comparably well.

Figure 4
:
Goodness-of-fit testing with kernel Stein discrepancy.
Here we plot the test power (i.e. the probability of rejecting the null) as a function of both the amount of model misspecification
\sigma
and the kernel length scale
\ell
.
Means and standard errors over 100 experiments are reported.

In general, selecting an appropriate Stein kernel might involve techniques such as data-splitting and a tractable approximation to the power of the test, suitable for being optimized over a training split of the dataset.
As a starting point, the reader is referred to
Jitkrittum et al. 2017
, where an explicit approximation to the test power is presented.
Several authors have considered additional strategies to increase test power, such as aggregating several tests
(
Schrab et al. 2022
)
, carefully selecting the Stein operator
(
Liu et al. 2023
)
, and spectral regularisation
(
Hagrass et al. 2026
)
.
At the same time, the computational cost of testing is being reduced
(
Jitkrittum et al. 2017
;
Huggins and Mackey 2018
)
, and additional functionalities such as robust testing
(
Liu and Briol 2025
)
, online testing
(
Martinez-Taboada and Ramdas 2025
)
, and relative goodness-of-fit testing for latent variable models
(
Kanagawa et al. 2023
)
are being developed.

28
Algorithms for Particle-Based Approximation

In the previous section we assumed the role of a passive observer, measuring the quality of a sample that has already been provided.
Now our attention turns to the question of how to actively
construct
discrete approximations to a distributional target
P
.
Again, we are motivated by applications such as those arising in Bayesian statistics, where one has access to
P
only through a density function that is unnormalized.
For demonstration purposes we will repeatedly consider the two-dimensional
Rosenbrock
target

\displaystyle p(x,y)\propto\exp\left(-x^{2}-3(y-x^{2})^{2}\right).

(70)

First, in
Sections

28.1
and
28.2
we present a selection of algorithms that aim to select states
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
such that the associated (uniformly-weighted) empirical measure is an accurate approximation to the target.
In
Section

28.3
we consider the task of assigning weights to a given sample, to produce a weighted empirical measure that may represent a more faithful approximation to the target.
This is complimented in
Section

28.4
, where we consider sparse approximation schemes that can facilitate both compression and improvement of sample quality at a reduced computational cost.
The situation is then specialized to that of approximating a single expected quantity of interest in
Section

28.5
.

28.1
Sequential Algorithms

First we consider sequential, or
extensible
algorithms, meaning that in principle an infinite sequence
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is produced.
In practice such algorithms are terminated after a finite number
n
of iterations, and the first
n
particles
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are taken to form an empirical approximation
Q_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
to the distributional target
P
.
The main advantage of extensible algorithms is that they may be run for as long as required until a sufficiently accurate approximation to
P
is obtained.

Stein Points

A natural algorithm to consider is sequential greedy minimization of Stein discrepancy, which selects the
n
th particle
x_{n}
in a manner that depends on the previously selected particles
x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}
according to

\displaystyle x_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\;\mathcal{S}_{P}\left(\frac{1}{n}\delta_{x}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{x_{i}}\right)

(71)

where as usual
P
is the distributional target, defined on
\mathcal{X}
.
The particles
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
selected in this manner are termed
Stein Points
.
The optimization problem in (
71
) can in practice rarely be analytically solved, and numerical methods are required.

Chen et al. 2018b
analyzed the accuracy of numerical optimization that would be required at each iteration to ensure convergence
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n})\rightarrow 0
in the case of the kernel Stein discrepancy.
For Stein discrepancies that control convergence, we can then conclude that
Q_{n}
converges to
P
.
Unfortunately the use of generic numerical optimization techniques introduces a curse of dimension in
d=\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{X})
.
To alleviate the curse of dimension,
Chen et al. 2019b
proposed
Stein Point MCMC
which instead solves

\displaystyle x_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{x\in\{y_{1}^{(n)},\dots,y_{m_{n}}^{(n)}\}}\;\mathcal{S}_{P}\left(\frac{1}{n}\delta_{x}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{x_{i}}\right)

(72)

where
(y_{i}^{(n)})_{i=1}^{m_{n}}
is a
P
-invariant Markov chain of length
m_{n}
, initialized at
y_{1}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{X}
.
See the left panel of
Figure

5
.
Provided that the mixing of the Markov chain is sufficiently rapid and
m_{n}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the consistency of (
72
) was established under quite general conditions.
For example, it is possible to let the initial state
y_{1}^{(n)}
depend on the history of selected points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}
, and a performant choice is the so-called
most influential
point
x_{i}
for which the Stein discrepancy with
x_{i}
removed, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{P}\left(\frac{1}{n-2}\sum_{j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}\setminus\{i\}}\delta_{x_{j}}\right),

is maximized.
The intuition for this choice is that
x_{i}
is critical to the approximation quality of
Q_{n-1}
and therefore adding another state
x_{n}
in a neighborhood of
x_{i}
may be beneficial.

Figure 5:
Stein points (left) and Frank–Wolfe–Stein points (right) were used to approximate the Rosenbrock target (
70
) (black shaded).

Frank–Wolfe–Stein Points

Consider now the Stein Points algorithm in the specific case of the kernel Stein discrepancy, so that the update (
71
) can be explicitly written in terms of the Stein reproducing kernel
k_{p}
(e.g. as in (
46
) for the Langevin KSD) as

\displaystyle x_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\;\frac{k_{p}(x,x)}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{p}(x_{i},x)

(73)

whose evaluation cost is seen to be
O(n)
.
The first term in (
73
) can be interpreted as a regulariser, and indeed one can also consider solving an unregularised version of this problem, to obtain a different but related sequence of points

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{n}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{p}(\tilde{x}_{i},x).

(74)

Chen et al. 2018b
noticed that sequence
(\tilde{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
produced by solving the unregularised problem in (
74
) is exactly the sequence one would obtain by applying the
Frank–Wolfe
optimization method to Stein discrepancy minimization, as has been studied in the literature on kernel methods
(
Chen et al. 2010b
;
Bach et al. 2012
, e.g.)
where the name
herding
is also used.
6
6

6

Another related technique,
MMD-FW

(
Futami et al. 2019
)
, uses Frank–Wolfe and the Langevin Stein operator to minimize an associated kernel MMD rather than the Stein discrepancy directly.

See the right panel of
Figure

5
.
This perspective suggests a variety of techniques from the Frank–Wolfe literature that could be used to improve approximation accuracy, such as additionally allowing the removal of sub-optimal states that were previously selected, or the use of weighted approximation (we will discuss weighted approximation in
Section

28.3
); see
Bomze et al. 2024
for background.

Perhaps the main consideration in choosing a Stein discrepancy in this context is that the convergence control properties of the Stein discrepancy should reflect the application in which the output of a sampling algorithm is to be used.
For example, if samples are to be used to approximate posterior expectations, then a Stein discrepancy that controls the convergence of such expectations should be used (c.f.
Section

18.4
).]

28.2
Batch Algorithms

An extensible algorithm cannot produce highly accurate approximations for all the values of
n
at which it can be terminated
(
Owen 2016
)
.
This can be seen intuitively through
symmetry-breaking
; suppose one wished to approximate a Gaussian
P=N(0,1)
using
n
particles.
For
n=1
, it is natural to select
x_{1}=0
at the central point, but then we are immediately forced to make a difficult choice
x_{2}
; either we also select
x_{2}=0
, in which case we are not accurately capturing the spread of
P
, or we are forced to break the symmetry of our approximation (even though
P
is symmetric).
Batch algorithms seek to avoid this predicament by specifying the number
n
of particles at the outset.
This allows for improved approximation for a comparable number
n
of particles compared to extensible algorithms, but means that if further accuracy is desired then (at least in principle) the algorithm must be re-run from scratch after
n
is increased.
Two illustrative batch algorithms will now be discussed:

Stein Discrepancy Descent

One natural construction of a batch algorithm starts with the so-called
Wasserstein gradient flow
of the Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q)
, which is in effect a form of continuous-time gradient descent with respect to the
Q
argument, producing a measure-valued trajectory
(Q^{(t)})_{t\geq 0}
where each
Q^{(t)}
is a probability distribution on
\mathcal{X}
.
Intuitively, at least, the gradient flow
Q^{(t)}
should converge to
P
under appropriate regularity conditions as
t\rightarrow\infty
.
For numerical purposes, the measure
Q^{(t)}
is approximated by a (uniformly-weighted) empirical distribution
Q_{n}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}^{(t)}}
whose support points
x_{i}^{(t)}
are now
t
-dependent.
This approach was considered in
Korba et al. 2021
, who derived the following numerical scheme based on gradient descent of the (squared) kernel Stein discrepancy:

\displaystyle x_{i}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=x_{i}^{(t)}-\frac{\epsilon}{n^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\nabla_{2}k_{p}(x_{j}^{(t)},x_{i}^{(t)})

where
\epsilon>0
is a
learning rate
to be specified.
It is also possible to consider the use of higher-order numerical optimization techniques, such as L-BFGS; we refer the reader to
Korba et al. 2021
.
Stein discrepancy descent can produce compact representations of the target
P
.
However, this comes at the expense of requiring second-order derivatives of the log-density of
P
.
An alternative strategy, which requires only first-order derivatives, is considered next.

Stein Variational Gradient Descent

The application of gradient descent to Stein discrepancy led to second-order derivatives of the log-density of
P
because the Stein discrepancy itself involved first-order derivatives of
P
.
One solution is to consider gradient descent on a different objective; in particular it is natural to consider the Kullback–Leibler divergence (
Definition

2.26
).

Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and consider a map
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form
T(x)=x+\epsilon g(x)
where
\epsilon\ll 1
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
From
Theorem

5.1
in
Chapter

5
we know that

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}\textnormal{KL}({T_{\#}Q}\|{P})\right|_{\epsilon=0}=Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)

(75)

where
\mathcal{T}_{P}
is the Langevin Stein operator (
Definition

3.2
), and we therefore assume that
g
is regular enough to belong to the domain of this Stein operator (cf.
Proposition

3.1
).
Given a set
\mathcal{G}
of candidates for the function
g
, a direction of steepest descent corresponds to selecting

\displaystyle g^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmax}_{g\in\mathcal{G}}Q(\mathcal{T}_{P}g).

(76)

A computationally attractive choice of set
\mathcal{G}
is the unit ball of a vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(K)
, for which the objective in (
76
) is recognized as a kernel Stein discrepancy (
Definition

4.8
).
Indeed, in this case

\displaystyle g_{P,Q}^{*}(y)=\int\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}K(x,y)\;\mathrm{d}Q(x)

(77)

can be explicitly calculated.
These calculations suggest a practical algorithm, where
Q
is replaced by a discrete distribution
Q_{n}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}^{(t)}}
and the locations of the support points
x_{i}^{(t)}
are updated in a time
t
-dependent manner following the direction of steepest descent:

\displaystyle x_{i}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=x_{i}^{(t)}+\epsilon g_{P,Q_{n}^{(t)}}^{*}(x_{i}^{(t)})

(78)

For the special case where the matrix-valued kernel
K
takes the form
K(x,y)=k(x,y)I
, the explicit form of the direction of steepest descent is

\displaystyle g_{P,Q_{n}^{(t)}}^{*}(x_{i}^{(t)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\nabla\log p)(x_{j}^{(t)})k(x_{j}^{(t)},x_{i}^{(t)})+\nabla_{1}k(x_{j}^{(t)},x_{i}^{(t)})

(79)

and this algorithm is known as Stein variational gradient descent (SVGD)
(
Liu and Wang 2016
)
.
(The case of a general matrix-valued kernel is discussed in
Zhuo et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2017
.)

For any reasonable kernel, such as the Gaussian kernel with fixed length scale
\ell
, the sequence of discrete distributions
Q_{n}^{(t)}
converges to an approximation of
P
, as illustrated in
Figure

6
(left).
In fact, with the right choice of initialization, step size, and step count
t_{n}
, the SVGD approximation
Q_{n}^{(t_{n})}
is known to converge to
P
in KSD
(
Gorham et al. 2020
;
Shi and Mackey 2023
)
at a
O(1/\sqrt{n})
rate
(
Banerjee et al. 2025
)
.
In practice, the kernel length scale
\ell
is usually made
t
-dependent, and a default setting is to take
\ell
equal to the median pairwise distances between the
\{x_{i}^{(t)}\}_{i=1}^{n}
; see
Figure

6
(right).

Figure 6:
Stein variational gradient descent with fixed kernel length scale
\ell
(left) and adaptively selected kernel length scale
\ell
(right) were used to approximate the Rosenbrock target (
70
) (black shaded).

The two terms in
(
79
) plays intuitive roles:
The term with the gradient
(\nabla\log p)(x)

pushes the particles towards
the high probability regions of
P
,
while the term with
\nabla_{1}k(x,x_{i})

serves as a repulsive force to enforce
a degree of diversity between the particles.
If there is no repulsive force,
or when there is only a single particle
(and the kernel satisfies
\nabla_{1}k(x,x^{\prime})=0
for
x=x^{\prime}
),
the solution would collapse to the local optima of the density for
P
, reducing to the maximum a posteriori problem.
Therefore, by using different particle sizes, Stein variational gradient descent provides an interpolation between maximum a posteriori to a full particle-based approximation, enabling an efficient trade-off between accuracy and computation cost.

Remark 6.1

(Well-definedness of Stein variational gradient descent for empirical measures)
.

Stein variational gradient descent can be viewed as iteratively updating the empirical particle measure
Q_{n}^{(t)}
in order to minimize
\textnormal{KL}({Q_{n}^{(t)}}\|{P})
. A crucial but subtle point here is that
\textnormal{KL}({Q_{n}^{(t)}}\|{P})
is technically infinite (or undefined) because the empirical measure
Q_{n}^{(t)}
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
P
; more specifically,

\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[\log q(X)-\log p(X)]

depends on the density
q
of measure
Q
, and is not properly defined when
Q
is an empirical measure.

A key observation is that
differences
of KL divergences under an invertible transport
remain finite and are computable from expectations under
Q
, even when
Q
is empirical.
Let
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism. Then

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}({T_{\#}Q}\|{P})-\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ =\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{T^{-1}_{\#}P})-\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})

(80)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ =-\,\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}\!\left[\log p(T(X))+\log\bigl|\det\nabla T(X)\bigr|-\log p(X)\right],

(81)

where
T^{-1}_{\#}P
denotes the pushforward of
P
by
T^{-1}
, whose density is

x\mapsto p(T(x))\,\bigl|\det\nabla T(x)\bigr|.

We used in (
80
) the invariance of KL divergence
under simultaneous invertible transformations, namely

\textnormal{KL}({T_{\#}Q}\|{T_{\#}P})=\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P}).

Note that the right-hand side of (
81
) depends on
Q
only through the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[\cdot]
,
so it remains well-defined for empirical
Q
.

Moreover, when
T(x)=x+\epsilon g(x)
with small
\epsilon
, the first-order expansion of
(
81
) recovers the Stein variational gradient descent objective decrease formula (e.g. (
75
)),
whose right-hand side also depends on
Q
only through
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}
.
This is why one can derive the Stein variational gradient descent particle update by replacing
Q
with the empirical
particle measure.

Remark 6.2

(Comparison to Stein Discrepancy Descent)
.

Stein discrepancy descent and Stein variational gradient descent are closely related.
To draw an analogy, if we are interested in minimizing a convex function
f(x)
over a Euclidean space
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we may attempt to minimize either
f(x)
or
\|\nabla f(x)\|
; minimizing either objective leads to the same result.
Accordingly, Stein discrepancy descent aims to minimize Stein discrepancy, which is akin to minimizing the gradient of the Kullback–Leibler divergence (
Theorem

5.1
), while in Stein variational gradient descent it is the Kullback–Leibler divergence itself which is minimized.

Since its introduction in
Liu and Wang 2016
, Stein variational gradient descent has been extended and improved in various ways.
A non-exclusive list of examples include: amortized Stein variational gradient descent
(
Feng et al. 2017
;
Wang and Liu 2016
;
Liu and Wang 2017
)
that learns neural samplers instead of particle approximation; gradient-free Stein variational gradient descent
(
Han and Liu 2018
)
which requires no gradient information of the target distribution
P
;
graphical Stein variational gradient descent
(
Wang et al. 2017
;
Zhuo et al. 2018
)
and matrix-kernel Stein variational gradient descent
(
Wang et al. 2019
)
which incorporate structured information in kernel to improve the performance in high dimensions;
Stein variational gradient descent on Riemannian manifolds
(
Liu and Zhu 2018
)
and Newton variants of Stein variational gradient descent
(
Detommaso et al. 2018
;
Chen et al. 2019a
)
;
stochastic variants that speed up by sub-sampling the particles
(
Li et al. 2020
;
Gorham et al. 2020
)
;
quantile Stein variational gradient descent that minimize a quantile loss for increased robustness
(
Gong et al. 2019
)
;
nonlinear Stein variational gradient descent that minimizes more general nonlinear loss functions beyond KL divergence
(
Wang and Liu 2019
)
;
Stein variational importance sampling
(
Han and Liu 2017
)
; and a
general particle optimization framework
(
Chen et al. 2018a
)
.

28.3
Stein Importance Sampling

Up to this point we have considered approximation using particles that are uniformly weighted.
An appealing feature of Stein discrepancies is that they enable a tractable solution to the
optimal weighted approximation
problem where, given
fixed
states
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
(which may have been obtained by any of the aforementioned algorithms in this Section), we seek a weighted measure

\displaystyle Q_{n}^{\star}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{\star}\delta(x_{i}),\qquad w^{\star}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{w\geq 0,\;1^{\top}w=1}\mathcal{S}_{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta(x_{i})\right)

for which the Stein discrepancy between
Q_{n}^{\star}
and
P
is minimized.
Indeed, consider the kernel Stein discrepancy from
Section

18
with Stein kernel
k_{p}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta(x_{i})\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j}),

the optimal weights
w^{\star}=(w_{1}^{\star},\dots,w_{n}^{\star})^{\top}
are then the solution to the linearly-constrained quadratic program

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\;w^{\top}K_{p}w\qquad\text{s.t.}\qquad w\geq 0,\;1^{\top}w=1

(82)

where
[K_{p}]_{i,j}=k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j})
.
The constraints appearing in (
82
) ensure that
Q_{n}^{\star}
is a probability distribution on
\mathcal{X}
.
This program does not admit a closed-form solution, but can be numerically solved.

This approach was first introduced in
Liu and Lee 2017
where it was called
black-box importance sampling
, since in contrast to traditional importance sampling (which samples
x_{i}\sim\pi
and assigns
\pi
-dependent weights
\tilde{w}_{i}\propto p(x_{i})/\pi(x_{i})
), knowledge of how the states
x_{i}
were generated is not required in (
82
).
It has seen success in Bayesian statistics, where
P
is a posterior distribution that is sampled using a (possibly biased) Markov chain Monte Carlo method; here Stein importance sampling can mitigate systematic biases in an input sample, for example, due to burn-in, tempering, approximate Markov chain Monte Carlo, or other off-target sampling.
Indeed, under appropriate conditions on the input sample, the Stein importance sampling approximation
Q_{n}^{\star}
is consistent in the sense that
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n}^{\star})\rightarrow 0
almost surely
(
Riabiz et al. 2022
, Thm. 3)
with
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n}^{\star})=O(1/\sqrt{n})
in probability
(
Hodgkinson et al. 2020
;
Li et al. 2024
, Thm. 1)
. In fact, under more stringent conditions, Stein importance sampling converges to
P
more quickly than an i.i.d. sample from
P
:
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n}^{\star})^{2}]=o(1/n)

(
Li et al. 2024
, Thm. 2)
.

The algorithm is illustrated in
Figure

7
(left), where we first sample states
x_{i}
independently from the standard bivariate normal distribution and then assign a weight to each state in order to approximate the Rosenbrock target in (
70
).
Compared to standard importance sampling, the weights assigned in Stein importance sampling are dependent; sampling
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
too close together due to chance is mitigated in Stein importance sampling, but not in standard importance sampling.
The right hand panel of
Figure

7
illustrates the behavior of Stein importance sampling with the positivity constraint removed; it is interesting to see that negative weights are active in enabling improved approximation in the sense of kernel Stein discrepancy.
This observation will become relevant in
Section

28.5
.

Figure 7:
Stein importance sampling with positive weights (left) and unconstrained weights (right) were used to approximate the Rosenbrock target (
70
) (black shaded). The size of each marker is proportional to the weight that the associated state is assigned; blue circles indicate a positive weight and red triangles indicate a negative weight.

Encouraged by the performance of Stein importance sampling in
Figure

7
, we can then ask which states
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
minimize the approximation error
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n}^{\star})
.
This is a challenging and open mathematical problem.
Even if we suppose that the states are drawn independently from a distribution
\pi(x)
, then an optimal choice of
\pi
is likely to be
n
-dependent.
As a heuristic, based on analysis of self-normalised importance sampling,
Wang et al. 2023
proposed to take
\pi(x)\propto p(x)\sqrt{k_{p}(x,x)}
.
For the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy with base kernel
k(x,y)=\phi(x-y)
, we have that
k_{p}(x,x)=-\Delta\phi(0)+\phi(0)\|\nabla\log p(x)\|^{2}
which typically increases in the
\|x\|\rightarrow\infty
tail.
This choice of
\pi
therefore has the potential to introduce over-dispersion relative to
P
and to place greater emphasis on the areas of
P
with the highest magnitude of gradient.

28.4
Sparse Approximation

Despite the elegance of Stein importance sampling, the computational burden of solving (
82
) can be considerable when the number
n
of samples is large; roughly speaking the cost is
O(n^{3})
.
Unfortunately
n
is often large for algorithms based on laws of large numbers, such as Markov chain Monte Carlo.
One can sub-sample a smaller number of states
m\ll n
at regular intervals from the Markov chain Monte Carlo output (this is usually called
thinning
), but then the approximation quality of the sub-samples may be poor in general.
It is therefore natural to seek instead an
optimal
subset of size
m
from the Markov chain Monte Carlo output.

This issue is closely related to that of optimal weighted approximation in
Section

28.3
, in that we can seek weight vectors for which at most
m\ll n
entries are non-zero.
This observation has motivated a range of sparse approximation techniques, which aim to iteratively construct an approximation of the form
Q_{n,m}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta(y_{i})
, where each
y_{i}
is an element from a list of candidate states
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, which could be Markov chain Monte Carlo output.
The canonical example is the greedy algorithm which, at iteration
j
, selects a state

\displaystyle y_{j}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{y\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}}\mathcal{S}_{P}\left(\frac{1}{j}\delta(y)+\frac{1}{j}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\delta(y_{i})\right)

(83)

for which the Stein discrepancy is minimized.
In the context of kernel Stein discrepancy, the greedy algorithm (
83
) is called
Stein Thinning
and has computational cost
O(mn)
if implemented with appropriate sufficient statistics
(
Li et al. 2024
, App. D.1)
.
Furthermore, under appropriate assumptions, the sparse approximation converges to the optimally weighted approximation
Q_{n}^{\star}
as
m\rightarrow\infty
with
n
fixed, since

\displaystyle\smash{\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n,m})^{2}\leq\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n}^{\star})}^{2}+\frac{\max_{i}k_{p}(x_{i},x_{i})(1+\log(m))}{m}.

(84)

Hence, Stein Thinning performs both bias correction and compression, yielding a sparse output nearly as accurate as the best simplex-weighted input.
See
Riabiz et al. 2022
for full details and
Teymur et al. 2021
where non-myopic and mini-batch extensions of the greedy algorithm are also considered.

Sparse approximation is particularly valuable in the context of predictive modeling, where one needs to integrate out posterior uncertainty regarding model parameters for the purpose of probabilistic prediction.
For sophisticated predictive models, such as those based on detailed mechanistic descriptions of physical processes, there can be a considerable computational cost associated with forward simulation from a model.
Then the ability to produce a sparse approximation of the posterior distribution
P
translates into ability to control the overall simulation cost.

One downside of the guarantee (
84
) is that it requires
m=\Omega(n)
sample points to match the usual
O(1/\sqrt{n})
convergence rate of
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n}^{\star})
. Fortunately, a number of extensions have been developed to recover the standard
O(1/\sqrt{n})
convergence rate with substantially fewer sample points. For example, the
Stein Kernel Thinning
algorithm of
Li et al. 2024
can deliver
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q_{n,m})=O(1/\sqrt{n})
error with
m=\sqrt{n}\operatorname{polylog}(n)
points, while, by using a weighted approximation, the
Stein Recombination
and
Stein Cholesky
of
Li et al. 2024
can each match this error with only
m=\operatorname{polylog}(n)
points.

28.5
Integral Approximation

Often one is interested in approximating a posterior
P
only as a stepping-stone to calculating posterior expectations of interest.
Indeed, given a (possibly weighted) empirical approximation
Q_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
to
P
, one can construct a corresponding approximation

\displaystyle\int f\;\mathrm{d}P\approx\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}f(x_{i})

(85)

to posterior expectations of interest.
However, in this two-step approach the weights
w_{i}
and states
x_{i}
have been selected in a manner that is agnostic to the function
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
It is therefore natural to ask whether one can do better by allowing either the weights, states, or both to be
f
-dependent.

Integration via Solution of the Stein Equation

One approach to improvement, outlined in the early work of
Stein 1986
, is to choose a Stein operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}
and Stein set
\mathcal{G}
and to attempt to solve the
Stein equation

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=c+(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(x)

(86)

for a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
, an element
g\in\mathcal{G}
, and
P
-almost-all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
Indeed, from the definition of a Stein operator we would then have
c=\int f\;\mathrm{d}P
being precisely the integral of interest.
Through the application of numerical methods to (
86
), one can arrive at
f
-dependent weights
w_{i}
and states
x_{i}
for approximation as in (
85
), as will now be demonstrated.

For the purposes of illustration, take
\mathcal{G}
to be the unit ball in a vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(K)
, and consider the minimal norm interpolant

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},\;g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\|g^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\quad\text{such that}\quad f(x_{i})=c^{\prime}+(\mathcal{T}_{P}g^{\prime})(x_{i}),\quad\forall i

(87)

at fixed
collocation nodes

\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Assuming distinct collocation nodes and
K
being positive definite, the value
c_{n}(f)
of
c^{\prime}
that solves (
87
) can be analytically calculated:

\displaystyle c_{n}(f)=\frac{1_{n}^{\top}K_{p}^{-1}f_{n}}{1_{n}^{\top}K_{p}^{-1}1_{n}}

(88)

where
[K_{p}]_{i,j}=k_{p}(x_{i},x_{j})
and
k_{p}
is the Stein kernel (
45
),
1_{n}
denotes a column vector of ones of length
n
, and
[f_{n}]_{i}=f(x_{i})
.

The
control functionals
estimator (
88
) corresponds to a weighted approximation of the form (
85
) where the weights are
w\propto K_{p}^{-1}1_{n}
, normalized such that
1_{n}^{\top}w=1
.
Importantly, this non-uniform weighting enables higher-fidelity estimation of the integral
\int f\;\mathrm{d}P
. For example, for suitable
f
,
\mathcal{T}_{P}
, and
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
drawn independently from
P
, the estimator (
88
) enjoys

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int f\;\mathrm{d}P-c_{n}(f)\right)^{2}\right]=O(n^{-7/6})

(89)

mean squared error
(
Oates et al. 2017
, Thm. 2)
, a strict improvement over the
\Theta(n^{-1})
mean squared error of standard Monte Carlo integration.
The weights
w
can also be understood as a relaxed solution to the Stein importance sampling problem in (
82
) with the positivity constraint removed; cf. the right panel of
Figure

7
.

The weights we have just derived are
f
-independent and the states were simply fixed; how can we take the specific function of interest
f
into account?
The answer comes through
f
-dependent selection of the kernel
K
.
Numerous practical methods for kernel choice are available, such as cross-validation, but in an attempt to give insight we consider here the error bound

\displaystyle\left|\int f\;\mathrm{d}P-c_{n}(f)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\left(\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}f=c+\mathcal{T}_{P}g\\
c\in\mathbb{R},\;g\in\mathcal{G}\end{subarray}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\right)}_{\text{(i)}}\underbrace{\mathcal{S}_{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\right)}_{\text{(ii)}}

that decomposes integration error into the product of (i) a term that depends on the true function
f
and the kernel
K
, but not on the states
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, and (ii) the Stein discrepancy associated to the empirical distribution
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
as an approximation to
P
, which is
f
-independent.
Compared to Stein importance sampling, which considers only term (ii) for a fixed kernel, we can instead aim to pick
K
to simultaneously balance the product of terms (i) and (ii) and thus minimize the overall error bound.
The
f
-dependence of term (i) then leads to
f
-dependent selection of the kernel
K
, and this can substantially improve the suitability of the weights that are used in approximating the integral of interest.

Remark 6.3

(Stein Control Variates)
.

Stein et al. 2004
adopt a related perspective on solving the Stein equation that takes the form of a variance reduction technique for Monte Carlo.
Indeed, the standard Monte Carlo estimator

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(X_{i}),\qquad X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}P

can be replaced by the alternative estimator

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(X_{i})-(\mathcal{T}_{P}g)(X_{i})

(90)

with any element
g
from the Stein set
\mathcal{G}
.
An optimal
(
Assaraf and Caffarel 2003
;
Mira et al. 2013
, or
zero variance
, as termed by)
choice of
g
would correspond to a solution of the Stein equation (
86
), while in practice one can seek
g
to minimize an estimate for the variance of (
90
).
For applications involving Bayesian posteriors it is unusual to have access to exact samples from
P
, and therefore direct solution of the Stein equation might be preferable to trying to reduce the variance of an estimator based on Markov chain Monte Carlo.
On the other hand, Stein control variates have been successfully used for gradient estimation, as we will see in
Section

30.1
.

Several works have contributed to the development of numerical methods for solution of the Stein equation, including

Oates et al. 2017
;
Oates et al. 2019
;
Barp et al. 2022
;
Si et al. 2020
;
Belomestny et al. 2020
;
South et al. 2022a
;
South et al. 2022b
;
South et al. 2023
;
Sun et al. 2023
;
Leluc et al. 2025
;
Belomestny et al. 2024
.

Collocation Nodes via Stein variational gradient descent

Given a (possibly
f
-dependent) kernel
K
, Stein variational gradient descent (
Section

28.2
) provides an attractive method for selecting collocation nodes
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
for integral approximation; we now discuss the properties of these nodes assuming that they are a fixed point of Stein variational gradient descent.
To simplify discussion we suppose that
K(x,y)=k(x,y)I
.
By the update rule (
78
), at a fixed point of Stein variational gradient descent

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}k(x_{j},x_{i})=0,\qquad\forall i=1,\ldots,n.

(91)

On the other hand, by Stein’s identity,
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}[\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}k(X,x_{i})]=0
.
This implies that the particles
exactly
estimate the expectation of functions of form
f(x)=\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}k(x,x_{i})
for each
i=1,\dots,n
.
By the linearity of expectation, the same holds for all functions in the linear span of this set.
That is,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})=P(f)

holds for all
f\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
, the
Stein matching set

\mathcal{F}^{*}\triangleq\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{T}_{P}^{(1)}k(\cdot,x_{i})+b\colon\forall a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\penalty\ b\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

Note that exactness holds when the collocation nodes
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are a fixed point set of Stein variational gradient descent, but there need not be a unique fixed point set.
Extending this, the expectation of functions that are close to
\mathcal{F}^{*}
can be estimated better than the ones far away from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
.
Specifically, let
\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\xi}
be the
\xi
neighborhood of
\mathcal{F}^{*}
, that is,

\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{*}=\{f\colon\inf_{f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}}\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\xi\},

then it is easily shown that

\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i}^{*})-P(f)\right|\leq 2\xi,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall f\in\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\xi},

see
Liu and Wang 2018
.

An interesting special case is when
P
is multivariate Gaussian and
k
is a polynomial kernel of degree
p
(
Example

2.15
), in which case the numerical approximation produced by Stein variational gradient descent exactly matches the first
p
moments of
P
.
For example, if we use a linear kernel
k(x,x^{\prime})=1+x^{\top}x^{\prime}
and the
number of independent particles is no smaller than
d
(more precisely, when the rank of matrix
[x_{i};1]_{i=1}^{n}
is no smaller than
d+1
), then the matching set
\mathcal{F}^{*}
contains all the linear and quadratic functions, and hence any fixed point of Stein variational gradient descent exactly estimates both the mean and the covariance matrix of
P
.
Because many practical distributions are close to Gaussian, thanks to the central limit theorem and Bernstein–von Mises theorem, this observation can be leveraged to design better algorithms to perform particularly well for Gaussian like distributions.

29
Training Generative Models

This section focuses on the challenges associated with training generative models.
An important class of
energy-based
generative models are associated with an intractable normalizing constant, and for this class Stein discrepancy and Stein dynamics provide useful alternatives to maximum likelihood estimation, in the form of
Stein contrastive divergence
(
Section

29.1
) and
minimum Stein discrepancy estimation
(
Section

29.2
).
For generating ultra-realistic data, adversarial approaches are often used; here Stein dynamics offers a complementary approach to training
generative adversarial networks
(
Section

29.3
).
Bayesian approaches can often be computationally challenging outside of simple conjugate settings; here Stein discrepancy can provide a useful objective for
variational Bayesian
methods (
Section

29.4
), while in a related way Stein dynamics can enable more expressive modeling choices in the setting of
variational autoencoders
(
Section

29.5
).

29.1
Stein Contrastive Divergence

A generic approach to constructing flexible probabilistic models on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is to take a flexible function approximator, such as a deep neural network, and to apply a positivity-enforcing transform to it.
This leads to a so-called
energy-based
model
P_{\theta}
with density of the form

\displaystyle p_{\theta}(x)=\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}\exp(f_{\theta}(x)),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Z_{\theta}=\int\exp(f_{\theta}(x))\mathrm{d}x,

(92)

where
f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a flexible (negative)
energy
function with parameters
\theta\in\Theta
.
Given a dataset
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, an important task is to select an appropriate value for the parameter
\theta
so that the energy-based model is capable of generating samples that are statistically similar to those in the dataset (the precise meaning of which will be application-dependent).
Unfortunately, the absence of a normalized density for
P_{\theta}
provides a substantial barrier to the use of classical statistical procedures such as maximum likelihood, since the implicit normalization constant in (
92
) will be
\theta
-dependent in general.
Indeed, if we inspect the gradient of the log-likelihood

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log p_{\theta}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\theta}(x_{i})\right]-\nabla_{\theta}\log Z_{\theta}

then the first term is computable but the second is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\log Z_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}\nabla_{\theta}\int\exp(f_{\theta}(x))\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}\int\exp(f_{\theta}(x))\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{\theta}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]

which requires computation of an intractable expectation with respect to
P_{\theta}
.
Letting
P_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
denote the empirical distribution of the dataset, one step of gradient ascent can be written as

\theta\leftarrow\theta+\epsilon\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{\theta}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]\}

for some learning rate
\epsilon>0
.
Intuitively, this update rule iteratively increases
f_{\theta}(x)
on the observed data from
P_{n}
(or the
positive sample
), while decreasing
f_{\theta}(x)
on the data drawn from the hypothesized model
P_{\theta}
(
a.k.a. negative sample
).
When the algorithm converges, the expectations of
\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(x)
under the empirical distribution
P_{n}
and the model
P_{\theta}
should be equal.

To proceed, practical algorithms need to approximate the expectation with respect to
P_{\theta}
, which could be done, for example, by using
Markov chain Monte Carlo
(
Geyer 1991
, e.g.,)
, contrastive divergence
(
Hinton 2002
)
, or variational inference
(
Wainwright et al. 2008
, e.g.,)
.
In particular, contrastive divergence stands out as a simple approach that approximates the model expectation term by running
m
steps of a
P_{\theta}
-invariant Markov transition kernel starting from the data distribution
P_{n}
:

\displaystyle\theta\leftarrow\theta+\epsilon\{\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim K_{m}^{\theta}(P_{n},\cdot)}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]}_{(\star)}\},

(93)

where
K_{\theta}
is a
P_{\theta}
-invariant Markov transition kernel and
K_{m}^{\theta}(P_{n},\cdot)
denotes the distribution obtained by applying
m
iterations of the Markov chain initialized at a random sample from
P_{n}
.
For an ergodic Markov chain,
K_{m}^{\theta}(P_{n},\cdot)
converges to
P_{\theta}
as
m\rightarrow\infty
, and hence contrastive divergence recovers gradient ascent on the log-likelihood.
In practice, however, it is advised to use a small
m
, to save computation.
In this case, the increment
(\star)
can be interpreted (up to a sign) as
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\mathcal{T}_{P_{\theta}}g(X)]
, where

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{P_{\theta}}g)(x)=\int g(y)K_{m}^{\theta}(x,\mathrm{d}y)-g(x)

is a Stein operator constructed from the discrete time Markov chain (cf.
Section

14.3
) and
g(x)=\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(x)
.

Instead of using a Markov chain, we may use the Stein variational gradient descent update as the perturbation in contrastive divergence.
That is, we can perturb the observed data
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
with a deterministic transform
x\mapsto x+\epsilon g_{\theta}(x)
as in Stein variational gradient descent,
where the velocity field

\displaystyle g_{\theta}(x)=g_{P_{\theta},P_{n}}^{*}(x)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{T}_{P_{\theta}}^{(1)}K(x_{i},x)

(94)

is associated with steepest descent, as in (
77
).
Note that the Stein operator in
Equation

94
is the Langevin Stein operator used in Stein variational gradient descent, which provides a deterministic counterpart to the Markov transition in contrastive divergence and naturally connects to Stein discrepancy, analogously to how contrastive divergence connects to Fisher divergence.
The gradient update of
\theta
is then

\displaystyle\theta\leftarrow\theta+\epsilon\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)-\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X+\epsilon g_{\theta}(X))]\},

(95)

where
g_{\theta}(x)
denotes the optimal velocity field from Stein variational gradient descent.

Stein contrastive divergence

(
Liu and Wang 2017
)

uses a symmetric variant of the perturbation

\displaystyle\theta\leftarrow\theta+\epsilon\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X-\epsilon g_{\theta}^{*}(X))-\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X+\epsilon g_{\theta}^{*}(X))]\},

(96)

where the contrast is taken with respect to the perturbation
x\pm\epsilon g_{\theta}^{*}(x)
from two opposite directions; the rationale for this is explained in
Section

29.2
.

29.2
Minimum Stein Discrepancy Estimators

Another approach to parameter estimation in energy-based models (
92
) is to
minimize the Stein discrepancy between the empirical distribution of the data,
P_{n}
, and the parametric model,
P_{\theta}
.
Recall from
Section

15
that the (classical) score matching approach of
Hyvärinen 2005
selects
\theta
to minimize the Fisher divergence

\displaystyle\mathrm{FD}(P_{n}||P_{\theta})=[\text{constant in $\theta$}]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}2(\Delta_{x}f_{\theta})(x_{i})+\|(\nabla_{x}f_{\theta})(x_{i})\|^{2},

which is obtained by plugging in the empirical distribution
P_{n}
in place of
Q
in (
41
).
The gradient descent of
\theta
is then
\theta\leftarrow\theta-\epsilon g_{\mathrm{FD}},
where

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{FD}}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}(\Delta_{x}f_{\theta})(X)+(\nabla_{x}f_{\theta})(X)\cdot\nabla_{\theta}(\nabla_{x}f_{\theta})(X)].

(97)

As it turns out, this update rule is the limit of contrastive divergence where the perturbation is defined by a single step of Langevin dynamics, and where the step size
\epsilon
approaches zero:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(\hat{X})-\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]=\epsilon g_{\mathrm{FD}}+o(\epsilon),

(98)

where
\hat{X}=X+{\epsilon}\nabla_{x}f_{\theta}(X)+\sqrt{2\epsilon}Z
is the Langevin update and
Z
is standard normal.
This can be seen by a second-order Taylor expansion. Let

\delta=\epsilon\nabla_{x}f_{\theta}(X)+\sqrt{2\epsilon}\,Z
so that
\hat{X}=X+\delta
.
Then

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X+\delta)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)+\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)\,\delta+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\delta^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)\,\delta+o(\|\delta\|^{2}).

Taking expectation over
Z
and using
\mathbb{E}[Z]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[ZZ^{\top}]=I
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(\hat{X})]-\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\epsilon(\nabla_{x}f_{\theta}(X)\cdot\nabla_{\theta}\nabla_{x}f_{\theta}(X)+\,\nabla_{\theta}\Delta_{x}f_{\theta}(X))+o(\epsilon).

Substituting this into (
98
) and taking expectation over

X\sim P_{n}
yields the claimed expansion.

Fisher divergence is just one instance of a Stein discrepancy; more generally a
minimum Stein discrepancy estimator
is defined as

\displaystyle\min_{\theta}\;\mathcal{S}_{P_{\theta}}(P_{n}),

for
some
Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}_{P_{\theta}}

(
Liu and Wang 2017
;
Liu et al. 2019
;
Barp et al. 2019
)
.
Consider for instance the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy (
Section

18.1
), which has been proposed as a convenient way to construct estimators for energy-based models that are robust to outliers in the dataset through appropriate choices of the kernel
(
Barp et al. 2019
)
.
Gradient descent on the (squared) kernel Stein discrepancy amounts to working with the gradient

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\mathrm{KSD}_{P_{\theta}}^{2}(P_{n})=2\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}(\nabla_{x}f_{\theta})(X)g_{\theta}(X)],

(99)

where
g_{\theta}
was defined in (
94
).

Compared with the classical score matching update (
97
), which involves calculating a third-order derivative
\nabla_{\theta}\Delta_{x}f
, gradient descent on the (squared) kernel Stein discrepancy involves only second-order derivatives, and is therefore easier to implement.

Further, performing a first-order Taylor expansion of (
99
) yields the Stein contrastive divergence update in (
95
):

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(\hat{X}_{\mathrm{SVGD}})-\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt=\epsilon\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}(\nabla_{x}f_{\theta})(X)g_{\theta}(X)]+o(\epsilon),

where
\hat{X}_{\mathrm{SVGD}}=X+\epsilon g_{\theta}(X)
.
Therefore, Stein contrastive divergence provides a finite difference approximation of gradient descent on the kernel Stein discrepancy, requiring only first order derivatives to be computed.
This finite difference interpretation motivates the symmetric perturbation used in (
96
), since the centered finite difference approximation provides closer alignment with the gradient of the kernel Stein discrepancy.

The asymptotic statistical properties of minimum Stein discrepancy estimators can be understood through the framework of
M
-estimators
(
Barp et al. 2019
;
Matsubara et al. 2022
;
Oates 2022
)
.
The specific case of minimum Stein discrepancy estimators based on finite Stein sets was considered in
Ebner et al. 2025
.
Minimum KSD estimators with diffusion Stein operators (see
Section

18.6
) have also been deployed and analyzed in the setting of causal model learning
(
Lorch et al. 2024
;
Bleile et al. 2026
)
.

29.3
Stein GAN

Contrastive divergence has been widely used for learning energy-based models
P_{\theta}
(
92
) , and can often train models with good test likelihood.
However, models trained by contrastive divergence often struggle to generate truly realistic images.
This is because contrastive divergence learns the model parameters based on a local perturbation in the neighborhood of the observed data,
and does not explicitly train the model to create images from scratch.
This difficult was sidestepped by generative adversarial networks
(
Goodfellow et al. 2014
)
,
which explicitly train a
generator
(a deep neural network that takes random noise and outputs
images) to match the observed data with the help of a
discriminator
that acts adversarially, to distinguish the generated data from data in the training set.
Motivated by generative adversarial networks, we can modify the contrastive divergence idea to explicitly incorporate a generator into the training process.

The idea is based on
amortizing
the sampling process of
P_{\theta}
with a generator, and using the simulated samples as the negative samples to update
\theta
.
To be specific, let
G_{\beta}
be the distribution obtained by passing random noise
\xi
(e.g. standard Gaussian noise) through a neural network
N_{\beta}(\cdot)
with parameters
\beta
.
We will first seek to adjust the parameters
\beta
adaptively to make
G_{\beta}
as close as possible to the model
P_{\theta}
, and then we will update
\theta
using contrastive divergence with the generator
G_{\beta}
in place of the model
P_{\theta}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\theta\leftarrow\theta+\epsilon\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim G_{\beta}}[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(X)]\}.

(100)

The key question here is how to update
\beta
so that the distribution
G_{\beta}
closely approximates the model
P_{\theta}
.
Given samples
\{\tilde{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
from the current generator
G_{\beta}
, i.e. generated as
\tilde{x}_{i}=N_{\beta}(\xi_{i})
, applying the Stein variational gradient descent update leads to new samples
\{\tilde{x}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, where
\tilde{x}_{i}^{\prime}=\tilde{x}_{i}+\epsilon g_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{i})
, that should represent a more accurate approximation to
P_{\theta}
.
The idea of amortized Stein variational gradient descent
(
Feng et al. 2017
)
is to update
\beta
by taking a gradient descent step on the least-squares objective

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left\|N_{\beta}(\xi_{i})-\tilde{x}_{i}^{\prime}\right\|^{2},

so that the generator
G_{\beta}
is encouraged to approximate the new samples
\{\tilde{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, which represent a better representation of
P_{\theta}
.
An explicit calculation leads to

\displaystyle\beta\leftarrow\beta+\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\nabla_{\beta}N_{\beta})(\xi_{i})g_{\theta}(N_{\beta}(\xi_{i})).

(101)

This is known as
SteinGAN

(
Liu and Wang 2017
)
.
Formally, this can be
viewed as approximately solving the following minimax objective function based on Kullback–Leibler divergence (
Definition

2.26
):

\displaystyle\min_{\theta}\max_{\beta}\big\{\textnormal{KL}({P_{n}}\|{P_{\theta}})-\textnormal{KL}({G_{\beta}}\|{P_{\theta}})\big\}

Here the energy model
P_{\theta}
, serving as a discriminator, attempts to get closer to the observed data
P_{n}
, and keep away from the “fake” data distribution
G_{\beta}
, both in terms of Kullback–Leibler divergence, while the generator
G_{\beta}
attempts to get closer to the energy model
P_{\theta}
using amortized Stein variational gradient descent.

SteinGAN
can be viewed as a Kullback–Leibler divergence variant of the generative adversarial network-style adversarial game
(
Goodfellow et al. 2014
)
, and draws explicit connections to maximum likelihood training of energy-based models, where the intractable sampling (negative phase) is amortized via a neural generator trained to approximate Stein variational gradient descent dynamics.
An example of the realistic images generated by SteinGAN is contained in
Figure

8
.

Figure 8:
Images generated by SteinGAN trained on the LSUN dataset
(
Yu et al. 2015
)
, consisting of nearly 3M images of bedrooms.
Reproduced with permission from
Liu and Wang 2017
.

29.4
Variational Methods for Posterior Approximation

A popular class of numerical methods for Bayesian analysis come under the umbrella of
variational Bayes
; the idea, in brief, is that the posterior distribution is approximated using a generative model.
This can offer several advantages, most notably making it straightforward to employ the posterior as the prior for subsequent analyses, since its density is explicit.
The main technical issue is how one goes about approximating the posterior with a generative model.

Classical approaches to variational inference attempt to approximate the target posterior
P
by selecting from a tractable family of distributions
\mathcal{Q}
one for which the Kullback–Leibler divergence (
Definition

2.26
)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\;\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P}),

(102)

is minimized.
Letting
p(x,y)
denote the joint density of the unobserved
x
and the observed data
y
, and similarly letting
p(x|y)
denote the conditional and
p(y)
denote the marginal, and
q(x)
the density of
Q
, the Kullback–Leibler divergence is

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}({Q}\|{P})

\displaystyle=\int q(x)\log\frac{q(x)}{p(x|y)}\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int q(x)\log\frac{q(x)}{p(x,y)}\;\mathrm{d}x+\log p(y)

so that minimization of the Kullback–Leibler divergence is equivalent to minimization of the evidence lower bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{Q}(y)=\int q(x)\log\frac{p(x,y)}{q(x)}\;\mathrm{d}x.

For certain combinations of prior, likelihood, and variational family
\mathcal{Q}
, the evidence lower bound can be analytically computed and thus the optimization problem (
102
) can be approached as a numerical optimization task.
However, this restriction to “conjugate” combinations limits the extent to which the variational approximation can accurately reflect the posterior in general.
Beyond the conjugate setting, Monte Carlo methods can be used to approximate the evidence lower bound
(
Ruiz et al. 2016
, and its gradient; see e.g.)
, but these require explicit access to the density of
Q
(and its gradient), inspiring a line of research into
normalizing flows

(
Rezende and Mohamed 2015
;
Kingma et al. 2016
;
Dinh et al. 2017
)
.

An alternative solution, which does not require access to the density of
Q
, is provided by Stein discrepancy.
That is, we can formulate

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\;\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q)

(103)

and select a Stein discrepancy so that
\mathcal{S}_{P}(Q)
can either be exactly computed or consistently approximated (e.g., from samples from
Q
).
This unlocks the possibility of solving (
103
) as a numerical optimization task.
In particular, this removes the restriction of an explicit density for
Q
, and enables flexible approximations such as
Q_{\theta}=T_{\#}^{\theta}Q_{0}
for a general neural network
T^{\theta}
, parametrized by
\theta
, and some reference distribution
Q_{0}
.
The latter construction was studied in combination with the Langevin kernel Stein discrepancy in
Fisher et al. 2021
, who found that in regular cases the approximations produced by minimizing kernel Stein discrepancy were no less accurate than those produced by minimizing the Kullback–Leibler divergence; see
Figure

9
.

Figure 9:
A comparison of variational approximations to a target
P
(left) produced by minimizing kernel Stein discrepancy (centre) and Kullback–Leibler divergence (right).
The variational approximation was based on a block neural autoregressive flow and the target
P
arose from a Bayesian analysis of a biochemical oxygen demand model.
Reproduced with permission from
Fisher et al. 2021
.

A related approach, called
operator variational inference
in
Ranganath et al. 2016
, considers the same minimax problem in (
103
), i.e.

\min_{\theta}\max_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\;\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q_{\theta}}[\mathcal{T}_{P}g(X)]

where
\mathcal{T}_{P}
and
\mathcal{G}
are the Stein operator and Stein set associated to the Stein discrepancy
\mathcal{S}_{P}
.
However, the authors instead proposed to parametrize
g
using a neural network and then to alternate between taking a stochastic gradient step to update
\theta
, and a stochastic gradient step to update the parameters of this neural network
g
.
This enables greater flexibility in the Stein set
\mathcal{G}
, in principle enabling the differences between
Q_{\theta}
and
P
to be better detected, but comes at a cost of tuning a more involved numerical optimization method.

29.5
Learning Variational Autoencoders

An elegant approach to constructing expressive generative models begins with a parametric generative model on an extended state space; let
p_{\theta}(x,z)
be a parametrized probability density where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are to be observed and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
are latent.
Even if
p_{\theta}(x,z)
is a relatively simple model, the marginal distribution with density

\displaystyle p_{\theta}(x)=\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\;\mathrm{d}z

(104)

can capture rather complex dependencies among the variables that are observed.
The aim is thus to learn
\theta
, for example using maximum likelihood based on a dataset
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
However, this can be challenging because (
104
) does not possess a closed form in general.

To proceed, explicit approximations are required.
One solution is to train an
encoder
network
q_{\phi}(z|x)
to approximate the conditional
p_{\theta}(z|x)
.
This is the main idea behind variational autoencoders, which maximize an evidence lower bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\theta,\phi}(x)=\int q_{\phi}(z|x)\log\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{q_{\phi}(z|x)}\;\mathrm{d}z

(105)

to jointly train both the model and encoder parameters
\theta
and
\phi
.
Indeed, we can rewrite (
105
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\theta,\phi}(x)=\log p_{\theta}(x)-\mathrm{KL}(q_{\phi}(\cdot|x)||p_{\theta}(\cdot|x))

which makes clear we are promoting large values of the likelihood
p_{\theta}(x)
while also requiring the encoder
q_{\phi}(z|x)
to be a good approximation to the conditional
p_{\theta}(z|x)
.
However, working with (
105
) usually requires a tractable density for
q_{\phi}(z|x)
, the same issue we encountered for variational Bayes in
Section

29.4
.
As a result, the expressiveness of the encoder network, and hence the potential of the variational autoencoder to uncover hidden structure, can be limited.

A potential solution is the Stein variational autoencoder
(
Pu et al. 2017
;
Feng et al. 2017
)
.
The idea of the Stein variational autoencoder is that, instead of specifying an encoder network, we can use Stein variational gradient descent to represent the conditional distributions
p_{\theta}(z|x_{i})
implicitly via a set of particles
\{z_{i,j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
.
Compared with the standard VAE, which assumes
q{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)
to be a Gaussian conditioned on
x
, the nonparametric particle approximation can capture richer posterior structures.

The approach of
Feng et al. 2017
then updates the model parameters
\theta
according to

\displaystyle\theta\leftarrow\theta+\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x_{i},z_{i,j}),

which can be interpreted as gradient ascent on the joint likelihood where the pairs
(x_{i},z_{i,j})
are viewed as a pseudo-dataset.
By the Fisher identity

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim p_{\theta}(z\mid x)}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\big].

Hence, if the particles
\{z_{i,j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
were exact samples from the posterior

p_{\theta}(z\mid x_{i})
, the update above would recover (up to Monte Carlo error) the exact gradient of the marginal log-likelihood
\sum_{i}\log p_{\theta}(x_{i})
.
To speed up computation, a
recognition
network
N_{\beta}(\cdot)
can be trained to mimic the Stein variational gradient descent particle dynamics, i.e.

z_{ij}=N_{\beta}(\xi_{j},x_{i}),\qquad\forall i=1,\dots,n,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ j=1,\dots,m,

where
\xi_{i}
are sampled from a noise distribution (e.g. standard Gaussian) and the parameters
\beta
are trained based on least squares, implemented via gradient descent as in (
101
).
This
amortization
avoids the need to run Stein variational gradient descent separately for each entry
x_{i}
in the dataset.

30
Gradient Estimation

The computational task of numerically computing a gradient

\displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q_{\phi}}[f(X)]

(106)

with respect to the parameters
\phi
of a distribution
Q_{\phi}
is encountered in many learning problems, including variational inference
(
Paisley et al. 2012
, cf.
Section

29.4
and)
and training variational autoencoders
(
Kingma and Welling 2013
, cf.
Section

29.5
and)
, where one seeks to perform gradient ascent on an evidence lower bound, and in reinforcement learning when one seeks to improve a policy by following a policy gradient
(
Williams 1992
, cf.
Section

30.2
and)
.
The difficulty arises, at a fundamental level, when the expectation
Eq.

106
cannot be exactly computed and when evaluation of
f
incurs a substantial computational cost.
As such, many authors have proposed numerical methods to approximate (
106
) using a small number
m
of samples (often
m\in\{1,2,3\}
); we will see how Stein operators can play a useful role in this context, focusing on the case where
Q_{\phi}
is discrete (
Section

30.1
) and the case of a
policy gradient
as encountered in applications of reinforcement learning (
Section

30.2
).

30.1
Gradient Estimation with Discrete Stein Operators

This section focuses on the case where
Q_{\phi}
is a discrete distribution on a set
\mathcal{X}
, where the size of
\mathcal{X}
renders exact calculation of (
106
) impractical.
A popular strategy in this instance is to rewrite the gradient as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q_{\phi}}[f(X)]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\phi}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)q_{\phi}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)\frac{\nabla_{\phi}q_{\phi}(x)}{q_{\phi}(x)}q_{\phi}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q_{\phi}}[f(X)\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X)]

(107)

where
q_{\phi}
is the probability mass function for
Q_{\phi}
.
A direct Monte Carlo approximation to (
107
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(X_{i})\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i}),\qquad X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}Q_{\phi}

is an unbiased estimator of (
106
) but can have a high variance unless an extremely large number
m
of Monte Carlo samples is used.
A simple trick to reduce the variance is called REINFORCE
(
Glynn 1990
;
Williams 1992
)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}[f(X_{i})-B_{i}]\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})

(108)

which preserves unbiasedness as long as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B_{i}\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})]=0,

(109)

that is, as long as
B_{i}\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})
is a
control variate
, a random variable with known (in this case, zero) mean.
Fortunately, the control variate condition
Eq.

109
is straightforward to engineer. For example,
B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})
has zero mean whenever
B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}
and
X_{i}
are independent as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}]\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{\nabla_{\phi}q_{\phi}(X)}{q_{\phi}(X)}q_{\phi}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}]\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\nabla_{\phi}q_{\phi}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}]\nabla_{\phi}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q_{\phi}(X)=\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}]\nabla_{\phi}1=0.

Similarly,
B_{i}^{\textup{zero}}\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})
is zero mean whenever
\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{zero}}\mid X_{i}]=0
, as, by the tower property,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{zero}}\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[B_{i}^{\textup{zero}}\mid X_{i}]\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i})]=0.

More generally,
B_{i}=B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}+B_{i}^{\textup{zero}}
also satisfies the covariate condition
Eq.

109
, and we will use the full flexibility of this decomposition in what follows.

This original REINFORCE algorithm set each
B_{1},\dots,B_{m}
equal to a constant, the value of which was chosen to minimize the variance of (
108
).
For
m>1
a popular variant is obtained by replacing
B_{i}
by the leave-one-out average of function values, resulting in the
R
EINFORCE
L
eave-
O
ne-
O
ut (RLOO) estimator
(
Salimans and Knowles 2014
;
Kool et al. 2019
;
Richter et al. 2020
)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(f(X_{i})-\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j\neq i}f(X_{j})\right)\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{\phi}(X_{i}).

(110)

The RLOO estimate is again, by construction, unbiased for the target gradient.
However, despite improving on the former estimators, the variance of RLOO can still be stubbornly high unless a large number
m
of samples are used.

Stein operators can serve as a useful tool to further reduce the variance of RLOO without sacrificing unbiasedness
(
Shi et al. 2022b
)
.
The idea is that we would like to design a better choice for
B_{i}
, ideally one for which
B_{i}\approx f(X_{i})
, so that the variance of (
108
) in turn is small.
To this end, let
\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}
be a Stein operator for
Q_{\phi}
; several possible discrete Stein operators were discussed in
Section

14
.
Then since
\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}g_{j})(X_{j})\mid X_{i}]=0
for
j\neq i
and any
g_{j}
in the domain of
\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}
, we can consider taking
B_{i}
of the form

\displaystyle\!\!\!B_{i}=B_{i}^{\textup{ind}}+B_{i}^{\textup{zero}}=\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j\neq i}f(X_{j})+\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j\neq i}(\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}g_{j})(X_{j}).

(111)

Since our goal is to approximate
f(X_{i})
,
a suitable choice for each
g_{i}
could in principle be obtained by solving the Stein equation (
86
)

\displaystyle f(x)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q_{\phi}}[f(X)]+(\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}g)(x),\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{X},

as discussed in
Section

28.5
, but for the settings that we have in mind the size of
\mathcal{X}
would make direct solution of the Stein equation impractical.
As a practical way forward,
Shi et al. 2022b
recommended using the Gibbs Stein operator (
38
) and to select
g_{i}
of the form

\displaystyle g_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{k\neq j}\!N_{\beta}(f(X_{k}),\nabla f(X_{k})^{\top}(x-X_{k}))

(112)

where
N_{\beta}
is a neural network with parameters
\beta
that are to be optimized.
Note that, even though each function
g_{j}
depends on
X_{i}
, we still have
\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}g_{j})(X_{j})\mid X_{i}]=0
, and hence the gradient estimator remains unbiased.
As such,
\beta
can be optimized via gradient descent on the sample variance of the gradient estimator, similarly to
Grathwohl et al. 2018
.
The use of the Gibbs Stein estimator was motivated by numerical stability in settings where the components of
X
take a small number of values (e.g.
\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{d}
), while for settings where the components of
X
take many different values the Barker Stein operator (a special case of the Zanella Stein operator;
Definition

3.8
) was recommended in
Shi et al. 2022b
.
Further, in certain applications (such as variational autoencoders;
Section

29.5
) the evaluations of
\nabla f
come at no additional cost
(
Titsias and Shi 2022
)
, meaning that (
112
) requires no more evaluations of
f
than RLOO.

Figure 10:
Training variational autoencoders on the Fashion MNIST dataset; a binary latent
z
of dimension
d^{\prime}=200
was employed and
m=2
samples were used to evaluate each gradient.
Reproduced with permission from
Shi et al. 2022b
.

On top of the local Stein control variates (cf.
Remark

6.3
) in
Eq.

111
,
Shi et al. 2022b
introduce a second set of global Stein control variates with additional learned test functions
\tilde{g}_{k}
of the form
Eq.

112
to further reduce variance:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\big[

\displaystyle(f(x_{k})-\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j\neq k}(f(x_{j})+(\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}g_{j})(x_{j})))\nabla_{\eta}\log q_{\eta}(x_{k})

(113)

\displaystyle+(\mathcal{T}_{Q_{\phi}}\tilde{g}_{k})(x_{k})\big].

(114)

This overall approach, where the Stein equation is exploited as a
double variance reduction
tool, was termed RODEO (
R
L
O
O with
D
iscrete St
E
in
O
perators) in
Shi et al. 2022b
.
The authors applied RODEO to train binary latent variational autoencoders
(
Kingma and Welling 2013
, cf.
Section

29.5
and)
.
The dimension of the latent variable
z
was
d^{\prime}=200
, and the Fashion MNIST dataset
(
Xiao et al. 2017
)
was considered.
In
Figure

10
, we see that RODEO significantly reduces gradient variance along the training path relative to other gradient estimation schemes such as
DisARM

(
Dong et al. 2020
)
and
Double CV

(
Titsias and Shi 2022
)
.
This in turn tends to lead to improved performance of the variational autoencoder; for further results see
Shi et al. 2022b
.

30.2
Control Variates for Policy Gradient

The final application that we present concerns
reinforcement learning
, whose starting point is a
Markov decision process
consisting of a set of
states

\mathcal{S}
, a set of
actions

\mathcal{A}
, and a Markov process
(S_{t})_{t=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{S}
such that
S_{t+1}
depends only on
S_{t}
and an action
A_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
.
In addition, we have a sequence of random variables called
rewards

R(S_{t},A_{t})
which depend only on the current state
S_{t}
and action
A_{t}
.
Reinforcement learning seeks a (stochastic)
policy

\pi:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
, meaning that to decide which action to take at time
t
we sample
A_{t}\sim\pi(S_{t})
, for which the expected discounted cumulative reward

J(\pi)=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}R(S_{t},A_{t})\right|S_{0}=s_{0}\right]

is maximal.
Here the initial state
s_{0}
is considered to be fixed, and
\gamma\in(0,1)
is a
discount factor
, prioritizing the early part of the reward sequence, which is user-specified.

To simplify presentation, let
\mathcal{S}
be a countable set, let
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
P_{\pi}
be the
discounted state visitation
distribution with mass function

p_{\pi}(s)=(1-\gamma)\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(S_{t}=s|S_{0}=s_{0}),

where
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}
indicates that actions
A_{t}
are chosen according to the policy
\pi(S_{t})
, so that we can use the shorthand

J(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[R(S,A)]=\mathbb{E}_{S\sim P_{\pi}}[\mathbb{E}_{A|S\sim\pi(S)}[R(S,A)|S]].

In the sequel we slightly overload notation so that
\pi(\cdot|s)
denotes the density function for the distribution
\pi(s)
, for each
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

In
policy gradient
methods, the policy
\pi
is parametrized, which we denote as
\pi_{\theta}
, and the parameters
\theta
are updated by gradient ascent on
\theta\mapsto J(\pi_{\theta})
.
From the
policy gradient theorem

(
Williams 1992
)
, the gradient of this objective is

\nabla_{\theta}J(\pi_{\theta})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[Q^{\pi_{\theta}}(S_{t},A_{t})(\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta})(A_{t}|S_{t})\right],

where the
action-value function

Q^{\pi}(s,a)
denotes the expected return if the initial state is
s\in\mathcal{S}
and the initial action is
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and for all subsequent steps the policy
\pi
is used.
Monte Carlo can then be used to approximate this gradient, assuming that the action-value function is available or has been estimated.
A key challenge is that Monte Carlo estimation of this gradient often suffers from high variance.

To address this, techniques such as REINFORCE (cf.
Section

30.1
) are often used to formulate an equivalent expectation, for which the variance of the associated Monte Carlo estimator is reduced.
A direct application leads to

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\left(Q^{\pi_{\theta}}(S,A)-B(S)\right)(\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta})(A|S)\right],

where
B(S)
depends on the state
S
but not the action
A
.
This ensures the value of the gradient is unchanged, via a similar argument to (
110
).
Common choices of
B
are a constant, or the
value function

B(s)=\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q(s,a)

(
Sutton and Barto 1998
)
.
However, because these traditional baselines are based on functions only of the state
S
, their potential to substantially reduce variance is limited.
Stein’s identity unlocks the possibility of making
B
both state- and action-dependent, while not altering the value of the gradient.

For any element
g_{s}
in the domain of the Langevin Stein operator for
\pi_{\theta}(s)
(
Definition

3.2
),

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A\sim\pi_{\theta}(s)}[(\mathcal{T}_{\pi_{\theta}(s)}g_{s})(A)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A\sim\pi_{\theta}(s)}\left[g_{s}(A)\cdot(\nabla\log\pi_{\theta})(A|s)+(\nabla g_{s})(A)\right]=0.

However, here the gradients are taken with respect to the action
A
, while policy gradients involve gradients with respect to parameters
\theta
.
A reparameterization trick can be used to bridge this gap: namely, if we can express actions sampled from
\pi_{\theta}(s)
as
A=f_{\theta}(s,\xi)
, where
\xi
is random noise independent of
\theta
and
s
, then
Liu et al. 2018
showed that

\mathbb{E}_{A\sim\pi_{\theta}(s)}\left[g_{s}(A)\cdot(\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta})(A|s)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(s,\xi)\cdot(\nabla g_{s})(f_{\theta}(s,\xi))\right].

where the final expectation is with respect to the innovation noise
\xi
.
This leads to a Stein operator-based alternative to REINFORCE,

\nabla_{\theta}J(\pi_{\theta})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}(Q^{\pi_{\theta}}(S,A)-B(S,A))(\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta})(A|S)\\
\hskip 80.0pt+\nabla_{\theta}f_{\theta}(S,\xi)\cdot\nabla_{A}B(S,A)\end{array}\right],

where
B(s,a)=g_{s}(a)
and each
g_{s}
is in the domain of the Langevin Stein operator for
\pi(s)
, for each
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
Thus we have derived state- and action-dependent functions that can be optimized to reduce the variance of the associated Monte Carlo estimator, while ensuring resulting estimator remains unbiased.
An empirical investigation in
Liu et al. 2018
found that substantial variance reductions, and thus improved sample efficiency, can be achieved in a spectrum of reinforcement learning applications using
B(S,A)
compared with simpler state-dependent
B(S)
.

Chapter 7
Conclusion

This monograph has collected together rigorous definitions and results that underpin recent and emerging methodological applications of Stein’s method.
In doing so, we hope we have provided a convenient and singular reference for practitioners in probabilistic inference and learning.
Since this topic represents an active area of research, we can at best hope to provide a snapshot of the state of knowledge at the time of writing, and our perspectives and understanding of the methodological aspects of Stein’s method will surely be further developed.
Indeed, even in this respect we did not aim for a truly comprehensive treatment, limiting scope to only the most canonical Stein discrepancies and the most straightforward methodological applications of Stein’s method.
There now is a rich and growing literature, some of which we highlighted in passing, which we hope the reader will be inspired to explore in detail.
